她的右手无力地搭在扶手椅的扶手上。手背隐约泛着一层淡淡的尘渍，指甲闪烁着宛如粉色象牙般的温润光泽。无名指上的那枚祖母绿戒指长久地夺去了我的全部心神。目睹着我痴迷的目光，她微微一笑，斜倚在椅中的双眸如鹰隼般敏锐地向下扫向那枚宝石。那鹰隼般掠过的明澈眼神直教我魂飞魄散脱胎换骨，我不禁暗自痛斥起命运那残忍无情的颠沛捉弄——竟未能容她安安稳稳留驻在合情合理的婚姻稳妥归宿之内。

“圈口有些松了，”她正幽幽说道。

“我方才正暗自纳罕呢。这戒指美得如此惊心动魄！难道您就不怕它从指间脱落遗失么？”

她摇了摇头，唇边挂着一抹狡黠的浅笑凝视着我。她的孩童心性丝毫不教人感到突兀隔阂。她自始至终做着她自家。“噢，不！我这里有一处指关节。我只消微微一弯。瞧瞧，它便万万掉不下来了。……”

“可听起来这倒仿佛是一桩精心策划的阴谋诡计！”

“这是一道法则，”她说道，“共有四道法则（4道法则），分别冠以甲、乙、丙、丁之名。法则（甲）罔顾世间以往的一切信条断言，一朵鲜花正因其必死无疑才显得不那么美丽动人。那是一道宗教法则，关乎凡百朝生暮死之物的虚妄无常，倘若你能参透我言下之意的话。法则（乙）则关乎这么一个确凿事实：生着颀长双腿的男子，在情场云雨上往往皆是拙劣无能的情人。那是一道异教法则。你大可提笔写就一篇论‘巧合的长臂’与‘偷情通奸者的短腿’之长篇宏论。对你而言这定然是一桩极妙的趣事。法则（丙）则关乎规劝妇道人家切莫轻信正人君子，皆因一个恪守荣誉的正人君子势必同时伺候奉侍着两位女主人。那是一道专供多情女子防身护命的明智法则，能教她们免受痴心错付的凄苦落空。法则（丁）则关乎这枚指环——依循耶和华（Jehovah）的戒律指引，圈口必须略微松动几分。”

“艾丽丝·斯托姆，您的朋友可委实神通广大法力无边啊！”

“啊，我非得仰仗他们不可！欲望乃是一个生着饥渴双眸的幼童，而一头恶龙正潜伏在暗处窥伺着他。这枚指环正是一道克制恶龙的护身符牌。”沙哑的嗓音陷入了遐思。此刻正值适合沉沦遐思的时分。她总习惯用浮皮潦草的闲谈来遮掩心中的凄楚隐痛。“我唤他作耶和华，皆因那乃是一位善妒的神明。我过去常以此出言戏谑嘲弄他，对他说合该是我嫉妒他才是，皆因一个恪守荣誉的正人君子岂非总要同时伺候两位女主人；而人尽皆知的是，那些背负污名恶名的荡妇们——我常这般揶揄他——在同一时刻心里却从来只侍奉一位神祇。然而他生平绝非一个精明世故的凡俗之人，满腹狐疑猜忌，倘若不是他生就那般惹人怜爱，你定会忍不住当场笑掉大牙。……”

===Original===

“And so he gave you the emerald to be as a witness against you, and to testify against your frailty?”

“Now take,” dreamed the husky voice, the great eyes fixed on the ceiling; and there was a smile in them, like a distant wave of music; “now take a night in Algeria. Take also a hill, and on the hill a garden.﻿ ﻿…”

“The Hotel St. George, Mustapha Supérieur, Algiers﻿—”

“Ah, don’t forget the American Bar!”

“And the Benares bowls﻿—”

“And *calorifères* too hot or too cold﻿—”

“And Arab carpets from Victoria Street﻿—”

“And Americans with low heels﻿—”

“And a passion for ‘mailing postals﻿—’ ”

“Not to mention veal every day﻿—”

“And a Soirée de Gala every Saturday﻿—”

“And the best-dressed women﻿—”

“Of Tunbridge Wells.”

“But take instead some red and purple flowers against a yellow wall, some oranges, a tangerine or two, three gazelles on a tennis-court, poppies tall as choirboys, the cactus, the palm, and the pyramid cypress-tree. And watch, my friend, two shadows that walk in the wicked shadow of the pyramid cypress, that stands in the garden like a dark torch keeping watch over disillusion. It is night, or have I already told you that? Ah me, ah me, now will she who walks there ever forsake her love, will she ever be disloyal to her vows, that were made with so much pomp and circumstance in the Guards’ Chapel at Westminster before a congregation notable for the absence of all her husband’s relations? Why, her heart is confident, her heart is fragrant with the honey of that moon’s passage, and she knows what she knows. And yet, and here is a most pitiful thing, there must be something in her, some fatal abandon, that sets men doubting, for he who walked with her in the wicked shadow of the pyramid cypress wore the silence of the destroyer, so that her heart cried that he was misnamed, for the mortal disease of his heel was suspicion. Now I must tell you that it was Christmas Eve, and after a little desultory conversation he said: ‘Here is a present for you, sweet,’ and he gave me this emerald which you are kind enough to admire. ‘Alas,’ I said, ‘it is a little big for me! It may fall from my finger, don’t you see?’ ”

===Chinese===

“正因如此，他才赠予你这枚祖母绿戒指，以此作为昭示你罪愆的铁证，以此指证你的薄弱摇摆么？”

“眼下且设想一番吧，”那沙哑的嗓音如梦呓般低语道，硕大明澈的双眸定定凝视着天花板；眸中泛起一抹笑意，宛如自远方悠悠荡来的一缕韶乐余音；“眼下设想一番阿尔及利亚（Algeria）的长夜吧。再设想一座苍翠山峦，山峦之上坐落着一处园囿。……”

“阿尔及尔高穆斯塔法圣乔治大酒店（Hotel St. George, Mustapha Supérieur, Algiers）——”

“啊，切莫忘却了那间美式酒吧！”

“还有那些瓦拉纳西铜碗（Benares bowls）——”

“还有那些时而奇烫无比、时而冰冷彻骨的暖气散热器（calorifères）——”

“还有自维多利亚街（Victoria Street）采购来的阿拉伯地毯——”

“还有那些踩着平底低跟鞋的美国旅客——”

“以及他们成日沉溺于‘邮寄明信片’的狂热癖好——”

“更不用提每日雷打不动的小牛肉大餐——”

“还有每逢星期六隆重登场的奢华盛大晚宴（Soirée de Gala）——”

“以及那些号称穿戴最雍容华贵的贵妇人们——”

“尽皆出自坦布里奇韦尔斯（Tunbridge Wells）。”

“然而且设想一番吧——黄色的泥墙边盛开着赤红与绛紫的花卉，一堆香橙，一两枚（1、2枚）蜜柑，三只瞪羚（3只瞪羚）在网球场上悠然漫步，生得同唱诗班童子一般高挑的罂粟花，仙人掌，棕榈树，以及高耸如金字塔般的挺拔丝柏。我的朋友，且端详端详漫步在那株金字塔丝柏狰狞阴影下的两道身影吧——那丝柏矗立在园囿之中，宛如一柄守护着幻灭凄凉的幽暗火炬。正值深宵良夜，抑或我先前已然正告过你？呜呼哀哉，命途多舛！漫步在那儿的伊人，此生难道当真会背弃她的一腔深情么？她难道当真会违背自家当初在威敏斯特近卫步兵礼拜堂（Guards’ Chapel at Westminster）内、在除却夫君全族亲眷无一到场的清冷宾客面前、伴着如许浩大排场与繁文缛节所立下的海誓山盟么？哎呀，她的芳心深信不疑，她的心头充溢着那一轮明月朗照所酿就的甘冽蜜意，且她自家心明如镜。然而——这委实是一桩最令人扼腕叹息的人间惨剧——在她身上定然潜藏着某种致命的放浪风情，教汉子们忍不住疑窦丛生；皆因当时在那株金字塔丝柏的狰狞阴影下同她并肩漫步的那个汉子，周身皆笼罩在毁灭者的森严死寂之中，以致她心底里忍不住惊呼，他委实担不起自家的名头，皆因他致命死穴的阿喀琉斯之踵，正是根深蒂固的无端猜忌。话说眼下我必须正告你，当时正值平安夜（圣诞前夜），在一番漫不经心的闲谈过后，他开口说道：‘这是赠予你的生辰薄礼，宝贝，’说着他便将这枚承蒙您不吝赞赏的祖母绿戒指递到了我手中。‘唉，’我当时娇嗔道，‘这戒指于我而言未免略大了一圈！它极易自我的指间脱落遗失，难道您瞧不出么？’”

===Original===

“ ‘Yes, it may fall,’ said he. ‘But if you are careful, my sweet, if you curve your knuckle in time, it won’t dream of falling, not it!’ And then I cried miserably, knowing there was a catch in this somewhere, for at that time I was not yet broken in and was still fearful of suspicion. And I cried: ‘Hector Storm, what do you mean?’ ”

“ ‘I mean, Iris, that you are as that ring﻿—’ ”

“ ‘Beautiful but loose, Hector?’ Ah, *timeo Danaos*!”

“ ‘Iris, will you never be serious! Yes, you are as that ring, which you must always wear on the third finger of your right hand. And as that ring may fall, Iris, so you may fall, for that is the sort of woman you are. But as that ring may be kept from falling, so may you keep yourself from falling. Oh, God,’ he said, ‘my life is darkness without you, I love you so, and it’s a perfect hell with you, I love you so!’ And he said much more that is unmentionable, and I learnt something, for it is only by listening to their husbands in moments of intimacy that well-brought-up women can become acquainted with certain good old English words. And though I pleaded bitterly that he was unfair to me, saying I was chained to him as my wrist might be chained to a star, which was no more than the truth, he insisted that I could be constant only to inconstancy, and so I was tired and went to bed. But look! Oh, look! Please look! Ah, the discourtesy of time! Really I must go now!”

I drew my eyes from her eyes to see that the dawn had slyly thrown a grey handkerchief over the window. It was but the shape of the dawn creeping out into the night, it was but a ghostly breath in the night, but it was the dawn. And I did not know what to say, for can a man deny the dawn, that speaks good sense in its vast elemental language?

===Chinese===

“‘不错，它是可能会掉落，’他说道，‘但只要你小心留神，我的心肝儿，只要你及时弯起指节，它决计不会掉落，万万不会！’随即我痛苦地哭喊起来，自知这话里定有玄机陷阱，皆因那时节我尚未被驯服磨平，依然畏惧猜忌的目光。我叫喊道：‘赫克托·斯托姆，你究竟是什么意思？’ ”

“‘我的意思是，艾丽丝，你恰如那枚戒指——’ ”

“‘虽美丽却松垮，赫克托？啊，我惧怕希腊人（*timeo Danaos*）！’ ”

“‘艾丽丝，你就不能正经一回么！不错，你恰如那枚戒指，你必须始终将它戴在右手的无名指上。正如那枚戒指可能会掉落，艾丽丝，你也可能会堕落，皆因你就是那样的女人。然而，正如能防备那枚戒指不至于掉落，你也同样能克制自己不至于堕落。哦，上帝啊，’他说道，‘没有你，我的生命便是一片黑暗，我是如此爱你；而同你在一起，却是一座彻头彻尾的地狱，我是如此爱你！’他还说了许许多多难以启齿的污言秽语，我也着实长了见识，皆因教养良好的妇人家唯有在温存的私密时刻倾听夫君的言语，方能知晓某些地道古旧的英语粗话。尽管我悲苦地申辩他对我不公，哭诉自己被他牢牢羁绊，宛如手腕被铁链锁在一颗星辰之上——这绝非虚言，而是千真万确——可他却一口咬定我唯独在‘水性杨花’上始终如一。于是我精疲力竭，径自上床歇息了。可是你瞧！哦，你快瞧！请看一眼吧！啊，时光竟是如此不知好歹！我当真必须告辞了！”

我将目光从她的眼眸移开，望见晨曦已然悄无声息地将一方灰色的手帕蒙在了窗扉上。那不过是破晓初现、潜入夜幕的一缕轮廓，不过是夜色中飘散的一丝幽魂般的吐息，但那确凿是晨曦。我竟不知该作何言语，皆因一个凡人又岂能抗拒那以其浩瀚本源之言诉说常理真谛的拂晓？

===Original===

The chair creaked and creaked. She was going now, there was no doubt about it. The texture of her face was grave, she was busy with the angle of her green hat. I examined the sword in my mind. The chair creaked and creaked, and then it was as though snapped by silence, and our startled eyes joined over the emerald that lay on the floor like the echo of the kiss, which was an unfair kiss. She shivered faintly, and drew herself taut, and was very proud. She was remote as the evening star, and very proud. Her eyes were dark as in a crypt, and her eyes looked lost, as though she had strayed into a maze. I lit a cigarette, and found my throat dry and parched.

She found difficulty in speaking. I was amazed.

“No,” she said. She shook her head. “Certainly not. My ring, please.”

Imperiously her finger pointed to the floor, but her eyes were as plaintive as a nun’s who has strayed into one of the corridors of hell. That I might walk with her there I again made myself a Judas to her hand, and she shivered with her whole body as in a torment, and she seemed to bite her lip from within.

“It means nothing,” she said coldly.

“I know,” I said.

She breathed deeply, with a hand pressed to one breast, as though it hurt her. I think it must have hurt her very much. I was sorry. She shook her head, as though she was in a cage, and then she was as still as a cut flower. The whole brim of the green hat was between me and her face, we were both terribly alone. Her right hand drooped naked over the arm of the chair, and I was bending down to pick up the emerald to replace it on the third finger when a cautious knocking came from below.

That was the second or third time of knocking, and each time it was less cautious, and I knew it to come from the policeman on the beat, who would be wishing to have the primrose car put in its proper place, which was not on the King’s highroad. I wondered if she had heard, but I could not see her face. I wondered if she heard me move. As I came to the door I switched out the light and the dawn pounced on her green hat, but she who wore it fought her battles carved in stone. She said something, I did not catch what, and I went downstairs and spoke with the policeman, who was an amiable middle-aged man of my acquaintance.

===Chinese===

椅子嘎吱嘎吱响个不停。她要离开了，此事毋庸置疑。她的面容神色凝重，正专注于摆正头上绿帽子的角度。我心中暗自审视着那把利剑。椅子不住地嘎吱作响，旋即仿佛被死寂猛然截断，我们惊异的目光在地板上的祖母绿处交汇——那枚戒指宛如那个不公之吻的回响，静静躺在地面上。她微微战栗了一下，身子绷得紧紧的，神态傲岸至极。她宛如昏星般遥不可及，孤高自持。她的眼眸深邃幽暗如地宫墓穴，眸光迷离惘然，宛若迷失于幽回曲折的迷宫之中。我点燃了一支香烟，发觉自己的嗓子干渴焦灼。

她似是极难开口。我不由得惊诧万分。

“不，”她说道。她摇了摇头。“绝不。请把我的戒指拿给我。”

她的手指傲然指向地面，可她的双眸却如同一位迷失在炼狱回廊中的修女般凄楚哀怨。为了能与她在那儿同行，我再次充当了背叛她玉手的犹大；她周身剧烈战栗，宛如深陷酷刑折磨，下唇仿佛从齿内被狠狠咬破。

“这毫无意义，”她冷冷说道。

“我明白，”我说道。

她深深地吸了一口气，一只手紧紧按住胸口，仿佛那里正隐隐作痛。想来定然是撕心裂肺之痛。我心生怜悯。她摇了摇头，宛如身陷樊笼的困兽，旋即又沉寂得宛如一朵被剪下的鲜花。绿帽子的宽大帽檐横亘在我与她的脸庞之间，我们两人皆陷于令人心悸的无尽孤独之中。她那光秃秃的右手无力地垂在扶手椅的侧边，我正俯下身去拾取祖母绿戒指，准备替她重新套上无名指，楼下却传来一阵谨慎的叩门声。

那是第二或第三回（第2或第3回）敲门，每一次敲击都比先前少了几分顾忌，我心知那是巡逻的警员在敲门，他定是希望那辆报春花色的跑车能停放到该去的地方，而非停在国王的大道之上。不知她是否听见了声响，但我看不见她的面容。不知她是否听见我的动静。当我走到门前时，顺手关掉了电灯，晨光顿时扑向了她的绿帽子，但头戴绿帽的伊人宛如石雕一般，正打着她自己的硬仗。她轻声说了句什么，我未能听清，随后便走下楼去同那位警员交谈——那是一位与我相识的和蔼中年人。

===Original===

“My brother is with me,” I said, “but he will be gone soon.” Shepherd’s Market was creeping out into the dawn, draped and mysterious with the shadows of night. A window here and there was alight against the dark pile of Camelot House. The great car stood like a bruise against the passage of eternity, dawn fought for it, night draped it, and the silver stork flew unseen. The small noises of dawn stirred sharply in the night, and the lamps wore pale, tired faces. “Summer’s well on,” said the policeman.

I reentered the sitting-room, saying impersonally: “I’m afraid you must go, as.﻿ ﻿…” The room was empty. The figure that had been carved in stone was wrapped in air. The disorder of the room lay jeering at me on the dim carpet of the dawn. It was all like a purposeless limbo stretched between the night and the day, the room, my life, hers, everything, the strong, the silly and the brave. The hundreds of books lay in soiled confusion on the floor, the wisdom of the world that has gone to the making of the soiled nothings that we are.

I was seized by a catholic anger against the woman. Through all the disenchantments of youth, despite the contagious impurities of life, in defiance of the crimes against love that we call love, I had kept romance for my ghostly companion. Romance was more than a silly lithe goddess coming down from a marble column. Romance was more than the licence to be shameless with clouded eyes. Romance did not steal through the fleshy portals of the heart, did not shiver at a Judas kiss, did not coil white trembling limbs into the puerile lusts of the mind. Romance was all that and was as much greater than that as a religion is greater than a church. To romance, which was the ultimate vision of common sense, sex, as sex, was the most colossal bore that had ever distracted man from his heritage. And she would palm a facet of this colossal bore off on me! She would have me barter my ghostly companion for the fall of an emerald, she would invade my thoughts, perhaps my life, in exchange for a puny pleasure that needs love to exalt it above the matchless silliness of what, with an excessive zeal for scientific classification, is known to our civilisation as the sexual act.

I picked up the emerald from the floor, and it smiled in the palm of my hand.

===Chinese===

“我的兄弟同我在一起，”我说道，“但他很快就会离开。”牧羊人集市正缓缓自晨曦中显现，周身披拂着夜色的迷离幽影。衬着卡米洛特大厦（Camelot House）庞大阴沉的轮廓，零零星星有几扇窗户透出亮光。那辆庞大的跑车如同一块淤青般横亘在永恒的时间长河中，晨曦为争夺它而战，夜色为它披上外衣，银色的鹳鸟在冥冥中隐没翱翔。破晓微末的响动在黑夜中敏锐地悸动，街灯显出一副苍白困倦的面孔。“盛夏已然来临啦，”警员说道。

我重新步入起居室，用冷淡客套的语气说道：“恐怕你必须离开了，因为……”房间内空无一人。那具宛如石雕般的身影已消融在空气之中。房间内的杂乱狼藉铺陈在黎明黯淡的地毯上，仿佛在对我肆意嘲弄。这宛如横亘在黑夜与白昼之间的一片虚无缥缈的灵薄狱，整个房间、我的生活、她的生活，世间万象，那坚强者、痴愚者与勇敢者，皆尽于此。几百本书（数百本书）狼藉凌乱地散落一地，汇聚成世间的智慧，最终却只造就了我们这般污秽不堪的微末微尘。

一阵对这个女人的普遍狂怒骤然席卷了我。经历过青春年华的所有幻灭，历经世俗生活具有传染性的污秽侵蚀，蔑视着那些被我们冠以爱情之名却戕害爱情的罪行，我始终将浪漫留作自己幽灵般的伴侣。浪漫绝非仅仅是一位自大理石立柱上款款走下的愚蠢轻盈的女神。浪漫绝非仅仅是在眼神迷离中放浪形骸的特权。浪漫不会潜行穿过肉欲的心灵之门，不会因犹大之吻而战栗颤抖，更不会将白皙颤抖的四肢蜷缩进心智幼稚的肉欲深渊之中。浪漫固然包罗这一切，却远比这一切博大宏伟，正如一种宗教远比一座教堂更为崇高浩瀚。对于作为常识终极洞见的浪漫而言，纯粹的肉欲乃是有史以来转移人类对其天赋遗产注意力的最庞大沉闷的无聊之物。而她竟妄图将这种庞大无聊的一鳞半爪强加于我！她竟企图让我为了一枚祖母绿戒指的滑落而典当掉我幽灵般的伴侣，她竟妄图侵入我的思绪、乃至侵入我的生活，换取的不过是微不足道的欢愉——那种唯有借助爱情方能升华、超越被我们当今文明以过剩的科学分类狂热称之为‘性行为’之无与伦比之愚蠢的欢愉。

我自地板上拾起那枚祖母绿戒指，它在我掌心泛着微笑。

===Original===

In the dusk of the bedroom, she lay coiled on the bed. The hush of her breathing was no more than the trembling servant of the silence. Then she coughed a small cigarette cough. It was the usual cough, and gave me back my confidence. “Iris Storm!” I said, but I wondered if I had spoken, the frail silence was so undisturbed. She was asleep.

Perhaps it was then that I realised that she was beautiful. She was asleep. Could any but the shape of beauty dare to wear that impertinence! She lay on her side, she lay anyhow. The green hat was gone.

“Iris!” I said. Her hair was thick and tawny, and it waved like music, and the night was tangled in the waves of her hair. It was like a boy’s hair, swept back from the forehead, which was a wide, clear forehead, clean and brave and sensible as a boy’s. Sensible, oh dear! The tawny cornstalks danced their formal dance on the one cheek that I could see, and the tip of a pierced ear played beneath them, like a mouse in the cornfield. Above her neck her hair died a very manly death, a more manly death than “bobbed” hair was ever known to die, and so it comes about that Iris Storm was the first Englishwoman I ever saw with “shingled” hair. This was in 1922.

I decided that I did not know what to do. I decided that that was just as well. “I will play,” I thought, “a waiting game,” and lit a cigarette. But in her tawny hair the night was tangled like a promise, and it smelled as grass might smell in a faerie land, and always about her there was that faint dry scent whose name I shall now never know. Her mouth drooped like a flower, and there was a little shiny bit in the valley between her cheek and her nose. To this I applied a little Quelques Fleurs talc powder on a handkerchief, that when she awoke she should not think so ill of herself as I did. Hers was a small, straight nose with an imperceptible curve, just as any straight line might have, and its tip quivered a little as she breathed. Her leather jacket *pour le sport*, that had a high collar trimmed with some minks, was flung open, and over the breast of her dark dress five small red elephants were marching towards an unknown destination. Towards her feet her hat lay with my hat.

===Chinese===

在卧室的幽暗暮色中，她蜷缩在床榻之上。她细微悠长的呼吸声，不过是死寂之中颤颤巍巍的仆役。旋即，她发出了一声吸烟者常有的微弱咳嗽。那不过是寻常的咳嗽，却让我重新找回了底气。“艾丽丝·斯托姆！”我唤道，但我怀疑自己是否当真出了声，那脆弱的静谧竟未受到丝毫惊扰。她睡着了。

或许正是在那一刻，我才真切意识到她何等美丽。她睡着了。除了至美本身的化身，世间又有谁敢展现这般桀骜不驯！她侧卧着，姿态随意而任性。那顶绿帽子已然不见了踪影。

“艾丽丝！”我唤道。她的秀发浓密而呈黄褐色，如音律般波澜起伏，夜色宛如缠绕在她发丝的波浪之间。那发型恰似男童的短发，从前额向后梳拢，露出宽阔明净的前额，如同男童般干净、勇敢且理智。理智，哎呀呀！黄褐色的玉米秆在她我所能看见的一侧脸颊上跳着规整的舞蹈，戴有耳洞的耳垂在其下若隐若现，宛若玉米地里的一只小鼠。在她的后颈上方，头发剪出了一种极具男子气概的利落收尾，比世人所熟知的“波波头（bobbed）”短发来得更为刚毅干脆；正因如此，艾丽丝·斯托姆便成了我生平所见第一位（第1位）留着“剪发头（shingled）”短发的英国女子。那是在1922年。

我自忖不知该如何是好。我认定这样倒也无妨。“我且静观其变，”我心想，随手点燃了一支香烟。然而在她黄褐色的发丝间，夜色宛如一纸誓约般纠缠交错，散发着仙境青草般的芬芳，且她周身始终萦绕着一缕淡淡的干燥馨香，其名目我至今亦无从知晓。她的唇角如花朵般低垂，脸颊与鼻翼之间的凹陷处泛着微弱的油光。我用手帕沾了些许“凯雪飞花”（Quelques Fleurs）爽身粉轻轻敷在那处，免得她醒来时如我一般觉得自己容颜憔悴。她的鼻子小巧而笔直，带着一道几乎难以察觉的弧度，恰如任何直线所可能蕴含的弧线一般，鼻尖随着她的呼吸微微颤动。她那件用于运动（*pour le sport*）、镶有水貂毛高领的皮夹克敞开着，在她深色连衣裙的胸口上，五只微小的红色大象（5只小红象）正朝着未知的归宿迈步前行。在她的脚边，她的帽子与我的帽子并排躺在一处。

===Original===

Gently, gently, gently as the phantom of myself, for was I not being better than myself? I would replace the emerald on the third finger of her right hand. I would, when hair that was not my own was pressed against my ear, and fingers that were not my own took the cigarette from my mouth, and teeth that were not mine bit my lip, and when the red elephants marching towards an unknown destination stirred breasts full of shadows, and a voice as clear and strong as daylight said: “But enough of this hell!”

===Chinese===

轻轻地，轻轻地，宛若我自身的幽灵般轻柔，皆因我岂非已然超越了平日的自己？我本打算将那枚祖母绿重新戴回她右手的无名指上。我正要这么做，不属于我的发丝却紧紧贴在了我的耳畔，不属于我的纤指自我的唇间抽走了香烟，不属于我的贝齿咬住了我的嘴唇；正当迈向未知归宿的红色小象随着笼罩在阴影中的胸口起伏摇曳之时，一道如白昼般清晰强劲的嗓音开口说道：“这地狱般的折磨，且到此为止吧！”

### IV / 第四节
===Original===

Of all that had once decorated the walls of my sitting-room there was left by the removers only a looking-glass in an ancient gold frame, above the fireplace. My mother had once given me an oil-painting, saying, “This will do nicely for your flat,” but I in my pride had thought a looking-glass would offend the frame more judiciously.

She stood before that.

“What is the time?” she asked her reflection, and I told her that it was ten minutes to six.

“Have you a comb?” she asked of her reflection, and luckily I had a comb which was not my comb. She looked at it and saw that it was so.

“Thank you,” she said to her reflection.

The light of the tawny hair mocked the clouded daylight, and when, with the palm of her hand on her forehead, she swept the comb from front to back, it flamed tiger-tawny and ate into my spirit. Tiger, tiger, burning bright, in the forests of the night.﻿ ﻿…

In the Upper Fifth at school there was a tall, cold-eyed blood called Dwight-Rankin﻿—I think he died on Gallipoli﻿—who used to sit at the desk just in front of mine. He was a man of the mode, wearing his fair hair plastered from front to back, and his neck was clean and unspotted as a girl’s, and I would spend minutes wondering whether, if one touched the gold down in which his hair ended high above his neck one would feel hair or only skin. The back of her head affected me like that; it was just like Dwight-Rankin’s, only dry, and tiger-tawny.

===Chinese===

在曾经装点我起居室四壁的所有物件中，搬运工只留下了一面嵌在古旧金框里的穿衣镜，悬挂在壁炉上方。家母曾赠我一幅油画，说道：“这幅画挂在你的公寓里恰到好处，”但我出于自傲，总以为挂上一面镜子反而更能巧妙地冒犯那具画框。

她便伫立在镜子前。

“现在几点了？”她向着镜中的倒影问道，我告诉她差十分六点（5点50分）。

“你有梳子么？”她向着镜中的倒影问道，凑巧我恰有一把不属于我的梳子。她打量着梳子，看清确是如此。

“多谢，”她对镜中的倒影说道。

黄褐色秀发的光泽嘲弄着阴沉的天光，当她将手掌贴在额头上、用梳子自前向后一掠而过时，满头秀发泛起虎褐色的烈焰，灼噬着我的心神。老虎，老虎，黑夜的森林中烈火般熊熊燃烧……

当年在学校的高五年级里，有个名叫德怀特-兰金（Dwight-Rankin）的高挑冷面阔少——想来他已战死在加里波利（Gallipoli）半岛——从前总坐在我正前方的课桌前。他是个讲究时髦的人，一头金发总是服服帖帖地由前向后梳得一丝不苟，他的后颈白皙无瑕，宛若少女，我常会花上好几分钟寻思，若有人抚摸他后颈上方发线终结处的金色茸毛，触碰到的究竟会是发丝还是光洁的肌肤。她脑后的轮廓给我的正是这般感觉；宛如德怀特-兰金一般无二，只不过干燥爽落，呈现出虎一般的黄褐色。

===Original===

She tore the small comb through the dancing curls on each cheek, so that they trembled like voiceless bells. It is a commonplace about women, as assiduously remarked by brilliant feminine psychologists as women’s “caprice” and “intuition,” that every woman must now and then make a “grimace of distaste” into a looking-glass. But she did not do that, nor need to. She was untouched, unsoiled, impregnable to the grubby, truthful hand of *lex femina*. She was like a tower of beauty in the morning of the world. The outlaw was above the law of afterwards, impervious and imperious. She was beautiful, grave, proud. How beautiful she was now! It was a sort of blasphemy in her to be so beautiful now, to stand in such ordered loveliness, to be neither shameful like a maiden nor shameless like a mondaine, nor show any fussy after-trill of womanhood, any dingy ember of desire. It was a sort of blasphemy in her, as it would be in a peacock to sing gracefully.

The silence got on my nerves, and I said something, anything. She looked over her shoulder at me, vaguely. She was the male of the species that is more fearless than mankind. I wondered what she was going to say.

“My hat, please,” she said. I appeared to have been holding it in my hand. With her left hand she crushed it on her head and kept her hand on the crown, looking at herself intently in the looking-glass. I was startled at her eyes in the looking-glass. They were cold blue stones, expressionless, caddish as a beast’s.

Down, down, with two fingers of her left hand, she pulled the brim of the green hat over her left eyebrow. She said: “I think I must have left my powder in the other room. Do you mind?” I brought her the case of white jade and the box of black onyx. She powdered, without interest.

“Goodbye,” she said. Her hand was held out, her eyes were full on mine, naked, expressionless. I felt that they were the heads of the nails under which she had nailed herself. It would be a kindness to let her go quickly, a kindness which she would not have allowed me had I been a woman and she a man.

“Goodbye,” I said. And suddenly the hand that lay in mine pressed mine, and she gave a vague, brittle laugh.

“It seems a pity,” she said; and then the eyes in the shadow of the brim seemed to open wide, wide.

“You see?” she whispered. “You see?”

===Chinese===

她用那把小梳子猛力梳理两颊跳跃的卷发，使卷发如无声的风铃般剧烈颤动。关于女人的陈词滥调——恰如睿智的女性心理学家们孜孜不倦论述的女人之“任性”与“直觉”——便是每个女人时不时总要对着镜子做出一副“厌恶的怪相”。可她既未如此，也无此必要。她未受侵染，尘垢不沾，对于“女性法则”（*lex femina*）那双肮脏而求真的手而言坚不可摧。她宛如世界清晨矗立的一座美丽高塔。这位亡命之徒凌驾于事后的法则之上，刀枪不入，傲慢自恃。她美丽、凝重、孤傲。此时此刻的她是何等美丽绝伦！在此时此刻展现出这般绝美，伫立在如此齐整雅致的风姿之中，既不像少女那般羞怯，又不像交际花（*mondaine*）那般不知廉耻，亦未显露出任何女人气的事后纷扰悸动，或任何欲望的黯淡残烬，于她而言简直是一种亵渎。这于她而言简直是一种亵渎，正如孔雀若能优雅啼鸣一般荒谬不可思议。

沉寂教我心烦意乱，我便随口说了几句，聊胜于无。她茫然地回过头来瞥了我一眼。她是这一物种中比寻常人类更为无畏的雄性存在。我不由得揣度她究竟要说些什么。

“请把我的帽子拿给我，”她说道。那顶帽子似乎一直被我攥在手里。她用左手将它一把按在头上，手按在帽顶，凝神端详着镜中的自己。镜中她的双眸令我惊骇不已。那是一双冰冷的蓝宝石，毫无表情，粗鄙凶顽宛如野兽。

向下，再向下，她用左手的两根手指（2根手指），将绿帽子的帽檐拉低掩过左眉。她说道：“我想我定是把香粉落在了隔壁房间。你介意帮我取一下么？”我为她取来了白玉粉盒与黑玛瑙盒子。她心不在焉地扑了扑粉。

“再会，”她说道。她伸出手来，双眸直勾勾注视着我的眼眸，坦露无遗，毫无波澜。我觉得那一双眼眸恰似将她自己钉死其下的铁钉的钉头。放她尽快离去乃是一种仁慈，一种倘若我是女子而她是男儿便断然不会施予我的仁慈。

“再会，”我说道。突然间，那只落在我掌中的手握紧了我的手，她发出一声飘忽凄清的轻笑。

“似乎怪可惜的，”她说道；旋即帽檐阴影下的双眸仿佛陡然睁得硕大、滚圆。

“你明白了吧？”她低语道。“你明白了吧？”

===Original===

But I could see nothing but her silhouette against the future days. I said: “We have begun at the wrong end; but can’t we work back?”

“Oh, no!” she whispered. “It is not like that a bit. You don’t understand.﻿ ﻿…”

Suddenly I said many things.

She seemed, her hand still in mine, to be absorbed in something just behind my right shoulder. There was such fear in her eyes that I cried sharply: “What is it?”

“The beast,” said the lips of the eyes of fear. “Just the beast.﻿ ﻿…”

The word I said was drowned in the din of a lorry that smashed through Whitehorse Street to Piccadilly. She took her hand gently from mine. “There is a dream,” she said, “and there is a beast.”

She smiled.

“That’s all,” she said.

“I can understand regret,” I said, “but﻿—”

“Ah, we can understand, you and I! We are as old as sand﻿ ﻿… at this moment.”

“But, Iris Storm, regret seems like a scar on you!”

“Not regret,” she said, so calmly. “Shame.” And she took my hand again, closely. “You must forgive me. I couldn’t have said that to any other man. My shame mustn’t shame you, please! But you have a cold mind, you are disenchanted, you understand. And oh, if one could be assoiled in human understanding! You see, I am not what you think. I am not of the women of your life. I am not the proud adventuress who touches men for pleasure, the silly lady who misbehaves for fun. I am the meanest of all, she who destroys her body because she must, she who hates the thing she is, she who loathes the thing she does.﻿ ﻿…” The breathless, pregnant voice seemed to fall to the floor, like a small bird with broken wings, and as it struggled upwards I said: “You are like a boy after his first love.”

“Oh, if it was boyishness!” And she took from the pocket of her leather jacket a tube of gold, and she broke it into two pieces, and she stared moonstruck at the carmine tongue of the lip-salve.

“To be born a chaste woman,” she said to the carmine tongue, “is good. I am in favour of chastity. I would die for purity, in theory.” She painted her mouth, staring moonstruck into the daylight. “Yes, I would die for purity. I wouldn’t mind dying anyhow, but it would be nice to die for purity.﻿ ﻿…”

I said thus and thus.

===Chinese===

可我眼前除了她衬着未来岁月的剪影，其余什么也看不清。我说道：“我们的开端全错了；难道我们不能从头来过么？”

“哦，不行！”她低语道。“根本不是那么一回事。你并不明白……”

骤然间，我说出了许许多多的言语。

她的手依然留在我的手心里，整个人却似乎全神贯注于我右肩后侧的某样物事。她的眸中流露出如许恐惧，以致我厉声喊道：“究竟怎么了？”

“野兽，”惊恐双眸下的双唇低语道。“不过是一头野兽……”

我的话语被一辆自白马街（Whitehorse Street）疾驰撞向皮卡迪利（Piccadilly）的货车轰鸣声彻底淹没。她轻轻将自己的手从我掌中抽回。“这里有一场幻梦，”她说道，“还有一头野兽。”

她嫣然一笑。

“仅此而已，”她说道。

“我能理解悔恨，”我说道，“可是——”

“啊，你与我，咱们两人都能理解！在此时此刻……咱们如同沙砾一般苍老沧桑。”

“但是，艾丽丝·斯托姆，悔恨在你身上宛如一道伤疤！”

“并非悔恨，”她如此平静地说道。“是耻辱。”她再次紧紧握住我的手。“你务必原谅我。换作任何旁人，我都决计吐露不出这番话。请不要让我的耻辱玷污了你！可你拥有一副清醒冷峻的头脑，你历经幻灭，你能体谅。而且，啊，倘若世人能在此类人情理解中得到赦罪该有多好！你瞧，我绝非你所想象的那般女子。我并非你生命中所经历过的那些妇人。我并非为了寻欢作乐而沾惹男人的骄横女冒险家，亦非为了找乐子而胡作非为的蠢妇人。我是世间最下贱可鄙的一个，不过是个因身不由己而毁灭自身躯壳的女人，是个痛恨自家面目、厌恶自家行径的女人……”那喘息促迫、饱含深意的嗓音仿佛跌落到了地板上，宛如一只折断了翅膀的幼鸟；正当它挣扎着向上扑棱时，我开口道：“你宛如一个经历初恋之后的少年。”

“哦，若那只是少年意气便好啦！”说着，她自皮夹克的口袋里摸出一管金色的唇膏，将其掰成了两截（2截），痴痴怔怔地凝视着那抹胭脂红的唇膏芯。

“生为贞洁女子，”她对着那抹胭脂红轻语道，“终归是一桩美事。我崇尚贞洁。在理论上，我愿为纯洁献出生命。”她涂抹着嘴唇，神情恍惚地凝望着白昼天光。“不错，我愿为纯洁赴死。无论如何我都不介意赴死，可若是能为纯洁而死，倒也不失为一桩美事……”

我这般那般地诉说了一番。

===Original===

“Yes,” she said, not having heard a word of mine, “it is not good to have a pagan body and a Chislehurst mind, as I have. It is hell for the body and terror for the mind. There are dreams, and there are beasts. The dreams walk glittering up and down the soiled loneliness of desire, the beasts prowl about the soiled loneliness of regret. Goodbye.”

“Then it must be ‘goodbye’?”

She looked at me with a strange, dark friendliness, and nodded.

“Because of shame,” she said. “But if I were different, I would like you for my friend﻿—”

To my interruption, which she did not hear, she said: “I have only one lover. But I know that only because I always feel unfaithful to him. It would be good to be really bad, but I am not even that. I only misbehave. I will see you again, when I have found my only love. Or I will see you again when I am qualified to die for purity. I will let you know, so you can be there. God bless you, dear.”

And I said what I said, that He had, with Iris Storm.

She went very white. “That shall be written down,” she whispered, “as the prayer of the only man who ever shamed a woman of her shame.”

“My days of adventure, Iris Storm, are over. A few years ago it would have seemed nothing to me that you should disappear as you came, into the great hole of London. To experts in adventure that is, I think, the usual procedure. But now I would like a trace of you. You must not leave me, quite. If I may not see you again, mayn’t I perhaps talk to you? Or, what is the main thing, feel that I could if I dared?”

She said she was in London now only on business that would last a few weeks, and lived always abroad. “But this is the telephone-number,” she said, and I was looking round for paper and pencil when she said “Here!” and her leather arm darted to the floor and came up with a book, and on the flyleaf of the book she scrawled the number with her lipstick.

High above the sharp noises of the young day I heard the scream of an electric-horn.

===Chinese===

“不错，”她说道，全然未曾听进我的一字一句，“正如我这般，拥有一副异教徒的躯壳与一颗奇斯尔赫斯特（Chislehurst）的心智，委实不是什么好事。这对于肉身是地狱，对于心智则是梦魇恐惧。世间既有幻梦，亦有野兽。幻梦闪耀着光华，在欲望污浊的孤独中穿梭徘徊；野兽则在悔恨污浊的孤独中四处游荡逡巡。再会了。”

“难道就非得是‘再会’不可么？”

她带着一种奇异而晦暗的温存望着我，点了点头。

“因为耻辱，”她说道。“但倘若我是另一副模样，我倒极愿引你为知己挚友——”

对于我的插话，她充耳不闻，径自说道：“我只有一位（1位）情人。可我之所以知道这点，仅仅是因为我总觉得自己背叛了他。倘若能成为彻头彻尾的坏女人倒也痛快，可我甚至连坏女人都算不上。我不过是行差踏错、举止不端罢了。待我寻觅到我唯一的挚爱时，我定会与你重逢。抑或待我拥有了为纯洁赴死的资格时，我定会与你重逢。届时我会知会于你，好教你能亲临见证。愿上帝保佑你，亲爱的。”

我说了我该说的话：上帝借着艾丽丝·斯托姆的降临，已然赐福于我了。

她的脸色瞬间煞白。“这番话当被载入史册，”她低语道，“作为世上唯一曾令一个女人为其耻辱感到自惭形秽之男子的祷词。”

“我的冒险岁月，艾丽丝·斯托姆，早已一去不复返了。若是放在几年前，你如来时一般倏忽消失在伦敦这口巨大深渊之中，于我而言本不足为奇。依我看，对于精于冒险的行家里手而言，那向来是惯常的做法。可如今我却希望能留住你的一丝踪迹。你断不可就这般彻底弃我而去。倘若我再也见不到你，难道我甚至不能同你说说话么？或者，更为紧要的是，至少能让我感受到，倘若我胆子够大，我便有权同你说说话？”

她说她如今在伦敦不过是为了处理几周便能了结的公事，平日里始终定居在海外。“不过这是电话号码，”她说道；我正四下寻觅纸笔，她却喊道：“这儿！”只见她那只套着皮革衣袖的手臂迅捷探向地板，拾起了一本书，在书的扉页上用口红潦草地写下了号码。

在破晓清晨尖锐嘈杂的声浪之上，我听见了一声汽车电喇叭的刺耳鸣叫。

## II / 第二章 卑微生灵的骑士
===Original===

The Cavalier of Low Creatures

===Chinese===

卑微生灵的骑士

### I / 第一节
===Original===

And that, I think, is all that there really is about me, as a person, in the tale. Of course, this first person singular will continue, and there’ll no doubt be any amount of “I this” and “I that,” but that is because of the nature of the work, and there’s never, the way I see it, much more than a pen behind it. Hilary, however, and Guy de Travest are not of my mind about this. We have recently been talking about these affairs, and a sad enough talk they made, and my two friends, my two seniors, were reluctantly compelled, they said, to disagree with me about my lack of responsibility in the events to be related hereinafter.

To me, the way I see it, it looks as though certain things were decreed to happen and that, therefore, they did happen: they had it in their blood, these people, that certain things should happen to them, and I could no more contrive these things than they could evade them. But Hilary and Guy, murmuring together in that astonishing unison which can only be found in two Englishmen who disagree upon everything in the world but on the fact that conduct is three parts of life, are of opinion that my substitution of the word “ptomaine” for “septic” really affected the course of events. Had I, they say in effect, spoken the truth like a brave little man, there would have been a divorce and everyone would by now have been happy, as happiness goes. And then, too, they have something to say about those two red lights, those two rear-lamps of two cars sweeping into South Audley Street﻿—had I told Iris, they say, about Gerald, those two red lights never would have been so close together. Oh, Guy, what a man is that! That latter-day thunder-god of dandies, that warrior of conduct, that man of cold eyes who never could give “gratuitous information” about anyone! Oh, Hilary, that friend of childhood!

===Chinese===

我想，关于我个人在整个故事中的戏份，大抵也就仅此而已了。诚然，这种第一人称单数的叙述仍将延续，其间无疑少不了诸般“我这般”、“我那般”的说辞，但那全然是由本书的性质所决定的；依我愚见，这背后充其量不过是一管笔杆子罢了。然而，希拉里（Hilary）与盖伊·德·特拉维斯特（Guy de Travest）在此事上却与我意见相左。我们近来谈及这些往事，谈话着实令人伤感，而我的这两位挚友（2位挚友）、两位长辈（2位长辈）却声称，不得不勉为其难地在后文所述事件中我所谓“毫无干系”一事上驳斥我。

在我看来，某些事情冥冥之中注定要发生，因而便实实在在地发生了：这帮人的血液里本就注定了要遭逢某些劫数，我既无从凭空撮合这些事，正如他们亦无从逃脱一般。然而希拉里与盖伊——唯有两个在世间万事上皆各执己见、唯独在“操守品行乃人生之大半”这一信条上深信不疑的英国绅士之间，方能寻得这般惊人的异口同声——他们二人低声私语，一致认为我当初用“尸碱中毒（ptomaine）”一词替换了“败血脓毒（septic）”，委实左右了后续事态的发展。他们言下之意，倘若我当时能像个无畏的小男子汉那般吐露实情，他们早就办妥了离婚，而眼下人人本该各得其所、尽享所谓的人间之乐了。此外，他们对于南奥德利街（South Audley Street）疾驰而过的两辆轿车尾部的那两盏红灯（2盏红灯）也颇有微词——他们说，倘若我当初将杰拉尔德（Gerald）的实情转告艾丽丝（Iris），那两盏红灯断然不会贴得如此之近。哦，盖伊，那究竟是怎样的一个人啊！那位当代花花公子的雷霆之神，那位操守品行的捍卫勇士，那位目光冷峻、绝不会向任何人泄露“不请自来的闲话”的冷面绅士！哦，希拉里，那位自幼相识的童年挚友！

===Original===

Hilary and Guy, friends of the late Barty March, had known Gerald and Iris since their earliest childhood. But Gerald had no sooner grown up than, at the impulse of his furious nature, he had turned away from his friends, his people: he had dropped out, had cut away; and no one, it’s not difficult to imagine, would want to intrude on that young man. But I was to find, after the coming of the lady of the green hat, that it wasn’t only at the impulse of his furious nature that Gerald had, well, withered fiercely into solitude. In very truth that Gerald had been a hero-worshipper; and in very truth he had become, as his sister had said, a nothing without his hero. Very few things had ever mattered to Gerald Haveleur March; but those few things, one was to learn, had mattered far too much.

His sister was, as it’s not impossible to have gathered, what is called déclassé﻿—even for a March or a Portairley. And that was why I had heard nothing about her from Guy or Hilary, for while Guy never gave gratuitous information about anyone, Hilary was held in thrall by that upside-down but virulent form of snobbery which will make of a man of property an extreme Liberal and a thoroughgoing diehard disapprover of anyone who let his, Hilary’s, caste down. Hilary, a sincerely good man, was an enemy of caste, he was an enemy of his own caste in particular, he did not believe in it; and yet, in the depths of that being where lurks a dragon that can ultimately defeat even the sincerity of a man of principle, Hilary believed in nothing else but caste.

And Iris, of course, had betrayed her caste to perfection. No one, you might say, could have done that more thoroughly than Iris. She had been malinspired to excess, she had reached Excelsior in the abyss. But she was ever completely not on her guard about what people might say or did say, she had an amazing, an enviable, snapped Hilary, talent for just not noticing things.

She had been quite surprised, Hilary told me recently, when once he had taxed her with being a renegade from her class. Genuinely surprised she was, Hilary says. It simply hadn’t, she had told him, occurred to her in that light.

===Chinese===

希拉里与盖伊皆是故去的巴蒂·马奇（Barty March）生前的挚友，自杰拉尔德与艾丽丝幼年起便同他们相识。然而杰拉尔德刚一长大成人，便凭着那股狂暴暴烈的性子，决然背离了自己的朋友与族人：他遁世离群，断绝往来；且不难想见，根本无人愿意主动去招惹那位年轻人。然而在头戴绿帽的夫人造访之后，我方才察觉，杰拉尔德之所以这般决绝凄冷地遁入孤独，绝非仅仅是出自他狂暴的本性使然。在心底深处，杰拉尔德曾是一个彻头彻尾的英雄崇拜者；且千真万确的是，正如他胞妹所言，一旦失去了心中的英雄，他便沦为了一个一文不名的空壳。曾能教杰拉尔德·哈夫勒·马奇（Gerald Haveleur March）挂怀于心的物事实在少之又少；然而日后世人方知，那仅有的几桩物事，教他挂怀得未免太深、太沉。

一如诸位不难猜见的那般，他的胞妹已然沦为了所谓的“流品下堕（déclassé）”之辈——即便对马奇家族或波泰利（Portairley）家族而言亦是如此。这正是为何我此前从未自盖伊或希拉里口中听闻过她的半点风声：盖伊从来不会对旁人随意多嘴饶舌，而希拉里则深深受制于一种颠倒却恶毒的门第虚荣心——这种虚荣心足以教一位家产殷实的体面人摇身变为极端自由党人，同时又对任何败坏其同阶层门风体统之辈抱有彻头彻尾的顽固鄙夷。希拉里本是个发自肺腑的正人君子，平素视阶层特权如寇仇，尤其对自己所属的阶层深恶痛绝，他断不信那一套；然而，在那个终究能击溃任何信条操守的幽暗心魔深处，希拉里笃信不疑的偏偏唯独只有门第阶层。

而艾丽丝呢，自然是将自家阶层的信条背叛得彻彻底底。你尽可以说，世间无人能比艾丽丝背叛得更为淋漓尽致了。她受尽了过度的恶劣驱使，在堕落的无底深渊中攀上了极致的“精益求精（Excelsior）”。然而她对于旁人可能说些什么或实际上说了什么，向来毫无防备戒心；希拉里没好气地嚷道，她自有一种令人惊叹、教人艳羡的本领——那便是对一切流言蜚语视若无睹。

希拉里近来告诉我，有一回他当面指责艾丽丝是本阶级的叛徒时，她竟流露出一副万分诧异的模样。希拉里说，她是当真感到诧异。她当时对他说道，自己压根就从未从这个层面上思量过。

===Original===

“Rushing about Europe like that,” Hilary had said, “you let England down. You’ve no idea, Iris, how these young foreign blighters hold Englishwomen cheap.” Iris had maintained she had a very good idea about that. (But you simply had to disagree with Hilary. He was like that. And he said “hm” all the time.) And you only had to travel on a liner to the East, she had said, to notice how British matrons reacted to foreign parts. As for Egypt! But she always did her best, she had said, to influence foreigners to a more lofty view of the gallantries of British matrons.

“People cut you,” Hilary had said, for that seemed to him an abominable thing, that she should have put herself into the position of being “cut”; and she had admitted having noticed glaciers, but she had maintained that it was a far, far better thing to be cut by a county eye than to be killed by the boredom of a county tongue. “I arose from the dead when I was twenty,” she had said. (Hilary, you understand, would provoke anyone.) “Your class,” Hilary had snapped, and she had said she had never actually thought of herself as belonging to any class. Her class would be, she supposed, the landed gentry, same as Hilary’s. She was proud, she had said, to belong to the same class as Hilary, and was very sorry indeed if she had hit him in the eye with her heel. But she hoped, she had said, that with him she had always been a lady.

That had annoyed Hilary very much indeed. But everything about any woman he liked would annoy Hilary very much indeed. Mr. Townshend was one of those Englishmen with an unlimited capacity for disapproving of any woman, whom he liked, who enjoyed being with other men as much as with himself; and an unlimited capacity for finding other reasons than that for his disapproval.

===Chinese===

“整日在欧洲各处（Europe）这般乱窜，”希拉里曾训斥道，“你丢尽了英格兰（England）的脸面。艾丽丝，你根本不知道那些外国的年轻浑小子有多瞧不起英国女子。”艾丽丝却坚称自己心里比谁都清楚。（可你横竖总忍不住要同希拉里抬杠。他就是那副德性，而且嘴里成天‘哼’个没完。）她说道，你只需搭乘一艘开往远东的客轮，便能亲眼瞧见英国的尊贵夫人们在异国他乡是副什么光景。至于在埃及（Egypt）就更不用提了！不过她说道，自己向来竭尽所能，引导外国人体谅英国夫人们的风流韵事，树立起更为崇高的见解。

“人家都同你断绝往来、对你视若无睹，”希拉里曾说道，皆因在他看来，她竟将自己置于遭人“当面无视”的境地，委实是一桩骇人听闻的丑事；而她承认自己确实领略过那般冰山般的冷遇，但她坚称，被郡里乡绅们白眼相待，远比被乡绅名流们枯燥乏味的舌头活活闷死要好上千百倍。“我二十岁那年（20岁那年）便已从死人堆里复活过一回了，”她说道。（诸位心知肚明，希拉里换作任何人都招架不住。）“你所属的阶层！”希拉里厉声喝道，而她却说自己实际上从未想过自己属于哪个阶层。她揣度自家阶层大抵属于乡绅乡爵之流，同希拉里一样。她说道，能同希拉里属于同一阶层，她深感荣幸，倘若自己曾用鞋跟碰伤了他的眼睛，她着实万分抱歉。不过她说道，她希望在面对他时，自己始终称得上是一位名媛淑女。

这话委实把希拉里惹恼得七窍生烟。然而，但凡是他心仪的女子身上的任何一桩琐事，都足以教希拉里恼羞成怒。汤森先生（Mr. Townshend）正是那种典型的英国绅士：对于自己心仪的女子，倘若对方乐意周旋于其他男人之间同乐意伴其左右一般无二，他便有着无穷无尽的鄙夷苛责之能事；且除了这层真实原由之外，他更有着无穷无尽的本领为此编造出诸般冠冕堂皇的借口。

===Original===

As for Gerald, Hilary had last known him as a “dark diabolical schoolboy” with a disturbing capacity for threatening silences and an immense﻿—“a corroding, almost,” Hilary said﻿—admiration for Iris. But not long after Barty March’s death﻿—everyone had loved *that* drunkard!﻿—he had quite lost sight of Gerald. Guy de Travest had been Gerald’s colonel in the Grenadiers for some time during the war, but he never spoke but once of Gerald as a soldier﻿—“young hellfire idiot”﻿—and never went near him while he lived above me in Shepherd’s Market. “Reminds me,” Guy said, “too much of Barty left standing too long with the cork out.” And that was more or less what Hilary said, too. One must say this for the warriors of caste and conduct: they seldom try to improve any man.

This chapter has been called The Cavalier of Low Creatures because it is about Gerald, and therefore it is a short chapter, for what on earth is there to say about Gerald? It isn’t at all a good description of him, but it is intended, if you please, more as a flourish, a naive gesture. For you simply can’t let Gerald stand without a flourish, without a something, anything. Besides, I liked him, and would like to do him a bit of good. He was, sans gesture, a zero with a scowl and a hat﻿—and a hat. Certainly, he once wrote a novel, but who does not once write a novel? I liked Gerald, but I would not give him a line if he wasn’t essential; and that is just what he is, essential, for these things simply couldn’t have happened without Gerald. He hated his sister, he had not seen her for ten years, yet it turned out that he was the most important factor in her life. And, decidedly, her love for him was one of the most important factors in *her* life. I wonder if he knows. But he too, even he, grew up in the end. I can hear him now, through the twilight of East Chapel Street, his shoulder against the saloon-door of the inn. “Give her my love,” he said. But you will hear him.

===Chinese===

至于杰拉尔德，希拉里对他的最后印象，不过是一个“阴郁魔怔的学生仔”，擅长令人不安的威慑性沉默，且对艾丽丝抱有极其深沉——希拉里称之为“几乎到了销魂蚀骨的地步”——的崇拜之情。但在巴蒂·马奇死后不久——人人都爱戴那位酒鬼！——他便同杰拉尔德彻底断了音讯。战争期间，盖伊·德·特拉维斯特曾一度在近卫掷弹兵联队（Grenadiers）担任杰拉尔德的上校团长，但他谈及作为行伍士兵的杰拉尔德时却仅有一句评语——“年轻的亡命疯子”——且在杰拉尔德住在我牧羊人集市寓所楼上期间，盖伊从未涉足探访。“他教我联想起了巴蒂，”盖伊说道，“简直就像一瓶拔了软木塞搁置太久的烈酒。”而希拉里的话大抵也是这个意思。对于这帮门第操守的捍卫者，世人不得不承认一点：他们绝少妄图去感化改良任何凡人。

本章之所以定名为《卑微生灵的骑士》（The Cavalier of Low Creatures），全因本章写的是杰拉尔德；正因如此，这篇章节篇幅短小，皆因关于杰拉尔德究竟有什么好大书特书的呢？用这名号来形容他断不算贴切，但若蒙诸位首肯，这无非是作为一笔修饰与渲染，一种天真率性的致意。皆因你断然不能任由杰拉尔德光秃秃地立在那儿，全无半点虚饰，全无半点点缀。更何况，我偏爱于他，且希望能稍稍为他说几句好话。若是褪去姿态修饰，他不过是一个整日蹙眉、头戴毡帽——一顶寒酸毡帽——的空洞虚无。诚然，他曾写过一部小说，可世间又有谁没写过一部半部小说呢？我固然喜欢杰拉尔德，但若非他至关紧要，我绝不会分给他半行笔墨；而他偏偏正是至关紧要之人，皆因若是没有杰拉尔德，后文诸般变故便断然不可能上演。他痛恨自己的胞妹，两人已有十年未曾谋面（10年未曾谋面），然而事实证明，他偏偏是她生命中最举足轻重的一环。而且毫无疑问，她对他的深情，正是她生命中最举足轻重的一环。不知他是否明了于心。但他终究，乃至连他这般人，最后也长大成人了。我如今仿佛仍能隔着东礼拜堂街（East Chapel Street）的暮色听见他的声音，他的肩膀倚靠着酒馆雅座的大门。“替我向她问好，”他说道。不过诸位后文自会听见他的言语。

### I / 第二节
===Original===

Sometimes I would see Gerald in the Café Royal. I would be dining, with Hilary maybe, and in the distance, cut as with a sharp knife in the tapestry of smoke and grubby faces, would be Gerald, darkly alone, a glass of whisky on the marble-top before him. One wouldn’t attempt to join him, for it made Gerald shy, desperate, if anyone sat with him while drinking. He hated being “messed about,” did Gerald; and if you joined him he would presently mutter something about an appointment (Gerald with an appointment!), leave his drink unfinished and go and order one somewhere else; and as I understood he hadn’t much money I did not like to drive him to that. Maybe, though, he was less shy with me than with anyone. “I like you,” he once said﻿—oh, darkly!

One never knew, as he sat there or as he strode about the streets, careless as a fakir impelled always towards a terrible and nameless penance, what he could be thinking of. Maybe he was thinking of nothing. Once I saw him come out of a Cinema Theatre with a look on his face as though he had been tortured. He always looked, you know, like something. You noticed him.

He had a grey suit. It was thin as paper, but still defiantly retained a little of that casual elegance which not even Gerald could wholly divorce from the combination of a good tailor and a lean Englishman. He never had but one other suit that I ever saw, a brown affair, but he bartered that with a boot-mender in Shepherd’s Market in exchange for mending his shoes. And he had a hat. That was a hat. And never was Gerald seen wearing an overcoat, no matter whether it blew, rained, snowed or froze. See him any winter evening striding down Half-Moon Street in the biting rain, his thin grey suit blackening with it, the jacket held by one button with deep creases into his waist, the shapes of his knuckles stuck through his trouser pockets, that hat﻿—there, but for the grace of God, went the most lovable man I ever met.

“Gerald﻿—I say, Gerald! Why don’t you wear a coat on a day like this? Gerald, aren’t you an ass!”

===Chinese===

有时我会在皇家咖啡馆（Café Royal）撞见杰拉尔德。我或许正同希拉里一道用餐，而在烟雾缭绕与尘俗面孔织就的图景深处，宛如被利刃劈开一般，便能望见杰拉尔德的身影——形单影只，神情阴郁，面前的大理石桌面上搁着一杯威士忌。没人会贸然上前同他搭话，皆因饮酒时若有任何人同坐，总教杰拉尔德感到拘谨与焦躁。杰拉尔德最讨厌被人“胡乱纠缠”；倘若你凑过去坐下，他不多时便会嘟囔着借口说自己另有约会（杰拉尔德居然会有约会！），丢下未喝完的酒杯，转去别处另点一杯；而据我所知他手头素来拮据，我不愿平白将他逼到那般田地。不过，比起面对旁人，他在我面前或许倒没那么拘束。“我挺喜欢你，”他有一回曾这般说道——哦，神情阴郁极了！

无论他是坐在那儿，还是在大街上阔步游荡，宛如一位漫不经心却始终被驱策着去履行某种可怖而无名之苦修的托钵僧，旁人永远无从揣度他心中究竟在想些什么。或许他脑海中其实一片空白。有一回我见他从一家电影院走出来，脸上的神色仿佛刚受过一轮严刑拷打。诸位心知肚明，他举手投足总带着某种独特的派头。你总会忍不住注意到他。

他有一套灰色的西装。那料子薄得如同一张纸，却仍旧桀骜地保留着一丝慵懒的优雅——那是即便杰拉尔德也无法彻底将一位优秀裁缝与一个精瘦英国绅士的组合所抹杀的风度。在我的记忆中，除了这一套，他便只有另外一套褐色的旧衣裳，可他早就把那套褐色西装同牧羊人集市的一位修鞋匠换了修鞋的工钱。他还有一顶帽子。那委实是一顶绝妙的旧帽子。而且无论狂风呼啸、淫雨霏霏、大雪纷飞还是天寒地冻，从没人见杰拉尔德穿过大衣外套。在任何一个冬夜，你总能瞧见他在刺骨冷雨中大步流星走在半月街（Half-Moon Street）上，单薄的灰色西装被雨水浸得发黑，上衣仅靠一颗纽扣扣着，腰间勒出深深的褶皱，指节的轮廓硬生生顶在裤袋里，戴着那顶帽子——若非上帝庇佑，那真是我生平所遇最教人怜爱的一个汉子。

“杰拉尔德——我说，杰拉尔德！在这样的鬼天气里，你怎么连件大衣外套都不穿？杰拉尔德，你难道是个傻子不成！”

===Original===

“Coat?” Thoughtful he was always, and his dark, sunk eyes would pierce the pavement or the sky with unutterable contempt. “Coat!” And he would repeat the word softly until, you understand, he had grasped the enormous idea, when he would say softly, savagely: “What the hell d’you mean, ‘coat’?” and away he would go, towards that terrible and nameless penance of his.

Well, the flourish goes, the gesture is gone, to the limbo that yawns for all such vanities in the very second of their birth. The Cavalier of Low Creatures was never, to be sure, hailed as more than a zero. But, even as the ground is not the limit of a man’s fall, as you may see in the picture with the trail of flame, so zero is not the limit of a man’s nothingness; for what is that which is nothing but so completely nothing that it may not have even the mark of nothing? It is, to be sure, zero without the formative circle round it.

That solitary drunkard, that soiled ascetic! Those nightmare women, soft as the grass of Parnassus, marvellously acquiescent, possible. Aphrodite, Ariadne, Anaïtis, white as marble, silent as marble, silent and acquiescent, possible, as only goddesses could be, the goddesses of soiled dreams, as no woman born of woman could ever be.﻿ ﻿…

And yet one might have been wrong in imagining the malcontents of the solitary drunkard’s mind. God only knows, of course, with what nightmare fancies the man plagued himself. Boys have them, and grow out of them; men, at least, do not admit even to themselves that they have not grown out of them, men do not admit even to themselves that while they indulge in continence they may suddenly find themselves stumbling in the burning darkness among the vile rubbish-heaps of desire.

===Chinese===

“大衣？”他向来若有所思，那一双深陷幽暗的眼眸总带着难以言喻的蔑视刺向人行道或苍穹。“大衣！”他会轻声重复这个字眼，诸位明白，直到他终于领会了这个宏大的念头，方才低沉而凶狠地啐道：“你到底冲我说‘大衣’是发什么神经？”随后他便扬长而去，径自奔向他那可怖而无名的苦修。

罢了，修饰已逝，姿态不再，尽皆归于那在虚妄降生刹那便张开巨口的虚无深渊之中。千真万确，《卑微生灵的骑士》从来未曾被捧为超越虚无零蛋的存在。然而，正如大地并非凡人下坠的极限——正如诸位在带有火焰轨迹的画幅中所见的那般——零蛋亦非一个人空虚虚无的极限；皆因何谓纯然的虚无，乃至彻底空无一物到连虚无的标记都不配拥有？千真万确，那便是一个连外围轮廓圆圈都不复存在的零蛋。

那个形单影只的酒鬼，那个蒙尘的苦行僧！那些如梦魇般的妇人，柔嫩宛若帕纳苏斯山（Parnassus）的青草，奇迹般顺从驯服，触手可及。阿芙洛狄忒（Aphrodite）、阿里阿德涅（Ariadne）、阿娜伊蒂斯（Anaïtis），洁白如大理石，沉寂如大理石，缄默而顺从，触手可及——唯有女神方能如此，那些污浊春梦中的女神，绝非凡间妇人所能企及……

然而，人们凭空揣度这位孤僻酒鬼心头的怨懑，或许本就是大错特错。当然，唯有上帝方才知晓那汉子究竟用何等梦魇般的幻想折磨着自家。少年人尚有此类心绪，且终会破茧蜕变；成年的男儿，至少绝不肯向自家承认自己尚未摆脱这般桎梏，男儿们绝不肯向自家承认，当他们沉溺于清心寡欲之时，冷不丁便会发觉自家在焦灼的幽暗之中、在欲望污秽的垃圾堆里踉跄跌撞。

===Original===

That women walked in all the delicious beauty of the unattainable through Gerald’s tortured mind, I know now. But I did not know it then, for never was a man so secret with another man as Gerald, never was a man so little given to discussing with another those inevitable matters of desire and concupiscence which only by being discussed can be seen in a proper and proportionate light. They should be aired, those secret silly things, that they may be seen for what they are. In the old days there was a god in a garden, and people would do their best to make pretty fancies out of their lusts, naming them to gods, satyrs, fawns, nymphs, sirens, sylphs; they, at least, got rid of them somehow. But now that we see them plainly for what they are, the nasty little enemies of our assault on nobility, a conspiracy founded by Saint Paul has smashed the god in the garden and hidden the pieces under the bed.

Gerald, who never spoke but he swore, was the cleanest-mouthed man I ever met with; while from his book one had gathered that there was one main idea in Gerald’s mind; this was purity. It was to do with that one brilliant-childish romance of his that, about seven years before the coming of the green hat, I had first met Gerald. Then, for more than five years, I had not seen nor heard of him, had forgotten him, when one day a lean, dangerous hawk of a young man coming out of the Hammam Baths in Jermyn Street suddenly stopped me. I knew later that he must have been in an agony of shyness, but at the time he merely looked intensely furious. I, not recognising him, thought he was going to hit me, and gaped at him.

Bitterly and darkly he told me that someone had told him there was a flat to be let above me in Shepherd’s Market. “I’m staying here at the moment,” he muttered, looking indignantly at the Hammam Baths. Several minutes passed before I could place him, for he had been in uniform that first time, in that transfiguring long-waisted grey coat of the Brigade of Guards.

===Chinese===

如今我方才知晓，女人们曾以那般可望而不可即的美妙姿态，穿行过杰拉尔德饱受折磨的心智。然而在当年我却对此一无所知，皆因世间从无一个男儿像杰拉尔德这般对旁人守口如瓶，从无一个男儿像他那般极少同旁人探讨那些不可避免的欲望与肉欲——那些唯有借着探讨方能置于恰当与合度之光芒下审视的心事。那些隐秘痴愚的心思本该宣泄晾晒出来，好教世人看清它们的本来面目。在往昔岁月里，园囿之中尚供奉着神祇，人们竭尽所能将自家的欲念编织成华美的遐想，将其命名为诸神、萨堤洛斯（satyrs）、半羊人（fauns）、仙女（nymphs）、塞壬（sirens）以及西尔芙（sylphs）；古人好歹借此将欲念宣泄遣散了。然而如今，当我们清清楚楚看清了它们的真面目——看清它们正是阻挠我们追求崇高的卑劣小敌——由圣保罗（Saint Paul）肇始的合谋却砸碎了园囿中的神像，并将碎块统统藏匿到了床榻之下。

杰拉尔德开口必带粗话咒骂，却是我平生所遇心思口齿最干净纯洁的汉子；而从他的著作中，人们亦不难发现杰拉尔德心头盘踞着一个核心念头：那便是纯洁。正是在绿帽子夫人降临前大约七年（约7年前），因着他那段璀璨而稚嫩的传奇罗曼史，我初次结识了杰拉尔德。在此之后长达五年多（5年多）的光阴里，我未曾见过他，亦未曾听闻过他的音讯，早已将他淡忘；直到有一天，在哲敏街（Jermyn Street）的土耳其浴室（Hammam Baths）门前，一个精瘦凶悍、宛如雄鹰般的年轻汉子冷不丁拦住了我的去路。日后我方知，他当时定然陷入了极度的羞怯折磨之中，但彼时他看上去却仅仅是一副怒不可遏的可怖模样。我一时没能认出他来，还以为他要向我挥拳动粗，惊愕地瞪大了眼睛望着他。

他神情阴郁而悲苦地告诉我，有人对他说我牧羊人集市寓所的楼上有一套空房招租。“我眼下暂住在这儿，”他嘟囔着，满脸愤懑地扫了一眼土耳其浴室。过了好几分钟，我才终于想起了他的身份，皆因我们初次相见时他身着戎装，穿着近卫步兵旅（Brigade of Guards）那件令人脱胎换骨的束腰灰色长大衣。

===Original===

Gerald appeared suddenly, in the winter of 1915, at the office of Horton’s *New Voice*. Now that Horton has left England on his adventure in un-individualism one does not hear much of *The New Voice*, but at that time and for long before *The New Voice* was, of course, a power, and Horton was a Power. Quite apart from Horton’s personal quality, you knew he was a Power because several of the greatest of the intelligent writers of our time kept on bitterly pointing out to their million readers what a futile man Horton was. Quite a number of the men whose names you can “conjure with” now﻿—it would be fun to meet that man who is always in the street conjuring with names!﻿—had begun by writing for Horton’s paper; but they had always gotten on his nerves by the time they had become the greatest of the intelligent writers of our time, and, since Horton was an honest man, he told them so, and he told them why, and he told them off, and they were furious. But the most inspired among the greatest of the intelligent writers of our time revenged themselves by republishing their *New Voice* stuff in book-form and omitting to mention *The New Voice* as the first medium of publication. That was discourteous of them.

We were correcting proofs when Gerald appeared. It was a Monday afternoon, and on Monday afternoons any of Horton’s writers who wished could turn up and correct either his own or someone else’s proofs and then go and have tea at the A.B.C. And Talk.

“Hello!” said Horton. “Hel*lo*!”

“Defence of the Realm,” murmured Home.

===Chinese===

杰拉尔德是在1915年隆冬冷不丁现身于霍顿（Horton）的《新声》（*New Voice*）编辑部的。如今霍顿已然离开英格兰去追寻他那非个人主义的探险，世人已不大听闻《新声》的名号，但在那个年月以及更早的长久岁月里，《新声》无疑是一股举足轻重的势力，而霍顿本人也是个呼风唤雨的显赫人物。抛开霍顿个人的才干品性不谈，你单凭当代几位最为卓越的知识分子作家成天向数以百万计的读者痛心疾首地抨击霍顿是个何等无能之辈，便深知他确凿是个人物。如今凡是名头响当当、能用来“呼风唤雨”的文坛巨擘——若能在大街上撞见那位整日借着大名招摇过市的魔术师倒委实有趣！——起初大抵都是给霍顿的刊物撰稿起家的；然而每当他们熬成当代最卓绝的才智作家时，往往早已把霍顿的神经惹得不胜其烦；偏偏霍顿是个秉性耿直的人，他直言不讳地告知他们原委，当面毫不留情地把他们痛骂一顿，教这帮大家们气得暴跳如雷。不过，在这帮当代最出类拔萃的才子之中，最富灵性之辈往往借着将自己在《新声》上刊载的旧稿结集成书、并故意隐去《新声》乃是首发刊物一事来进行报复。这委实有失体统礼数。

杰拉尔德现身的那天，我们正忙着校对清样。那是一个星期一下午，在周一下午，霍顿麾下但凡得空的撰稿人尽可自行前来，校对自家或旁人的清样，随后一道踱步到A.B.C.茶室去喝茶。并且高谈阔论。

“你好啊！”霍顿说道。“你*好*啊！”

“《王国保卫条例》（Defence of the Realm）来啦，”休姆（Home）低声咕哝道。

===Original===

We were not prepared for Gerald. We had, of course, seen soldiers before; indeed, there was one in the room at the moment, the philosopher Home, who was to be killed a year or so later. But Gerald was a Figure, he was martial. The herald of the dominion of hell upon earth, that was Gerald. Take one small, frowsty room, the staff (Miss Veale) addressing wrappers at a desk by the window, Horton blue-pencilling at the other desk by the door, four of us sitting cramped round correcting proofs on bound annuals of *The New Voice* on our knees, smoking, muttering﻿—enter six-foot-two of the Brigade of Guards with a face as dark as night and the nose of a hawk and the eyes of one who has seen Christ crucified in vain. The panoply of war sat superbly on Gerald. He looked a soldier in the real rather than in the technical sense of the word: he looked, you know, as though he had accepted death and was just living anyhow in the meanwhile. Ah, see him then! Not even Gerald’s malevolent slackness in attire could make that long-waisted grey coat with the red-silk lining sit on him but imperially. Not Gerald’s the common-or-garden chubby face of a Guard’s subaltern. Gerald was no chap. He glowered at us.

“Eh,” he stammered. “I say﻿ ﻿… I’ve been told you people.﻿ ﻿…”

“He’s heard about us,” said Home sympathetically. “Sit down, boy. On the floor, I’m afraid.”

Gerald began a fierce scowl at him﻿—then grinned. Dear Gerald!

“Well?” smiled Horton. Always courteous was Horton, in manner.

“Heard,” muttered Gerald, “that you didn’t care what you published.﻿ ﻿…”

“Oh!” said Horton. “Well, we don’t care how good it is, if that’s what you mean.”

You couldn’t guess that Gerald was so shy that he could scarcely speak. You thought he stammered just because he stammered, not because he was so shy that he could scarcely get a word out. A man had no right to look like Gerald, an ensign of the fallen Prince of Light, and be shy; but that was always Gerald’s trouble, he never was given the credit for being shy, he put himself between you and any sympathy with him, he made it clear that he didn’t want your infernal sympathy. Just then, for instance, he looked as though he had strayed into *The New Voice* to send us all to blazes on general principles. And Horton looked as though he was quite prepared to go. Horton preferred bad-tempered men.

===Chinese===

我们对杰拉尔德的不期而至全无防备。我们自然并非没见过当兵的；事实上，当时屋里就坐着一位军人——哲学家休姆（Home），大约一年后（约1年后）他便阵亡殉国了。然而杰拉尔德委实是个非同凡响的傲然人物，周身充满着凛冽的军旅杀伐之气。地狱在人间君临统治的先驱使者，那便是杰拉尔德。试想一间逼仄污浊的陋室：唯一的职员（维尔小姐，Miss Veale）正坐在靠窗的办公桌前填写邮寄封套，霍顿在靠门的另一张办公桌前用蓝铅笔删改稿件，我们四个人（4个人）局促地围坐在一旁，膝头摊着《新声》的年度合订本校对清样，吸着烟，嘴里念念有词——冷不丁，一位身高六英尺两英寸（6英尺2英寸）、隶属近卫步兵旅的军官闯了进来：面容漆黑如夜，鼻若鹰隼，那一双眸子宛如亲眼目睹了基督徒然受难在十字架上一般。那一身戎装披挂在杰拉尔德身上，气度非凡。与其说他是字面条例意义上的行伍军人，毋宁说他是真正意义上的战士：诸位心知肚明，他看上去仿佛早已坦然领受了死亡的宿命，眼下不过是漫不经心地随性苟活罢了。啊，且瞧瞧当年的他吧！即便杰拉尔德那肆无忌惮的邋遢穿戴，也无法掩盖那件带有红绸里衬的束腰灰色长大衣穿在他身上所散发出的帝王般威严。杰拉尔德绝没有近卫连队初级尉官那种司空见惯的圆滚滚胖脸。杰拉尔德绝非等闲平庸之辈。他怒目圆睁地瞪着我们。

“呃，”他结结巴巴地说道，“我说……有人同我说起过你们这帮人……”

“他听闻过咱们的名头啦，”休姆满怀同情地插话道，“坐吧，小伙子。恐怕只能委屈你坐地板上了。”

杰拉尔德起初凶神恶煞地狠狠瞪了他一眼——随即却咧嘴笑了。亲爱的杰拉尔德！

“怎么说？”霍顿微笑着问道。霍顿在待人接物上向来彬彬有礼。

“听说，”杰拉尔德嘟囔道，“你们不论收到什么烂稿子都敢照登不误……”

“哦！”霍顿说道，“嗯，倘若你指的是好稿子，那么不管文章有多好，我们确凿都不在乎。”

你绝难猜想到杰拉尔德竟羞怯得几乎吐不出字来。你以为他结巴不过是天生口吃，绝不会想到他竟是因为害羞得无地自容而语无伦次。一个生得像杰拉尔德这般——宛如堕落的光明之子的旗手——的汉子，本无权这般腼腆怯懦；可这偏偏始终是杰拉尔德的症结所在：没人会相信他是在害羞，他硬生生横亘在旁人与对他的怜惜同情之间，斩钉截铁地表明自己根本不屑于你那该死的同情。就拿彼时而言吧，他瞧上去仿佛之所以误闯进《新声》编辑部，纯粹是为了凭着自家一贯的行事准则把我们统统轰进地狱里去。而霍顿的神情却显得仿佛早已做好了下地狱的万全准备。霍顿向来偏爱坏脾气的人。

===Original===

“There’s this,” Gerald muttered, and lugged out an enormous typescript from the deep pocket of his grey coat. “Novel,” he scowled at Horton. “Thought perhaps.﻿ ﻿…” and he planted the thing with a thump on Horton’s desk. Horton grinned. Horton had had much too much to do with professional novelists to think that a novel by a subaltern of Grenadiers was necessarily unreadable. “Bit long, isn’t it?” he smiled.

“Long?” Gerald stammered. “Of course it’s long! Been writing it for four whole months.”

“Ought to be good,” said Home gravely.

“It’s *awful*,” grinned Gerald, “but, you see.﻿ ﻿…”

“Quite,” said Horton busily. “Now, I’ll.﻿ ﻿…”

“Hello!” said Horton, for Gerald was not. Horton threw the typescript to me to read. Of course, it was mad. *The New Voice* published most of it, and then Heinemann’s published it in the autumn of 1916 and ran it into three editions while people were still disentangling their eyes from the paper wrapper, which showed a woman with purple eyes crucifying a pleasant young man.

*The Savage Device* is open before me as I write, and its opening lines are: “The history of Felix Burton is the history of an ideal and a vision. They had nothing to do with one another except that the pursuit of the vision hardened him and blooded him for the attainment of the ideal. The ideal was aristocratic, in the sense that it was a striving after nobility in life: the vision was a contradiction, as scientific as it was mystic. The ideal was, of course, defeated: the vision, of course, defeated him. The ideal was purity: the vision had something to do with pain.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“还有这个，”杰拉尔德嘟囔道，从他那件灰色大衣深不见底的口袋里掏出一大捆沉甸甸的打字油印稿。“一部小说，”他蹙眉瞪着霍顿，“心想或许……”说着，他把稿子“砰”的一声重重砸在霍顿的案头。霍顿咧嘴笑了。霍顿同形形色色的职业小说家打过太多交道，绝不至于武断地认为近卫掷弹兵联队一位初级尉官写的小说就必然不堪卒读。“篇幅未免有点长吧？”他微笑着问道。

“长？”杰拉尔德结巴道，“当然长啦！整整写了四个月（4个月）呢。”

“那想必不同凡响，”休姆严肃地说道。

“其实*烂透了*，”杰拉尔德咧嘴笑道，“可你们瞧……”

“明白，”霍顿忙不迭地应道，“眼下，我且……”

“哎呀！”霍顿惊呼道，皆因杰拉尔德早已脚底抹油不见了踪影。霍顿把那叠打字稿扔给我读。显而易见，这书委实疯癫狂妄。不过《新声》还是刊载了其中的大半篇幅；随后海涅曼出版社（Heinemann’s）在1916年秋天将其结集出版，当世人尚在费劲移开定定凝视那绘有一名紫眸女子将一个相貌讨喜的年轻小伙钉死在十字架上的护封插画的目光时，此书便一连加印了三版（3版）。

此刻在撰写此文时，《野蛮圈套》（*The Savage Device*）正摊开在我的面前，开篇头几行是这般写的：“费利克斯·伯顿（Felix Burton）的生平，便是一段理想与异象的交织史。二者本风马牛不相及，唯独对异象的追逐磨砺了他的心志、教他历经杀伐淬炼，从而为追寻崇高理想奠定了根基。所谓理想，乃是贵族式的追求，意指对生命中至高尊贵的执着追寻；而异象则是一组矛盾体，既合乎科学玄理，又充斥着神秘幽奥。理想自然以破灭告终；异象自然教他身败名裂。那份理想关乎纯洁；而异象则不可避免地同痛苦紧紧纠缠……”

===Original===

The “vision,” so far as one could see, had everything to do with pain; in fact it was pain, and the vision might or might not come afterwards. (And I detest that word “mystic.”) The book was exciting and interesting because of a strange mixture of high romance, desperate villainy and an abysmal bitterness. The war came in, naturally. Gerald’s hero had minority ideas about the war﻿—letting the landed gentry down again! As for the pain﻿ ﻿… Young Burton’s idea of it had not to do with pain as a fact, but as the most sublime among drugs. You know? “In fact,” Gerald wrote, “it is the only drug that cannot debase a man. It can kill him, but there are worse ways of dying than being killed.” It was full of quotations like that, but Gerald threw them at you with a dash sadly lacking in the originals. Young Burton was, of course, going to die in the war.

Young Burton, it appeared, had studied the major and minor tortures of crime and martyrdom. There was a long description of tortures, if you liked that kind of thing. I have seen Gerald’s books on them, with illustrations﻿ ﻿… very interesting. Then young Burton had come across the old, old idea that after a certain limit of pain there is a definite state of bliss and definite and glorious visions of a real reality which men by ordinary are too sodden or too timorous to see. But poor old Gerald, try as he would, couldn’t make *The Savage Device* a novel of ideas: it remained a novel of adventure, with an inhuman interest. Young Burton went everywhere in the world, having adventures, getting magnificently hurt﻿—South Sea stuff﻿—studying the effect of pain on men’s minds. A Chinese bandit helped him to quite a number of visions.

===Chinese===

就旁人所能领会而言，所谓“异象”全然与痛苦脱不开干系；事实上它本身便是痛苦，至于异象究竟是否会在痛苦之后降临，倒全凭机缘。（我素来最厌恶“神秘”这个字眼。）这部书之所以扣人心弦、趣味盎然，皆因它奇异地糅合了高尚的浪漫情怀、亡命徒般的邪恶以及深不见底的辛酸苦楚。大战的情节自然不可或缺。杰拉尔德笔下的主人公对大战抱有一套小众离经叛道的偏见——再次令乡绅阶层大失所望！至于那痛苦嘛……年轻的伯顿对它的理解，并非将其视作既成事实的皮肉之苦，而是视作世间最崇高的麻醉灵药。诸位可明白？“事实上，”杰拉尔德写道，“这是世上唯一不会教一个人品格堕落的灵药。它固然能置人于死地，可世间更有比死于非命更为凄惨的殒命之道。”全书充斥着此类警句名言，但杰拉尔德将它们砸向你时所带有的那种凌厉风发，却是前人原句中令人扼腕缺失的。年轻的伯顿，自然注定要在大战中马革裹尸。

这位年轻的伯顿似乎潜心钻研过古往今来针对罪愆与殉道的诸般大小酷刑。倘若你偏爱此道，书中自有一大段详尽细致的酷刑描绘。我曾翻阅过杰拉尔德搜集的相关专著，里面还配有插图……着实引人入胜。随后，年轻的伯顿偶然接触到了一种古老久远的观点：当肉体痛苦跨越某一极限之后，便会催生出一种确凿无疑的至乐化境，以及对真正现实之清晰而辉煌的异象洞见——而寻常凡夫俗子往往因过于浑浑噩噩或生性胆怯而无缘得见。然而可怜的老杰拉尔德任凭怎样绞尽脑汁，也未能将《野蛮圈套》写成一部思想观念小说：它自始至终仍是一部充斥着反人类趣味的冒险小说。年轻的伯顿行遍天涯海角，四处冒险历难，落得遍体鳞伤却壮怀激烈——诸如南太平洋（South Sea）风情云云——只为探究痛苦对人心智的影响。一位中国土匪甚至助他催生出了好几次玄妙的异象。

===Original===

Then he plucked Ava Foe from a “dive” in San Francisco, she became Mrs. Burton, and then he had every opportunity for judging the visionary qualities of mental pain. That part was fiendishly well written, the hell that Ava Burton gave him. But young Burton’s ideal of purity was, naturally enough, schoolboy stuff: fine in parts, but stuff. The only part of it that was good was that it was, somehow, purity. On the sexual side young Burton deserved almost all he got from his, one thought, unnecessarily callous young wife. In Ava Foe, I couldn’t help thinking after the coming of the green hat, Gerald had let himself go about Iris. I realised then how he must first have worshipped and then hated his twin sister. What on earth, one wondered, could she have done to him to make him hate her like that? Ava wasn’t in the least like her, of course, but Ava might quite well have been like any sister to any brother who hated her. But this fierce, devilish, medieval passion﻿—why? Yet I should have guessed something of the reason after Iris had told me that young Burton was “Boy,” Gerald’s hero of before the war. But it never occurred to me to connect Iris’s casually dropped “Boy” with the legendary Boy Fenwick of Careless-Days-Before-the-War fame. He will have his place, that dead Boy Fenwick. A deep place.

“Felix Burton’s” idea of what a man should be to live nobly﻿—he was full of those large strivings of Young Men which were in vogue in the Careless-Days-Before-the-War﻿—seemed to take the form of wanting to found a new race of something like potent eunuchs. Young Burton was, of course, without the lusts of the body. Ava Foe wasn’t. Nor did young Burton want any of your waste of time in graceful lovemaking; he wanted a sort of ruthless companionship, with occasional patches of mating; he did *not* want to procreate gracefully, but with a sort of furious absentmindedness. Imagine Ava﻿—Iris! Imagine Gerald himself drawing the woman of his nightmares, that soft possible woman of lonely dreams, detesting her for destroying him﻿ ﻿… and for destroying Boy! One wondered, in reading, if Gerald had ever known a woman. The dark knight of purity﻿ ﻿… the fallen knight of purity, but how fallen!

===Chinese===

随后，他自旧金山（San Francisco）的一处下等“下流酒吧”里搭救了艾娃·福（Ava Foe），她摇身一变成为了伯顿夫人，于是他便获得了千载难逢的良机去评判精神痛苦所蕴含的异象特质。那一部分写得入木三分、堪称魔性绝伦——描摹艾娃·伯顿带给他的无间地狱。然而年轻的伯顿心头那份关于纯洁的理想，自然不过是学生仔的幼稚玩意：虽有几分可圈可点之处，终究是幼稚胡诌。其间唯一值得称道的，便是它不知怎的确实称得上是纯洁。在两性私隐一端，年轻的伯顿从他那位在旁人看来冷酷得有些过分的小娇妻那里所遭受的一切冷遇，几乎全是他咎由自取。在头戴绿帽的夫人造访之后，我禁不住暗自忖度，在艾娃·福这个人物身上，杰拉尔德分明是对艾丽丝肆意倾泻着怨愤。我彼时方才顿悟，他定然是先将自己的孪生胞妹奉若神明，随后又对她恨之入骨。世人难免揣度，她究竟对他做过些什么，竟教他恨她恨到这般地步？当然，艾娃身上全无半点艾丽丝的影子，但对于任何一个痛恨自家族妹的兄长而言，艾娃倒真真切切像是世上任何一个惹人痛恨的妹妹。然而这股狂暴、魔怔、宛若中世纪幽灵般的激愤——究竟所为何来？其实，在艾丽丝告知我年轻的伯顿原型正是“男孩（Boy）”——也就是战前杰拉尔德心目中的英雄——之后，我本该猜出一二端倪的。但我压根未曾将艾丽丝漫不经心脱口而出的“男孩”，与战前无忧岁月中名声大噪的传奇人物“男孩”芬威克（Boy Fenwick）联系在一处。那位故去的“男孩”芬威克，在后文自会占有一席之地。一个举足轻重、深沉无比的位置。

在“费利克斯·伯顿”眼中，一个男人若要活得崇高应当具备何等心智——他满脑子充斥着战前无忧岁月中一度在青年才俊间风靡的那些宏伟抱负——似乎具体化为妄图繁衍出一支类似“威猛太监”的新种族。显而易见，年轻的伯顿心无半点肉体欲念。艾娃·福却并非如此。年轻的伯顿更不愿将大好光阴虚掷在温存风雅的谈情说爱之上；他渴求的是一种冷酷无情的同伴情谊，间或穿插着零星的交配媾和；他*绝不*愿温文尔雅地繁衍后代，而是带着一种近乎狂暴的漫不经心。且设想一下艾娃——便是艾丽丝！且设想一下杰拉尔德亲笔描摹出自己梦魇中的女子，那个孤寂春梦中柔媚驯服、触手可及的女人，痛恨她摧毁了自家……更痛恨她摧毁了“男孩”！掩卷之余，世人不由得揣摩杰拉尔德生平究竟是否曾真正涉足过女人的温存。那位纯洁的幽暗骑士……那位纯洁的堕落骑士，可那是何等惨烈的堕落啊！

### III / 第三节
===Original===

I did not see Gerald whilst I was shaking the dust of Shepherd’s Market forever off my feet, for he was still asleep. I left Shepherd’s Market. The hearty-looking man and the thin wizened man who said “Oi!” and the little bent old man with the bloodshot eyes gave me farewell.

That afternoon I snatched a few moments from the arranging of the new place, which was only round the corner, to go round and tell Gerald that his sister had been to see him on an Impulse. I had grown to feel responsible for Gerald: his solitude was somehow like a scar across one’s own life, a rebuke.

I came upon him in our lane. I have forgotten to say that Gerald, after a particularly hard spell of dipsomania, would go riding on a hack from the Mews nearby. He had a pair of fine polo breeches with which to do that, and with the fine polo breeches (Moss Bros.) went Barty March’s riding-whip and the jacket of the old grey suit and that hat. A highwayman on an off day, that is what he looked like in the mean lane, passing the time of day with the little bent old man with the bloodshot eyes.

“You’ve been drinking,” said Gerald severely to me.

“Billy Goat’s won the two-thirty,” wheezed the little bent old man. His hat was the captain of Gerald’s hat.

One didn’t, perhaps, look one’s best in the middle of a removal. But Gerald, confound the man, looked positively healthy, taut, tempered, weathered. *Ach, le sale type anglais!* I told him that his sister had called. “On an Impulse,” I said.

Gerald stared at me, his cigarette halfway to his mouth. “Oh!” he said. “Oh!﻿ ﻿…”

“Here’s her telephone number,” I said. He didn’t take the slip of paper I held out.

“ ’Ere,” said the little bent old man, “I’ll give it ’im when he’s better.” Gerald lowered his cigarette, scowling at me pathetically. No one else would have known it was “pathetically.”

“Iris called hell!” he accused me. “How you lie! What?”

“Honest to God, Gerald!”

He flipped away his cigarette and dug his free hand into his pocket as though it was a weapon. Those deep eyes scowled at me, but I wondered what they saw.

“That beast,” he whispered, “oh, that beast.﻿ ﻿…”

I left him.

===Chinese===

当我永远抖落牧羊人集市的尘埃踏上归途时，并未见到杰拉尔德的身影，皆因他彼时尚在沉睡。我离开了牧羊人集市。那位面色红润的壮汉、那位喊着“喂！”的瘦削干瘪汉子，以及那位双眼布满血丝、身躯佝偻的小老头，纷纷向我道别。

那天下午，我从拐角处新寓所的布置杂务中抽出片刻工夫，折返回去告知杰拉尔德，他的胞妹曾凭着一时的冲动登门寻访过他。我心头对杰拉尔德不知不觉生出了一种责任感：他的孤僻离群不知怎的宛如横亘在旁人生命中的一道疤痕，无声地苛责着世人。

我在我们那条小巷里迎面撞见了他。我先前忘却交代了，杰拉尔德每逢经历一场尤为严重的酗酒暴饮发作之后，总爱从附近的马房租一匹马骑着溜达。为此他特备了一条上好的马球马裤，而配上那条上好马球裤（出自莫斯兄弟洋服店，Moss Bros.）的，则是巴蒂·马奇留下的马鞭、那套旧灰色西装的上衣，以及那顶毡帽。那模样宛如一个休沐放假的绿林强盗，走在狭窄破败的小巷里，正同那位双眼充血的佝偻小老头闲聊寒暄。

“你喝过酒了，”杰拉尔德神色严厉地冲我说道。

“‘山羊’（Billy Goat）赢了两点半场（2点30分场），”佝偻小老头喘着粗气嘟囔道。他头上戴着的那顶破毡帽，简直称得上是杰拉尔德那顶帽子的统帅。

在忙乱的搬家当口，一个人的尊容或许难免略显狼狈。可杰拉尔德——真拿这家伙没辙——瞧上去竟出奇地健朗、精悍、硬朗而饱经风霜。哎，这该死的英国种（*Ach, le sale type anglais!*）！我告诉他他的胞妹曾来过。“凭着一时冲动，”我说道。

杰拉尔德定定瞪着我，手里的香烟刚送到嘴边一半。“哦！”他说道，“哦！……”

“这是她的电话号码，”我说道。他并未伸手去接我递出的纸条。

“拿来吧，”佝偻小老头说道，“等他好些了我替他收着。”杰拉尔德放下了香烟，带着一副可怜巴巴的愁容怒视着我。除了我，断不会有旁人看出那神情竟是“可怜巴巴”。

“艾丽丝来找我，简直是见鬼！”他当面指责我道，“你这大话编得可真离谱！什么名堂？”

“对天发誓，杰拉尔德，千真万确！”

他随手弹飞了香烟，将空出的那只手死死插进裤袋，仿佛握着一柄凶器。那一双深陷的眼眸狠狠瞪着我，但我不知那眸子里究竟看见了什么。

“那个畜生，”他低语道，“哦，那个畜生……”

我转身离去。

===Original===

And I did not see him again until the twelfth evening later. I wish I had. I ought to have been to see him, for I was in the habit of seeing Gerald, and during those twelve days he might, I think he would, have told me about the silly, shoddy thing that had happened, and I could have helped to make him see it as only a silly, shoddy thing. What made me feel responsible for Gerald was that his livid, unreasonable, childish contempt for all accepted things was not contempt at all, but fear, just plain fear. He was, I mean, so afraid of life that he simply couldn’t exist but by pretending to despise it. Piercing that tortured vanity, I felt that life was a huge hungry beast ready to maul Gerald if he so much as tried to placate it﻿—by using, say, a little pumice-stone on his fingers. And one could never, after having seen through his furious *blague*, be rid of an acute sense of the shamefaced childishness in the man, a childishness beaten down, gone crooked, which could only do him a hurt if it was not watched. And one didn’t, quite definitely didn’t, want Gerald to be hurt more than he already hurt himself by just breathing.

But, whether it was because that involuntary whisper of his about his sister had sickened me even more than I had thought at the moment, or whether it was merely because I was too busy with arranging myself into the new place, I simply did not seem to have the time to look him up during those twelve days. I wish I had.

===Chinese===

在此之后，直到十二天后的傍晚（12天后的傍晚），我才再次见到他。我真希望自己早些去见他。我本该去看看他的，皆因我素有探望杰拉尔德的习惯；在这十二天里（12天里），他很可能——依我之见，他必定会——向我吐露那桩荒唐下作的变故，而我也本可以帮他看清那不过是一桩荒唐下作的琐事罢了。教我对杰拉尔德深感放心不下的，是他对世俗公认的一切物事所抱有的那种铁青、蛮横、孩子气的轻蔑，其实根本不是什么轻蔑，而是恐惧，彻头彻尾的赤裸恐惧。我的意思是，他是如此畏惧生活本身，以至于唯有借着假装鄙夷生活方能苟延残喘。洞穿了他那层饱受折磨的虚荣心，我只觉得生活宛如一头饥肠辘辘的庞大巨兽，倘若杰拉尔德稍有半分试图讨好它的举动——比方说，用少许浮石磨去手指上的污垢——那巨兽便会立刻扑上来将他撕得粉碎。而且，在看穿了他那些狂怒的虚张声势（*blague*）之后，人们心中总会挥之不去地感受到这汉子身上那种羞赧难当的稚气——一种惨遭践踏压抑、已然扭曲畸形的孩子气；倘若无人看顾，这种孩子气只会教他自讨苦吃。而旁人是当真、确凿不愿看到杰拉尔德受到更多的伤害了——皆因他光是活在世上呼吸吐纳，便已然伤痕累累。

然而，究竟是因为他那句情不自禁针对胞妹的低语让我感到恶心，其恶心程度远超我当时所料，抑或仅仅是因为我忙于打点新寓所而分身乏术，在这十二天里，我竟当真抽不出工夫前去探望他。我真希望自己当初去过了。

===Original===

Nor, during those twelve days, would it have come very amiss to talk a little about Mrs. Storm. One would have liked to know just a little of the history of that shameless, shameful lady. After all, one didn’t every day meet a woman with a pagan body and a Chislehurst mind. But naturally neither Guy nor Hilary was available during those twelve days, for that is a way friends have; Guy because he was down at Mace with the Mayfly, and Hilary just because he was tiresome. Hilary, Guy wrote from Mace, was helping a Liberal to fight a musty bye-election in some Staffordshire place. “As if,” Guy wrote, “a Liberal ever won, as if a Liberal could ever win without a pretty long start! and a handicapper can never get a grip on anything in a Liberal to give him a start on﻿—sticky little fellows they are, always sliding away somewhere. And as if it mattered whether a Liberal did or didn’t win. He’ll only get squashed with his own petard.” And, however it was, Hilary’s Liberal didn’t win, so maybe Guy was right. “In ten years’ time,” says Guy, “Hilary will be the only Liberal left in Parliament, looking happier and younger and more sickening than ever.”

It was on the fifth morning after the coming and going of the green hat that I was on an instant afflicted with an impulse, and did on the same instant act upon it.

“Hello!” I said.

“Hello!” they said. They were a she.

“Could I speak to Mrs. Storm, please?”

“Who is that speaking, please?”

I quibbled quite in vain.

“I will put the name down in her little book,” said the she kindly.

“Thank you,” I tried not to say bitterly. To ring someone up on impulse and then have your impulse perpetuated in a Little Book!

“Mrs. Storm is not in town,” said the she.

“Oh, I see,” I said. It is a detestable habit some people have of saying “in town” or “out of town.” What town? There can’t, honestly, be any real harm in saying London.﻿ ﻿…

“Is there any message? I always take her messages.”

“Oh, no,” I said. “Thank you very much. Goodb﻿—”

“This is Mrs. Oden speaking.”

“Oh,” I said. “Mrs. Oden?”

“Yes?” said Mrs. Oden.

“Well, thank you very much,” I said. “Goodb﻿—”

===Chinese===

而且，在这十二天里（12天里），若是能私下谈论一番斯托姆夫人（Mrs. Storm），倒也不失为一桩趣事。世人总希望能多打听一点关于那位既不知廉耻又深陷耻辱的贵妇人的生平往事。毕竟，一个人并非每天都能遇上一位兼具异教徒躯壳与奇斯尔赫斯特（Chislehurst）心智的女子。然而在这十二天里，盖伊与希拉里自然一个也联系不上——朋友之间向来如此；盖伊去了梅斯庄园（Mace）忙着钓蜉蝣鳟鱼，而希拉里则纯粹是因其性情惹人厌烦。盖伊在梅斯来信中写道，希拉里正忙着在斯塔福德郡（Staffordshire）的某个偏僻角落协助一位自由党人打一场沉闷陈腐的补选。“仿佛，”盖伊写道，“自由党人真能赢似的，仿佛自由党人若是没有天大的起步让分便能赢似的！更何况评磅员在自由党人身上压根抓不住半点可以给予让步的凭据——他们尽是一帮黏糊糊的小滑头，总爱往不知什么阴暗角落里滑溜钻营。再者，自由党人赢与不赢又有何相干？他横竖不过是被自家埋下的炸药轰个粉碎罢了。”无论原由究竟如何，希拉里支持的那位自由党人终究败选了，足见盖伊所言非虚。“十年之内（10年之内），”盖伊调侃道，“希拉里将成为议会（Parliament）里残存的唯一一位自由党人，瞧上去比以往任何时候都更加春风得意、年轻少壮，也更令人作呕。”

正是在绿帽子夫人去留之后的第五个早晨（第5个早晨），我心头冷不丁泛起一阵难以遏制的冲动，并当机立断付诸了行动。

“喂！”我说道。

“喂！”那头应道。声音是个妇人。

“请问我可以同斯托姆夫人通电话么？”

“请问哪位找她？”

我百般遮掩闪烁其词，终归是徒劳无功。

“我会把您的尊姓大名记在她的小备忘本上，”那位妇人语气亲切地说道。

“多谢，”我竭力克制住嗓音里的酸苦。凭着一时冲动拨通电话，结果自家的冲动竟被白纸黑字永久镌刻在了一本小备忘录里！

“斯托姆夫人眼下不在城里，”那位妇人说道。

“哦，我明白了，”我说道。有些人总爱把“在城里”或“出城了”挂在嘴边，委实是种惹人厌恶的恶习。究竟是哪座城？老老实实说伦敦（London）难道真会少块肉不成？……

“有什么口信需要转达么？她的口信向来由我代收。”

“哦，没有，”我说道。“非常感谢您。再——”

“我是奥登夫人（Mrs. Oden）。”

“哦，”我应道，“奥登夫人？”

“您有什么事吗？”奥登夫人问道。

“哎，十分感谢您，”我说道。“再——”

===Original===

“She never is, you know,” complained Mrs. Oden. Now that was a loquacious lady. I do not wish to be belittling anyone else, but I am sure that she talked more in the next few minutes than any other person of the same chest-expansion in England. She seemed to have been suffering from silence all that morning until my ring. I learnt later that Mrs. Oden had once been Iris’s governess, that there was always a floor reserved for Iris in her house in Montpellier Square, which house Mr. and Mrs. Oden owed entirely to Iris’s generosity.

“She went off to Paris the other day,” complained Mrs. Oden, “at a *moment’s* notice. Here today and gone tomorrow. It is too bad of her, when we never see anything of her. She is too vague, I always tell her. I suppose she had made some arrangement with you, Mr. er, has forgotten to put you off, and now you are disappointed?”

“Oh,” I said. “Yes, certainly.”

“Well, I expect her back any day, but how long she will stay this time I have not the faintest idea. Really it is too bad, she gets vaguer every year. And here has her aunt Lady Eve Chalice been wanting her address in Paris, and I have not the faintest idea of anything! What did you say the name was? Oh, yes, of course, I have it down. She will see it as *soon* as she returns, I promise you. Yes, yes. Goodbye, goodbye.”

It was five days later that there came to my hand a large box labelled from “Edouard Apel et Cie., rue de la Paix, Paris” and stamped “By Air.” Within the large box were several smaller flat boxes, and within these were reams upon reams of finest white notepaper, but good, manly stuff, stamped with my new address; and if that notepaper had its way I never would have another address, for there was enough in those small flat boxes to last a reasonably reticent man for all time. No note came with them. I searched. Then, across the top sheet of the third box that I opened, I found scrawled in pencil in an absurd, schoolgirl hand: “That one day you may write to me to say that you have forgiven me for the only dignity I have left: the dignity of the.﻿ ﻿…”

I could not make out that last word for several days. It was scrawled right across the foot of the sheet, a long squiggle with one eye looking out from the middle of it which might have been an a. At last I thought it was “unaware.”

===Chinese===

“您也知道，她向来不大着家，”奥登夫人抱怨道。这委实是一位喋喋不休的多话贵妇。我绝无贬低旁人的意思，但我敢断定，她在接下来的几分钟里所吐露的字句，比全英格兰任何一位拥有同等胸围的妇人家都要多得多。在我的电话铃声响起之前，她整整一个上午似乎都饱受孤寂缄默的折磨。我后来方才知晓，奥登夫人曾担任过艾丽丝的家庭女教师，她在蒙彼利埃广场（Montpellier Square）的宅第里始终为艾丽丝保留着整整一层楼面，而奥登夫妇之所以能坐拥这栋宅第，全仰仗艾丽丝当年的慷慨相赠。

“前几天她动身去了巴黎（Paris），”奥登夫人抱怨道，“说走*拔腿*就走。今儿个还在这儿，明儿个便没了影踪。这委实太不像话了，我们平日里连她的影子都见不着。我总对她说，她做事未免太飘忽不定了。我估摸着她大概同您事先定下了什么约定，嗯，先生，随后却又忘却了通知您取消，结果眼下让您白跑了一趟吧？”

“哦，”我说道，“正是，确实如此。”

“咳，我料想她这几天随时都可能回来，可这一回她究竟能住多久，我可当真一无所知。委实太不成体统了，她一年比一年更飘忽。眼下连她的姨母伊芙·查利斯贵夫人（Lady Eve Chalice）都急着打听她在巴黎的住址，可我当真两眼一抹黑什么都不知道！您刚才说您尊姓大名怎么称呼来着？哦，对，对，我已然记下来了。我向您保证，她一回来*立刻*便能瞧见。好啦，好啦。再见，再见。”

五天之后（5天之后），我收到了一只沉甸甸的大包裹，上面贴着“巴黎和平街埃杜阿尔·阿佩尔公司”（Edouard Apel et Cie., rue de la Paix, Paris）的标签，盖有“航空件（By Air）”的戳记。大包裹内装着好几只扁平的小盒子，盒内则是一叠又一叠上等的雪白信纸——质地硬朗扎实、极具男儿气度，且均印有我的新寓所地址；倘若依着那些信纸的用意，我这辈子大抵再也无需更换地址了，皆因那些扁平小盒里的信纸存量，足够一个生性谨慎内敛的汉子受用终生。里面并未附带任何字条。我四下翻找。随后，在我拆开的第三只盒子（第3只盒子）最上层的一张信纸上，我发现用铅笔以一种荒诞稚嫩的学堂女童笔迹潦草地写着：“但愿有朝一日，你能致信于我，道出你已然宽恕了我仅存的唯一尊严：那份……”

有那么好几天，我都未能辨认出那最后一个字究竟为何。那字迹横贯纸页底部，是一道长长的波浪蜷曲线条，正中间探出一只宛如小圆圈的眼睛，瞧上去依稀像个字母a。最后我认定那应当是“浑然未觉（unaware）”。

===Original===

Much later Iris told me that it was “unaware.” She said: “I picked out the phrase from a book I was reading, and sent it to you like a flower.”

===Chinese===

很久以后，艾丽丝告诉我那确凿是“浑然未觉（unaware）”。她说道：“那是我从手头正在翻阅的一本书里摘抄下的一句话，宛如一朵鲜花般寄赠于你。”

## III / 第三章 为了纯洁！
===Original===

For Purity!

===Chinese===

为了纯洁！

### I / 第一节

### I /

### I /

### I / 
===Original===

The cavalier of low creatures dies hard; surviving even our gesture, he loiters dangerously in the tail of our eye, he awaits, with piratical calm, the final stroke; and only will he fade and be forever gone, despised, and distraught, before the face of him who bore the magic device “for purity,” whose ghost was to be raised by Mr. Townshend over dinner on the twelfth night after the coming of the green hat. For, his wretched Liberal being at last retrieved from somewhere beneath the foot of the poll, that gentleman was again among us, saying “hm.”

We have so far seen but the shadow of Mr. Townshend; now, at last, this shadow must emerge into the tale of the weak Marches as the person of Mr. Townshend of Magralt. He emerges, as becomes a man of property who believes in progress as though it were a pain, in a dinner-jacket, *le smoking*, a Tuxedo; of which the bow-tie is gathered together with that dexterous carelessness which is the affectation of elderly Englishmen who cannot put up with any affectations whatever. Now there is no known explanation for this phenomenon of the sickly bow-tie among Englishmen of over forty years of age. That they are all blackguards, Mr. Shaw has assured us. But haven’t they, God bless one’s soul, eyes! It is not, of course, of the least importance whether a bow-tie falls straight or crooked, particularly on a grown-up man. It is not, after all, of the least importance whether one is clothed or naked. But one may, in passing, be permitted to wonder on the curious dispositions of the blind goddess Chance, whereby not once in a long lifetime, not even by one little bit of a fluke, will one of these elderly gentlemen ever tie a bow to fall even approximately right. They must, therefore, do it on purpose. But for what purpose? Let them, I say unto them, tie their bows carefully while the bow-tying is good, for voices from the Clyde are rising loud and everywhere those snobs are dominant who affect that the shirt of democracy should be a dishclout.

===Chinese===

卑微生灵的骑士绝非轻易便会咽气；他甚至在我们那番姿态修饰之下死里逃生，危险地在我们余光所及之处徘徊流连，以亡命之徒般的从容静候着最后一击；唯有在当年身负“为了纯洁”这面魔幻铭牌之人的英魂面前，他方会彻底黯然退场、永远消逝，为人所鄙弃，为人所神伤——而那道英魂，偏偏正要在绿帽子夫人降临后的第十二天夜晚（第12天夜晚），由汤森先生（Mr. Townshend）在晚宴席间唤醒招引。皆因他那位可怜巴巴的自由党候选人终于自得票榜的最底端被人捞了出来，那位绅士也得以重返我们之间，口中依然“哼”个不停。

迄今为止，我们所见到的不过是汤森先生的孤影；而眼下，这道影子终于作为马格拉尔特的汤森先生（Mr. Townshend of Magralt）本尊，赫然步入了衰亡脆弱的马奇家族的故事之中。他适时现身，正如一位笃信社会进步正如笃信皮肉之痛般殷实的有产绅士应有的做派那般，身着一件晚礼服无尾半正式西装（*le smoking*，亦即吸烟装大礼服）；衣领前系着的领结带着一种娴熟老练的漫不经心——这正是那些容不得世间半点做作之态的老派英国绅士自家独有的矫揉做作。如今在英格兰，对于年过四十（40岁以上）的英国绅士为何总爱系出歪七扭八的恶心领结这一怪现状，向来找不出合理解释。肖伯纳先生（Mr. Shaw）曾向我们信誓旦旦地保证，说他们无一例外全是一帮无赖混账。可老天爷在上，难道他们自己就没长眼睛么！当然，一个成年男子身上的领结系得正不正、歪不歪，本就无足轻重。终归而言，一个人究竟是衣冠楚楚还是赤身裸体，亦断然无关宏旨。但在闲谈之余，人们难免会为盲目机遇女神的古怪安排而诧异称奇：在漫长的一生之中，哪怕仅凭一时侥幸，这帮年长的绅士们竟从来不曾有哪怕一次将领结系得大体端正。由此可见，他们定然是故意为之。可究竟是图个什么名堂呢？我且正告他们，趁着领结尚能打理体面的大好光景，好生将自家的领结系齐整吧；皆因克莱德河畔（the Clyde）工人的呐喊声正日益高亢，而笃信“民主的衬衫理应同洗碗抹布一般粗陋”的庸俗之徒更早已在四处大行其道。

===Original===

However, Mr. Townshend’s shadow does not even yet grow in substance without some difficulty. Between him and us, towards the dinner-hour, intrudes, knife-like, that deuce of cavaliers, he of the hat that Frederick the Great would have envied, for that wrecker of homes liked his hats soft and malleable, he liked to twist and torture them as though they were no more than men. In fine, Gerald made me late for dinner.

The clock of the Queen Street Post Office stood at three minutes before eight o’clock as I passed on my way to Hilary’s house in Chesterfield Street. The roar of the marching hosts of Piccadilly was as though muted by the still evening air. The small straight streets of Mayfair lay as though musing between the setting of the sun and the rising of the theatre-curtain. Neat errand-boys, released for the day, kicked their heels about on the curbs. The drivers of the sauntering taxicabs looked inquiringly, impersonally, into the faces of hurrying pedestrians. Limousines lounged softly by. Past me strode intently a tall raven-haired woman in a bright green wrap with a high sable collar, and moving frantically below were bright green shoes and bright green stockings that appalled the suave dignity of the evening light. These are not the only green properties we shall see in this tale, for women of the mode wore very much of green in the year 1922; although, of course, some women were not necessarily of the mode even when they wore green. Some women should not wear green. To such, their husbands should say: “My dear, I can’t help saying it again, but really I’ve never seen you look as well as when you’re in black.”

It was from the Curzon Street corner, just by Jolley’s the chemist, that I saw Gerald. He was across the road, against the entrance of the little tunnel that leads into Shepherd’s Market, buying an evening-paper off a friend of ours, Mr. Auk, who used to have his stand just there.

I crossed towards Gerald. I would be a few minutes late for dinner, that was certain, but if ever I was punctual at Hilary’s he never was dressed: a sense of conduct being the property of imperious men, who must disregard the servile virtue of punctuality.

===Chinese===

然而，汤森先生的虚影若要蜕化为有血有肉的实体，眼下却仍旧少不了一番波折。正当晚宴时分将至之际，横亘在我们与他之间的，宛如一柄尖刀般冷不丁插进来的，偏偏是那位魔王般的骑士——那位头顶所戴软帽连腓特烈大帝（Frederick the Great）都会为之艳羡的汉子；皆因那位摧家破舍的暴烈君王最爱将自家的帽子揉弄得柔软服帖，最爱随心所欲地扭曲折磨帽子，正如折磨麾下的臣民一般。总而言之，正是杰拉尔德害得我赴宴迟到了。

当我取道前往希拉里位于切斯特菲尔德街（Chesterfield Street）的宅第时，路过皇后街邮局（Queen Street Post Office），钟楼上的指针正指向差三分八点（7点57分）。皮卡迪利大街上川流不息的人马喧嚣，仿佛尽皆被凝滞的晚风柔化了声息。梅费尔区（Mayfair）狭窄笔直的街巷，宛如在日落与剧院大幕拉开之间静默沉思。下班得以解脱的利落跑街小厮们，正在路沿上百无聊赖地踢着鞋跟。闲逛游弋的出租车司机们，神情冷淡而探询地打量着匆匆过路的行人的面孔。一辆辆豪华轿车悄无声息地滑行而过。一名身材高挑、乌发如云的女子神色专注地从我身旁大步走过，身裹一件配有紫貂高领的鲜绿色披肩，而在披肩下方急促移动着的，则是鲜绿色的鞋子与鲜绿色的丝袜，刺目得令这暮色宁谧的尊贵光景为之侧目。在本书之中，这绝非我们所能见到的唯一一抹绿色长物，皆因在1922年，讲究时髦的贵妇们普遍对绿色情有独钟；尽管诚然，有些女人即便穿戴一身翠绿，也断然谈不上什么时髦。有些女人压根就不配穿绿色。对待此类女子，她们的夫君理当正告道：“亲爱的，我虽不愿多嘴，但我生平委实从未见你穿过比黑衣更合身的衣裳了。”

正是在寇松街（Curzon Street）拐角处乔利药房（Jolley’s）门旁，我望见了杰拉尔德的身影。他正站在马路对面，背靠通往牧羊人集市的狭窄小地道入口，从我们的一位熟人——常年在该处摆摊的奥克先生（Mr. Auk）手里买晚报。

我穿过马路朝杰拉尔德走去。晚宴迟到几分钟已成定局，但倘若我哪回当真准时抵达希拉里府上，他往往连衣裳都没换好：严苛的操守规矩向来是专断独裁之辈的特权，而他们向来对“守时”这种奴才般的卑微美德不屑一顾。

===Original===

I could not see Gerald’s face as he stood on the curb glancing at his paper, the brim of that hat was so low over his right eye. Mr. Auk winked at me as I came up. “Oiled, that’s wot!” whispered Mr. Auk.
