Not an American was left on Fifth Avenue, nor an Argentine in the Americas; while Australian fruit-farms deplored the absence of their masters, and Canada adored the ton of her peerless millionaires.

We had no sooner entered among the company than Hilary was for going at once: but Risotto having rewarded us with a sofa-table﻿—for he and Hilary had, as the saying is, been boys together when Hilary had been attached to the Embassy in Paris and Risotto was ennobling patrons of the Ritz to *l’aristocracie internationale*﻿—he and I prevailed on Hilary to stay by ordering for him an angel-on-horseback, to which he was notably partial; while I, Risotto said, would have a haddock with a nice egg on it.

===Chinese===

然而，忠诚俱乐部在青史之上本无甚值得大书特书之处，唯独其餐厅总监朱利奥·迪·里索托骑士（Chevalier Giulio di Risotto）此人除外。我们无须在此停下笔墨去艳羡俱乐部究竟开出了何等天价年俸方始费尽九牛二虎之力说服骑士先生离开他在拉帕洛（Rapallo）的隐居庄园；但他委实物有所值，这一点断无人敢于否认，皆因里索托无论走到何方，随身总带着自家的独门绝技。他将这项发明冠名为“国际贵族圈”（*l’aristocracie internationale*）——诸位明白，这乃是他为自家侍奉的这帮显贵所量身定制的名号；而这帮人对此更是趋之若鹜、爱之若狂。

潜心研读哲人与政客的生平固能启迪心智、升华灵魂；然而若能抽空斜睨一眼二十世纪里索托这般奇特的存在，却也不禁能教一个人的头脑变得圆通活络。作为古往今来最卑躬屈膝的奴才之一，他偏偏成了我们这个时代最飞黄腾达的成功典范；且更是英格兰贵族阶层行将就木的鲜明凶兆。他是马基雅维利（Machiavelli）同风尘交际花小姐私通苟合所诞下的孽种：刁钻、精瘦、面色惨白、干瘪油亮得宛如一张鲨鱼皮；他漫步穿梭于欧洲各大顶级奢华餐馆之间聚敛万贯家财，总在暗中审慎甄别、为他的“国际贵族圈”招兵买马；且曾在他那位才智逊色却为人更为谨慎的同行——戛纳与多维尔的科尔尼谢先生（M. Cornuché）——百家乐赌台前豪赌至破产过两次（2次），展现出了真正的贵胄派头。“创立”忠诚俱乐部，在未来的传记作家笔下定然被尊奉为里索托职业生涯的至高巅峰。他的“国际贵族圈”终于得到了最彻底的妥帖侍奉：纽约第五大道（Fifth Avenue）上再无半个美国豪客滞留，美洲大陆亦找不见任何阿根廷富贾；澳大利亚的水果庄园痛惜庄园主的久客不归，加拿大则顶礼膜拜自家盖世百万富翁的高贵做派。

我们刚一步入大厅，希拉里拔腿便想开溜；但多亏里索托特意赏光为我们安排了一张靠墙的沙发长桌——皆因正如俗语所言，早在希拉里供职于巴黎使馆、而里索托尚在丽兹酒店（Ritz）将各路宾客加官晋爵擢升进“国际贵族圈”的年月里，他们二人便早已称得上是少年旧雨——于是里索托同我一道，借着替希拉里点了一份他平素最偏爱的“马上天使”（培根卷生蚝，angel-on-horseback），方才成功说服他安坐下来；至于我嘛，里索托做主说道，不妨来上一份盖有一枚鲜嫩荷包蛋的熏黑线鳕鱼。

===Original===

Hilary, like all middle-aged men who detest nightclubs, at once left me to dance with the first acquaintance he saw. This was Mrs. Ammon. Whereas I, in not dancing, was following an example set by many present. We, we watched our elders dance with each other’s mothers, and for them the band on the balcony played with a sensibility approaching grief. There was no tune. But it is absurd, this querulous demand of young people for “tunes”! Our fathers and our mothers have done with “tunes.” Let there, our uncles say, be a rhythm. Let there, say our aunts, be syncopation. There was a rhythm. There was syncopation. Grave, profound, unforgettable, there was a rhythm. It had a beat like the throbbing of an agonised heart lost in an artery of the Underground. Dolorous it was, yet phantasm of gaiety lay twined in it. They call this rhythm the Blues. It reminded you of past and passing things. It reminded you of the days when, people over forty had still enough restraint not to crowd out every ballroom and nightclub with their dancing in open formation, playfully aiming at each other’s tonsils with their feet. It reminded you of the scent tangled in the hair of she with whom you had last danced to that rhythm. You saw the soft line of her face by your shoulder, the tender pocket behind her ear, the absorbed excursion of her breath through her nostrils, the dark eyebrow over which you would lightly pass the third finger of your left hand but that it would soil the tip of it. You mourned the presence of the dead. You mourned the memory of the living. They call this rhythm the Blues. It reminded you of regret. It reminded you of a small white face suddenly thrown back against your arm with a smile that disturbed the dance. It reminded you of the desire that pleasantly turns to dust when you are desired. It reminded you of things you had never done with women you had never met. You danced again at the Ambassadeurs at Cannes, with the masts of yachts drawn ebony-black between the tall windows and the pale blue night over the sea. The Lido lay like a temptation before your mind, and the songs of the gondoliers raved into the measure of whispering feet.

===Chinese===

正如所有深恶痛绝夜总会的中年男子那般，希拉里刚一落座便撇下我，同一位他迎面撞见的首位熟人滑入舞池起舞去了。那位熟人乃是安蒙夫人（Mrs. Ammon）。至于我嘛，安坐不舞，不过是在效仿在场诸多同侪的做派罢了。我们，我们安坐着端详长辈们同彼此的高堂生母翩翩起舞，而楼厅露台上的乐队则带着一股近乎哀痛的深情为他们伴奏。乐曲全无调式旋律。然而，当今年轻人动辄苛求“旋律调子”，委实荒谬至极！我们的父辈与母辈早已听腻了所谓“调子”。我们的叔伯们宣称，只要有节拍律动即可。我们的姑婶们嚷道，只要有切分音便足矣。这儿确凿有节拍。这儿确凿有切分音。庄重、深邃、令人难忘，在这儿回荡着一种节拍。它的跳动宛如一颗迷失在地下铁道动脉深处的痛苦心脏在剧烈搏动。它是那般凄楚悲切，却又在内里交织着轻佻欢愉的幻象。世人将这种节奏律动称作“布鲁斯蓝调”（the Blues）。它教你追忆起往昔与正在逝去的诸般事物。它教你追忆起那个年过四十（40岁以上）的人尚有起码的克制教养、绝不至于在各大舞厅与夜总会里排成散兵队形、嬉皮笑脸用脚尖直冲着旁人的扁桃体胡乱飞踢的遥远岁月。它教你追忆起上一回伴着这等节拍起舞时缠绕在伊人发丝间的那缕馨香。你望见贴在自己肩头的柔和面庞轮廓，望见她耳后的娇嫩凹陷，望见她鼻翼间全神贯注流转的细微吐息，望见那道若非生怕弄脏了左手无名指尖便真想用指腹轻轻抚摸而过的深色黛眉。你为逝者的音容而哀悼。你为生者的记忆而叹惋。世人将这种节奏称作布鲁斯蓝调。它教你追忆起悔恨。它教你追忆起一张小巧白皙的面庞冷不丁倚靠在你的臂弯里、嘴角泛起的一抹惊扰了舞步的微笑。它教你追忆起当你被人渴慕时欲念却惬意化作微尘的虚无。它教你追忆起你从未曾同从未谋面的女人们行过的诸般风流事。你仿佛再次在戛纳的大使俱乐部（Ambassadeurs at Cannes）起舞，高耸的雕花窗扉与海面上幽蓝的夜色之间勾勒出游艇漆黑如乌木般的桅杆。利多岛（Lido）宛如一道诱惑横陈在你的脑海深处，而贡多拉船夫的悠扬唱腔则狂乱地消融在窃窃私语般的舞步节拍之中。

===Original===

The Spanish King brushed by you at San Sebastian, eating salted almonds, again you hesitated in the dance at Biarritz to listen to the roar of the Atlantic, and across a perfumed street in Seville you again saw the shiver of a mantilla through the cracked window of a cabaret. You danced again beneath the vermilion moon of Algeria, between the American Bar and the pyramid-cypress tree. You danced again in the Bois in Paris, the trees like monstrous black pagodas against the night, the stars brilliant as sequins on an archangel’s floating cloak, the magically white faces of women, the lights in the night making love to the black shadows in their hair, their lips red as lobsters, their armpits clean as ivory, the men talking with facile gestures, the whole tapestry of the Château de Madrid like a painted fan against a summer night. They call this rhythm the Blues, which is short for a low state of vitality brought about by the action of life on the liver. O Baby, it’s *divine*!

That is what they say, our elders.

Astorias, *chef d’orchestre*, stood at rest by the edge of the balcony, his violin under his arm, his bow gently tapping the edge of a bowl of nameless ferns that hid his feet. His negligence is informed with depression, his poise leans on melancholy. The Blues, that man knows. He seems to wonder why he is there, why anyone is there, why everyone is there. No one can tell him, so he goes on doing nothing, lonely as a star in hell. He does not toil, nor spin, nor play his violin. From the crowded floor a woman, her face powdered brown, her mouth scarlet as the inside of a pomegranate in a tale by Oscar Wilde, beseeches him with an arm black-gloved to the shoulder to continue to play. He yields.

===Chinese===

西班牙国王（Spanish King）嚼着盐焗杏仁在圣塞巴斯蒂安（San Sebastian）同你擦肩而过；你再次在比亚里茨（Biarritz）的舞步中迟疑驻足，静听大西洋的狂暴怒涛；而在塞维利亚（Seville）香气袭人的街巷对过，你隔着歌舞厅破裂的窗扉再次望见一顶轻纱头巾的曼妙颤动。你再次在阿尔及利亚（Algeria）朱红色的朗月之下、在美式酒吧与金字塔丝柏之间翩翩起舞。你再次在巴黎的布洛涅森林（Bois in Paris）起舞，参天古木衬着夜幕宛如一座座狰狞漆黑的宝塔，繁星璀璨宛如大天使飘拂斗篷上的金色亮片，女人们的面庞白皙得宛如施了魔法，夜色中的华灯同她们发丝间的黑影温存调情，她们的樱唇赤红如龙虾，腋下光洁如象牙，男人们带着轻佻潇洒的手势高谈阔论，整座马德里城堡酒楼（Château de Madrid）的绚烂织锦衬着夏夜宛如一柄绘满彩绘的折扇。世人将这种节奏称作布鲁斯蓝调，乃是凡尘生活戕害肝脏所致的元气衰竭之简称。哦，宝贝儿，这简直*太神圣美妙*啦！

我们的长辈们正是这般啧啧赞叹的。

乐队首席阿斯托里亚斯（Astorias, chef d’orchestre）正闲散地伫立在楼厅露台的边缘，小提琴夹在臂弯底下，琴弓轻轻敲打着遮掩住他双脚的一盆无名蕨类植物的边缘。他的慵懒中渗透着抑郁，他的身姿倚靠着伤感。对于布鲁斯蓝调，那汉子体味得彻彻底底。他似乎在纳闷自家究竟为何会身在此处，旁人究竟为何会身在此处，所有人究竟为何会身在此处。无人能解答他的困惑，于是他便继续无所事事，孤寂得宛如悬挂在地狱深渊里的一颗孤星。他既不纺纱，亦不织布，更不肯拉动琴弦。自拥挤不堪的舞池之中，一名面庞敷着褐色脂粉的贵妇——她的红唇猩红得宛如奥斯卡·王尔德（Oscar Wilde）童话里的石榴籽一般——抬起一截戴着及肩黑手套的手臂，苦苦央求他继续拉琴。他终于妥协了。

===Original===

Nearby was a corner-table of eight young people. Maybe they would dance later on. Suddenly one of the girls would give a loud laugh, and then there would be silence. Of the four young men one looked as Richard of Gloucester might possibly have looked, a little bent, a little sinister, and pale, as though he had been reading a treatise on diseases far into the night before. They were four married couples, and they had all been boys and girls together, and they had a son and daughter apiece, and they all went to the same dentist. The women had white oval faces, small breasts, blue eyes, thin arms, no expression, no blood: literally, of course, not genealogically. One of them stared with wide blue eyes right into people’s faces, and blinked vaguely. She was lovely. These eight young people were very happy. They ignored everything but themselves, in whom they were not very interested. Presently a prince of the blood joined them, there was a little stir for a minute or two, a little laughter, and then he rose to dance with the girl of the blind blue eyes. As she danced she stared thoughtfully at the glass dome of the ceiling. She looked bored with boredom.

===Chinese===

在紧邻的角落餐桌旁，围坐着八名年轻人（8名年轻人）。或许待会儿他们也会下场跳舞。冷不丁，其中一位姑娘会放声大笑起来，随即周遭又陷入一片死寂。在四名年轻男子（4名年轻男子）之中，有一位瞧上去颇有几分格洛斯特公爵理查（Richard of Gloucester）的模样：略显佝偻，神情阴鸷，面色苍白，仿佛前一夜秉烛夜读医学病理专著直到天明一般。他们乃是四对年轻夫妇（4对夫妇），自幼青梅竹马一道长大，各自膝下育有一儿一女（各生1儿1女），且平素光顾的都是同一位牙医。那些女人们皆生着白皙的鹅蛋脸、小巧的胸脯、蔚蓝的双眸、纤细的手臂，毫无表情，毫无血色——显而易见，这纯粹是指气色而言，而非指门第血统。其中一位姑娘睁着滚圆的蓝眸直勾勾注视着众人的脸庞，茫然地眨巴着眼睛。她娇艳绝伦。这八位年轻人快活极了。他们除了自家之外对周遭一切皆视若无睹，而他们对自家其实也谈不上有多大兴致。不多时，一位王室宗亲加入了他们，席间起了一阵一两分钟（1、2分钟）的细微骚动，泛起零星笑声，随后王爷便起身同那位长着一双空洞蓝眸的姑娘下场跳舞去了。起舞之际，她若有所思地定定凝视着天花板上的玻璃穹顶。她瞧上去对“无聊”这件事本身都已感到厌烦透顶。

===Original===

There were many green dresses: jade-green, October green, rusty green, soft green, sea-green, dying green, any shade of green that would suit the expiring voices of formal women in a garden by Watteau. There were thirty-nine green dresses. There was a Jewess of the wrong sort in the wrong sort of green. She looked like a fat asparagus whose head had been dipped in dressing and then put in a warm place to dry. She dried in patches. A caravan of pearls crawled upwards from her bosom to her throat, and she said to Mr. Trehawke Tush, the novelist: “The only decent cocktails you can get in Paris are at the Ritz Bar, but the people are so odd. My Archie wants to stand for Parliament. What do you think?” Mr. Trehawke Tush, portraits of whose prewar face must be familiar to everyone, was the most successful of the younger novelists, and had earned from Miss Rebecca West the praise that he was “the leader of the spats school of thought.” Mr. Trehawke Tush will go down to history as the originator of Pique as a profitable literary idea. He had hit on the discovery that English library subscribers will wholeheartedly bear with any racy and illegal relation between the sexes if the same is caused by Pique. He had observed that the whole purpose of a “bestseller” is to justify a reasonable amount of adultery in the eyes of suburban matrons. He had observed that in no current English novel was there ever a mention of any woman having a lover because she wanted a lover: she always took a lover because something had upset her, as in real life she might take an aspirin. Mr. Trehawke Tush had then created Pique, and was spoken of as a “brilliant feminine psychologist.” Since the rise of Mr. Trehawke Tush no reviewer will take any count of a writer as a “brilliant feminine psychologist” unless he can explain the regrettable adultery of his leading female character by the word Pique. This will also persuade *Punch* reviewers to consider the tale wholesome. Mr. Trehawke Tush was up to all those dodges. He said: “I have just finished a serial for *The Daily Sale*. I want to show up this kind of thing, the waste, the Indecency of it. All these girls. I thought the editor might take objection to certain passages, as there is some strong bedroom stuff in it, but he only asked me to change one thing. I had put ‘he kissed her where he would,’ and so I changed it to ‘as he would.’ ”

===Chinese===

在场有许多绿色的衣裙：翡翠绿、深秋绿、锈绿、柔绿、海绿、残枯绿，凡是适合在华托（Watteau）笔下的园囿中那些体面妇人奄奄一息的柔声细语的各种绿调，应有尽有。在场总共有三十九件绿色裙袍（39件绿色衣裙）。其中有一位品位低劣的犹太妇人，身上裹着一件品位极其低劣的绿衣。她瞧上去如同一根肥硕的芦笋，笋尖在调味汁里蘸了一蘸，随后被搁在温暖处风干。可她浑身风干得斑斑驳驳。一串如驼队般的珍珠项链自她的酥胸一路蜿蜒爬向咽喉，她正对着小说家特雷霍克·塔什先生（Mr. Trehawke Tush）说道：“在巴黎（Paris），唯独在丽兹酒吧（Ritz Bar）方能喝到像样的鸡尾酒，可那里的宾客未免太古怪了。我家阿奇（Archie）打算竞选议会议员。您意下如何？”特雷霍克·塔什先生——人人对他在战前的那张尊容画像想必耳熟能详——乃是当今年轻一辈小说家中最春风得意的一位，曾荣获丽贝卡·韦斯特小姐（Miss Rebecca West）的盛赞，被誉为“绑腿短袜思潮流派的领袖”。特雷霍克·塔什先生在青史上注定将作为将“负气赌气（Pique）”转化为赚大钱之文学灵感的鼻祖而名垂青史。他敏锐地察觉到：只要男女之间的一切风流出格与不伦苟合皆是由于“负气赌气”所致，英格兰的租书馆订阅户们便会满心欢喜、心安理得地全盘笑纳。他洞察到，一部“畅销书”的全部旨归，无非是在郊区体面主妇们眼中为适度的通奸行径寻得合情合理的借口。他更留意到，在当今英国的小说里，从不曾有哪怕一处胆敢提及某个女人之所以养情郎纯粹是因为自家渴求情郎：她之所以找情郎，永远是因为某桩琐事惹恼了她，正如在现实生活中她头疼便随手吃下一片阿司匹林一般无二。于是特雷霍克·塔什先生创造了“负气”，并被坊间赞誉为“睿智的女性心理学家”。自特雷霍克·塔什先生崛起以来，任何书评家若非见到作家能用“负气”一词来为女主人公令人扼腕的通奸行径开脱，便决计不肯承认该作家称得上是“睿智的女性心理学家”。这一招更能成功诱使《笨拙》杂志（*Punch*）的书评家们认定全书主旨健康有益身心。特雷霍克·塔什先生对诸般骗局伎俩门儿清。他说道：“我刚为《每日大特卖报》（*The Daily Sale*）写完一部连载小说。我就是想狠狠揭露一番此类世风，揭露其中的挥霍浪费与不知廉耻。那些成天鬼混的姑娘们。我原以为主编大人会对某些段落提出非议，皆因里面颇有些露骨火辣的卧室私密描写，可他最终只要求我修改了一处（1处）。我原先写的是‘他在她身上随心所欲乱吻’，于是我便依言改成了‘他依着自家的心意吻了她’。”

===Original===

In a corner far across the crowded room sat Venice Pollen, most sedately between her father and her mother. We waved, and decided that it was too crowded to dance; but we did not know, Venice and I, that we were met that night in darkness.

Observe Venice. We will always be found on Venice’s side, and why? because she is a darling. Mark her now, and how the smoke about her clears, how clean she is, and so excited! For Venice! You know she is excited because she is so still, there between her hard father and her monstrous fat mother. Mark her there, a green flower with a mad golden head. And her eyes are blue, mad blue, and she is the queen of ten thousand freckles, of which she is very contemptuous, saying: “Who wants freckles?” And she had a noble forehead which would crinkle when she did not catch what you said, and that was often enough, for she was always talking herself. “Darling, darling, *darling*!” That is what she would say. And on her lion’s-cub head was a tumult of short dusty-gold hair, which was by nature rebellious, so that she must ever and again be giving her head a fierce backward shake, as though that was going to do any good. Mark her there, so sedate between her hard father and her monstrous fat mother. Not sedate really, Venice! Yet she must be sedate now, for Venice, who by ordinary knew not fear, was as though fascinated by fear of her father, who was none other than Nathaniel Pollen, once of Manchester, but now of Hampshire and Berkeley Square, for was he not as rich as Croesus would have been had Croesus owned the half of the newspapers of England?

===Chinese===

在拥挤大厅对过极遥远的一个角落里，威尼斯·波伦（Venice Pollen）正极其端庄娴静地端坐在自家双亲中间。我们彼此遥遥招手示意，断定舞池过于拥挤不宜下场跳舞；然而威尼斯与我彼时皆浑然未觉，那一夜我们在幽暗冥冥之中竟已然聚首。

且端详端详威尼斯吧。我们永远会站在威尼斯这一边，究竟所为何来？全因她是个惹人疼爱的心肝宝贝。且在此时瞧瞧她吧：周遭的烟雾是如何因她而消散，她是何等清爽明媚，又是何等激动兴奋！为了威尼斯！你深知她此刻心潮澎湃，皆因在严苛的父亲与臃肿庞大的母亲之间，她安坐得是如此纹丝不动。且端详端详端坐在此处的她吧：一朵顶着一头狂野金发的绿色鲜花。那一双眼眸呈蔚蓝色，狂热纯粹的蔚蓝，她是统领一万颗雀斑（10000颗雀斑）的女王——尽管她对自家的雀斑向来嗤之以鼻，嚷道：“谁稀罕长雀斑呀？”她长着一副高贵饱满的前额，每逢没能听清你的言语时便会微微蹙起，而这等情形更是家常便饭，皆因她自家向来嘴里喋喋不休。“亲爱的，亲爱的，*亲爱的*！”她总爱这般唤人。在她那宛如幼狮般的脑袋上，蓬松凌乱地顶着一头暗金色的短发，天生桀骜不驯，以致她时常必须用力向后猛甩脑袋，仿佛这般甩头当真能理顺发丝似的。且瞧瞧端坐在此处的她吧，在严酷的父亲与肥硕的母亲中间显得是如此娴静端庄。其实威尼斯骨子里何曾真正端庄过！然而眼下她却不得不端庄守礼，皆因寻常天不怕地不怕的威尼斯，此刻仿佛被对父亲的畏惧所深深震慑住了——那位父亲不是旁人，正是纳撒尼尔·波伦（Nathaniel Pollen），起家于曼彻斯特（Manchester），如今坐拥汉普郡（Hampshire）庄园与伯克利广场（Berkeley Square）宅邸的大亨；皆因倘若古希腊巨富克洛伊索斯（Croesus）曾坐拥全英格兰半数的报业帝国，恐怕其豪富也不过同波伦先生相仿吧？

===Original===

So there sat Venice, most excited-still, undoubtedly waiting for Napier. They were lovers, Napier and Venice, and in three days they would be married. Dark, shy, handsome Napier! Favourite of the gods, you might well call him, yet his was that rare, surprising quality which will keep a man poised in continual sunshine, which will never let him droop and laze in the certainty that his sins of omission and casualness will be forgiven him. He was, to talk for a change of the things that matter, in the Foreign Office, and worked conscientiously hard at a career which would﻿—“undoubtedly,” they said, “undoubtedly”﻿—in the course of time place Napier among the most honoured of the nation’s servants; although he would﻿—“undoubtedly,” one can’t help feeling, “undoubtedly”﻿—reach in the course of time the very same pinnacle if he did no work at all, for England and America are the only two countries left in the world wherein men’s charm and good looks are really appreciated by men in the political, high financial, diplomatic, and educational spheres.

Our table faced the swing-doors across the room, and through the crowd of dancers one could see who passed in and out. There was a press of young men standing vaguely by the door, perhaps doubtful whether they should stay or go to return another day. A very haughty and flushed-looking lady, expensively dressed in a dernier cri, which she wore like armour, tramped past them, looked suspiciously into their bland faces, and out. She suspected they might be thinking she was going to more than powder her nose. They were, she was, who cared?

===Chinese===

于是威尼斯便端坐在那儿，心潮澎湃却纹丝不动，毫无疑问正苦苦静候着内皮尔（Napier）的到来。内皮尔与威尼斯乃是一对情侣，且再过三天（3天后）他们便将喜结连理。面容英挺、生性腼腆、英俊潇洒的内皮尔！你尽可将他奉为天之骄子，然而他身上却自有一种罕见而令人惊叹的品质——那便是能让一个男儿始终沐浴在无尽阳光之下，绝不至于因为笃信自家的疏漏懈怠总能博得旁人宽恕而自甘堕落懒散。若是换个话头谈谈紧要的正经事，他供职于外交部（Foreign Office），且在一项随着岁月流逝“毫无疑问”——坊间皆言之凿凿宣称“毫无疑问”——定能将内皮尔送入举国最受尊崇的公仆勋列之中的仕途上兢兢业业、恪尽职守；尽管世人情不自禁暗忖，即便他平日里游手好闲尸位素餐，假以时日他也“毫无疑问”能登上同一权力巅峰，皆因英格兰与美利坚乃是当今举世仅存的唯二两个（2个国家）在政界、金融巨擘圈、外交使团与学界中男儿的风度魅力与出众仪表能真切博得男人们由衷赏识赞许的国家。

我们的长桌正对着大厅对过的双扇弹簧门，透过拥挤的舞客人群，门前人来人往的身影尽收眼底。有一群年轻后生正茫然挤在大门旁，大抵在犹豫究竟该留下来、抑或改日再登门寻欢。一名神态极其高傲、面泛红晕的贵妇——周身裹着一袭价格昂贵的当季最新款霓裳（*dernier cri*），宛如披挂着一副重铠一般——大踏步从他们身旁穿行而过，满腹狐疑地扫视着这帮年轻人温和的面孔，随后扬长步出大门。她疑心这帮小伙子大抵在琢磨她此去绝非仅仅是给鼻尖补补香粉那么简单。小伙子们确凿在这么想，她也确凿要行苟且之事，又有谁在乎呢？

===Original===

A voice rose above the saxophone at the table to the left of mine. It came from a heavy, drooping man with the eyes of a schoolboy, the smile of a genius, the gestures of a conqueror, and the face of a bully. He said: “There are two things in England that not even God could afford to be truthful about: Himself and the Navy.” With the man of destiny was the most beautiful woman in Ireland (Ulster) and a dark woman with a high bust and flashing eye, who spoke Cockney with an American accent. Her father was a lord. She said: “I am growing to detest London. There is nowhere to go and nothing to do when you get there.” The most beautiful woman in Ireland (Ulster) had hair as black as a raven’s wing and two aquamarines for eyes, while the symmetry of her features appalled the epithet. She said: “I took my little Juno out to tea with Fay Avalon today and she was so naughty on the handsome parquet floor, the mother’s darling!”

===Chinese===

在我左侧餐桌旁，一道嗓音盖过了萨克斯风的轰鸣。那声音出自一个身躯沉重臃肿、体态微驼的汉子：长着一双学童般的眸子，泛着天才般的微笑，带着征服者般的手势，却生就一副恶霸般的面孔。他说道：“在英格兰有两样物事（2样物事），乃至连上帝都负担不起讲真话的代价：祂自家，以及皇家海军（the Navy）。”伴随在这位天命骄子身旁的，乃是全爱尔兰阿尔斯特省（Ulster）最美丽的佳人，以及一位酥胸高耸、眼眸闪烁、操着一口带有美式口音的伦敦东区土话（Cockney）的深肤色贵妇。后者的父亲是位勋爵贵胄。她说道：“我如今越来越厌弃伦敦了。世间根本找不见什么值得去的地方，而一旦你当真去了那里，又全无正经事可做。”全爱尔兰阿尔斯特省最美丽的佳人生就一头乌黑如鸦羽般的秀发，那一双美眸宛如两颗海蓝宝石（2颗海蓝宝石），五官的匀称精致更令世间一切溢美之词相形见绌。她说道：“我今儿个带我家的小朱诺（Juno）去同费伊·阿瓦隆（Fay Avalon）共饮下午茶，它把人家精美的拼花木地板弄得一塌糊涂，这调皮的小宝贝儿！”

===Original===

Then things happened. Gerald happened. Gerald and Aphrodite.﻿ ﻿… Venice, Iris, Guy de Travest, Hugo Cypress, Napier, Colonel Duck, Gerald﻿ ﻿… if only one had a cinema for a moment! And there was also my Lady Pynte, with whom I should have been dancing. Where Mrs. Ammon went there also went Cornelia Pynte, and where Lady Pynte went there also went Angela Ammon. They were fine hearty women. And since Hilary was dancing with Mrs. Ammon I ought instantly to have begged the honour of taking the floor with Lady Pynte. There she sat, across the room, alone, a fine hearty woman. But, then, one goes to a nightclub to think, to be alone, to be comfortable, to eat a haddock. Lady Pynte thought dancing Good Exercise, and she was taller than me, too. A fine woman. Once, as Hilary toiled by with Mrs. Ammon, he whispered fiercely over her shoulder: “Why don’t you dance with the old trout?” But I drowned discourtesy to Lady Pynte in wine, for it was a “late night” at the Loyalty, which meant that you could drink wine until they took it from you. Lady Pynte was renowned as one of the five best women riders to hounds in the country. It was said that the foxes in the Whaddon Chase country ceased laughing when anyone said “Pynte!” near them. But Lady Pynte also had her politics, and she headed Movements; while Angela Ammon was more of a literary turn. Lady Pynte liked young men to Do; Mrs. Ammon to Dare. Lady Pynte liked young men to be Healthy and Normal; Mrs. Ammon preferred them to be Original. Lady Pynte liked Boys to be Boys; Mrs. Ammon didn’t mind if they were girls so long as they were Original. Lady Pynte insisted on Working for the Welfare of the People at Large and Not Just Our Own Little Class, she played bridge with a bantering tongue and a Borgia heart, she maintained that the best place at which to buy shoes was Fortnum & Mason’s, and if she saw you innocently taking the air of a sunny morning she would say: “You look not at all well, my good young man. Why don’t you take some Clean, Healthy exercise? You ought to be Riding.” That was why one maintained a defensive alliance with one’s haddock rather than do the manly thing and dance with Lady Pynte. She would say one ought to be riding, and for four years I had hidden from Lady Pynte the fact that I did not know how to ride. I simply did not dare to confess to Lady Pynte that I could not ride.

===Chinese===

随后变故骤生。杰拉尔德登场了。杰拉尔德与阿芙洛狄忒……威尼斯、艾丽丝、盖伊·德·特拉维斯特、雨果·赛普里斯（Hugo Cypress）、内皮尔、达克上校（Colonel Duck）、杰拉尔德……倘若此刻手头有一台电影放映机该有多好！此外还有佩恩特贵夫人（Lady Pynte）——我本该下场同她起舞的。安蒙夫人走到哪里，科妮莉亚·佩恩特（Cornelia Pynte）便跟到哪里；佩恩特夫人走到哪里，安吉拉·安蒙（Angela Ammon）便随行至何处。她们皆是体格健硕、快活爽朗的壮硕贵妇。既然希拉里正同安蒙夫人翩翩起舞，我本该立刻躬身恳请荣幸邀请佩恩特夫人滑入舞池。她正孤身一人端坐在大厅对过，一位体格健硕的豪爽妇人家。然而，凡人之所以踏足夜总会，本就是为了沉思默想、为了独处自遣、为了贪图惬意、为了大嚼盘中的黑线鳕鱼。佩恩特夫人笃信跳舞乃是强身健体的绝佳运动，且她的个头甚至比我还高出一筹。好一个魁梧壮硕的妇人。有一回，正当希拉里费劲巴拉地搂着安蒙夫人自一旁吃力滑过时，他越过夫人的肩头恶狠狠地冲我耳语道：“你怎么不下场同那条老鳟鱼跳一曲？”但我借由杯中烈酒将对佩恩特夫人的失礼冲淡殆尽，皆因今夜适逢忠诚俱乐部的“通宵特权夜”，意指只要侍者尚未强行收走酒瓶，你爱喝到几时便能喝到几时。佩恩特夫人乃是闻名遐迩的全英五大最佳猎狐女骑手之一（5位最佳女骑手之一）。相传在瓦登狩猎区（Whaddon Chase）出没的狐狸，只要一听闻身旁有人吐露出“佩恩特”的名号，便吓得连叫声都戛然而止。然而佩恩特夫人亦热衷政治，整日领导着各路社会运动；而安吉拉·安蒙则更富文人雅趣。佩恩特夫人喜欢年轻人脚踏实地苦干；安蒙夫人则喜欢年轻人敢想敢为。佩恩特夫人喜欢年轻人康健而端方正常；安蒙夫人则偏爱年轻人特立独行富于独创。佩恩特夫人喜欢男儿生就一副刚毅男儿气魄；安蒙夫人则只要对方富于独创性哪怕举止宛如闺秀少女亦毫不在意。佩恩特夫人坚决主张必须“为广大普罗民众的福祉而奋斗、而非仅仅着眼于我们自身这个狭隘的小阶层”；她打桥牌时嘴里冷嘲热讽、心肠却宛如波吉亚家族（Borgia）般狠辣；她一口咬定全天下买鞋的最佳去处当属福南梅森百货（Fortnum & Mason’s）；且倘若在一个阳光明媚的清晨撞见你无辜地散步透气，她准会训斥道：“我的好小伙，你瞧上去气色糟透了。何不去做点干干净净、有益身心的康健运动呢？你本该去策马骑行的。”正因如此，人们宁可同自家的黑线鳕鱼结成坚不可摧的防御同盟，也断不愿展现男儿气概下场同佩恩特夫人起舞。她准会念叨说凡人应当去策马骑行，而在过去的整整四年里（4年里），我始终对佩恩特夫人死死隐瞒着自家压根不会骑马的难堪事实。我根本不敢当面向佩恩特夫人坦白自己压根就不会骑马。

===Original===

I had already tried to pave the way to that denouement by confessing that I came from the lower classes, but she did not appear to think that any class could be so Low as that. She would show one round her stables, and one felt an awful fool standing there in the cold being expected to be intelligent about the various horses, whereas one could only mutter, “Ah, good horse!” or “Oh, there’s a fine horse!” until one day I remembered what Peter Page, the critic, had once told me, that whenever he was shown a horse by a horse-lover he would instantly say “What withers!” and thus create a sound and manly impression as a horse-fancier. But when I came out impressively with “What withers!” I thought that Lady Pynte looked at me suspiciously, and Hilary, who was also fancying horses with us, told me later that it wasn’t done to look a lady straight in the eye and say “What withers!” Horses make life complicated, that is what it is.

Hugo Cypress, dancing by with his wife Shirley, called out: “Ho, there! Seen the evening-paper? Friend of yours.﻿ ﻿…”

“What?” I said. “Hugo.﻿ ﻿…” But what on earth was this about the evening-paper? I was agitated﻿—suddenly, I was very agitated indeed. There is something quite beastly about evening-papers, beastly and naked.﻿ ﻿…

Astorias stayed his men, and Hilary came back to the table. Gloomily he looked at the angel that was frozen to its horse. And he looked worried.

“Hilary, what’s this I heard Hugo murmuring about the evening-paper?”

“Gerald,” said Hilary. “Hm.﻿ ﻿…”

“But what, Hilary? Not serious, surely?”

“Oh, not serious,” Hilary grunted. “Not serious. Hm. Just a nasty silly mess, I think. Didn’t catch what. Hm.﻿ ﻿…”

I realised then that I had known all the time. That curious, hopeless grinning.﻿ ﻿… But, good Lord, what sort of a mess? Hilary didn’t know. “Something in the evening-paper,” he said. Hilary looked hurt, worried, and I had that jumpy feeling that I must do something at once. But what sort of a mess? A drunkard’s row? What? Hilary didn’t know, and I was just about to ask the waiter if he could find me an evening-paper when two figures by the door held my eye. And a third just behind them.

===Chinese===

我先前曾试图借着坦白自家具备下层阶级出身为这一摊牌打下铺垫，可她似乎压根不以为世间竟有哪个阶级会低贱到不会骑马的地步。她常领着人参观自家的马厩，教人在寒风瑟瑟中伫立在那儿、被满心期许能对各色骏马品头论足一番时感到自家宛如一个彻头彻尾的蠢汉，只能翻来覆去咕哝着：“啊，好马！”抑或“哦，真是一匹骏马！”直到有一天，我想起了评论家彼得·佩奇（Peter Page）曾传授给我的秘诀：每逢爱马之人向他引见骏马时，他总会当场脱口赞叹道：“好一副鬐甲（withers）啊！”从而给旁人留下一个老道、阳刚的相马行家印象。然而，当我煞有介事地脱口赞叹出一声“好一副鬐甲！”时，我只觉得佩恩特夫人狐疑地上下打量着我；而当时同样伴随在一旁相马的希拉里日后告诫我说，直勾勾盯着一位贵妇的眼眸大呼“好一副马鬐甲”，在体统礼数上是万万行不通的。马匹教生活变得纷繁复杂，事情正是这般光景。

雨果·赛普里斯正同自家的娇妻雪莉（Shirley）自一旁滑跳而过，扬声高呼道：“喂，老兄！瞧见晚报了么？关于你的一位朋友……”

“什么？”我说道，“雨果……”然而晚报上究竟登了什么名堂？我心头不由得一阵焦躁——猝不及防间，我当真焦躁万分。晚报向来透着一股极其卑劣下作的气息，既野蛮又赤裸……

阿斯托里亚斯示意乐手们歇息片刻，希拉里折返回了餐桌旁。他郁郁不乐地凝视着那块已然冻结僵硬的培根生蚝热点心。他满脸愁容。

“希拉里，我刚才听雨果嘴里咕哝着关于晚报的事，究竟是怎么一回事？”

“杰拉尔德，”希拉里说道。“哼……”

“可究竟出了何事，希拉里？总不至于太严重吧？”

“哦，算不得严重，”希拉里嘟囔道。“算不得严重。哼。依我看，不过是惹上了一桩恶心愚蠢的烂摊子罢了。我没听清究竟是何事。哼……”

彼时我方才顿悟，原来我心底一直心知肚明。那副诡异而绝望的冷笑……然而，仁慈的主啊，究竟是何等烂摊子？希拉里对此一无所知。“晚报上登了什么东西，”他说道。希拉里显得既痛心又忧虑，而我心头则泛起一股坐立不安的冲动，只觉得自家必须立刻付诸行动。可究竟是何等烂摊子？酒鬼寻衅滋事打架斗殴么？究竟所为何事？希拉里一问三不知，我正打算招手叫侍者去为我寻一份晚报，大门旁的两道身影（2道身影）却骤然攫住了我的目光。紧随其后的，还有第三道身影（第3道身影）。

===Original===

“Kids!” murmured Hilary, with a sort of grudging smile. And they looked just that, for all their beauty﻿—“kids.” One saw them playing together under a tree. A long while ago, they had played together under a tree. The favourite of the gods and the shameless, shameful lady.﻿ ﻿…

“Hm,” grumbled Hilary. “Imitation.﻿ ﻿…”

But I knew, for I once had a friend who was a taxidermist. There were 396 white ermines round Iris. White and tawny and white. She was like a light, and you hadn’t realised what an infernal dungeon the place was until the door had suddenly opened and she had come in, wrapped in cloth of soft snow. Boy’s head, curly head, white and tiger-tawny. She was like a light, a sad, white light. I can’t describe her but like that. Napier had been standing by the door, waiting for the dance to cease, so that he might join Venice. Then Iris had come in, grave, very unselfconscious. She didn’t see Napier. He didn’t see Iris. Her companion was Colonel Duck, M.F.H.

“God, that man!” sighed Hilary. Oh, Iris was hopeless! Why, of all men, Colonel Duck?

Napier made to walk away. Iris and Colonel Duck made to follow Risotto. Maybe one of the 396 white ermines just brushed Napier’s sleeve. Maybe this, maybe that. “Kids!” said Hilary. Napier had started round, looked blank: tall, slender, dark-haired, dark eyes always fevered with a fear of you could never tell what﻿—they almost blinked now, you thought, at the light that Iris was, and she with her pools of eyes simply blazing with surprise and an unsure smile parting the painted mouth. “Napier!” “Iris!” As though, you know, someone with a soft “There!” had turned a tap somewhere. They smiled completely. Well, they would, the old friends. Naturally. She wouldn’t, I was sure, be calling him “Naps,” and she detesting abbreviations and the like.

===Chinese===

“两个孩子罢了！”希拉里勉强挤出一丝不情愿的微笑低语道。尽管他们生得如此风华绝代，瞧上去却真真切切不过是——“两个孩子”。世人仿佛能望见他们当年在一株大树底下嬉闹玩耍的光景。在极其久远的往昔岁月里，他们曾在一株大树底下嬉戏相伴。天之骄子与那位不知廉耻又深陷耻辱的贵妇人……

“哼，”希拉里嘟囔道，“假货仿造品……”

可我心知肚明，全因我曾有一位做动物标本剥制师的朋友。包裹在艾丽丝周身的，乃是整整396只雪白鼬皮（396只白鼬皮）。雪白、黄褐，雪白相间。她宛如一盏明灯；在这扇大门冷不丁推开、她裹着一袭柔白如雪的华服步入其中之前，你甚至压根未曾察觉这间所在原本是座何等昏暗如地狱般的逼仄囚牢。少年般的短发、蜷曲的发卷、雪白的面庞与虎褐色的秀发相映成趣。她宛如一盏明灯，一盏凄清幽冷的白炽明灯。除了这般形容，我委实找不出更贴切的字眼了。内皮尔原本正伫立在大门旁，静候舞曲终了以便穿过人群走向威尼斯。正在此时，艾丽丝步了进来，神情凝重端庄，全无半点扭捏做作之态。她并未看见内皮尔。内皮尔亦未看见艾丽丝。伴随在她身旁的，乃是猎狐犬队总管达克上校（Colonel Duck, M.F.H.）。

“老天爷啊，怎么偏偏是他！”希拉里叹息道。哎，艾丽丝委实无可救药！天底下有那么多男儿，她为何偏偏挑中了达克上校？

内皮尔正欲举步离去。艾丽丝与达克上校则正欲随在里索托身后前行。或许正是那396只白鼬皮中的某一小撮绒毛，恰巧轻拂过了内皮尔的衣袖。或许是出于这般缘故，或许是出于那般缘故。“两个孩子啊！”希拉里叹道。内皮尔猛地转过身来，神情一片错愕茫然：身形高挑、瘦削、黑发如漆，那一双深陷幽暗的眸子向来总带着一股旁人永远无从揣度的惊恐热病之色——在此时此刻，你只觉得在那道宛如明灯般的艾丽丝的光华映照之下，那一双眼眸几乎惊愕得眨巴个不停；而艾丽丝那双如深潭般的眼眸中更是充盈着炽烈的惊诧，一抹犹疑未定的微笑轻轻分开了敷有唇膏的薄唇。“内皮尔！”“艾丽丝！”诸位明白，那情景宛如有人轻声道了一声“好啦！”，随后拧开了体内的某一处闸门。他们相视展颜开怀而笑。是啊，老友重逢，自当如此。理所当然。我敢断定，她决计不会唤他作“纳普斯（Naps）”，皆因她向来对昵称缩写之流深恶痛绝。

===Original===

The wrong sort of Jewess gave a short, audible outline of Iris to Mr. Trehawke Tush. Hilary stared at her venomously. Then he stared across the room at Colonel Duck venomously. Colonel Duck stood behind Iris’s white shoulder, a red dragon of a man, smiling relentlessly with his well-known geniality. Napier did not appear to see Colonel Duck, M.F.H. Napier and Iris were talking very quickly, laughing, maybe rather shyly. Then Astorias, refreshed, hurled his men against the conversation; bravely it held on for a second or two, then lay shuddering and shattered, and gone was Napier, gone Iris towards a table with Colonel Duck, whose red, relentless geniality showed no hint of the certain fact that the next time he was at that talkative club of his he would say that Napier Harpenden had been another of Iris Storm’s “affairs” and might quite well be again, Iris Storm being what she was. Notably good at all games and sports was Colonel Duck, M.F.H., and therefore tolerated with respect by decent men.

“I wonder if she knows anything about Gerald,” I was saying, when from her table across the room she seemed to be beckoning. To Hilary, not to me. She looked very serious. The emerald shone on the third finger of her right hand. She did not appear to see me. I felt bitter.

“Hm,” growled Hilary. He wanted to be persuaded to go. He wanted to go reluctantly. “Hate that Duck man so,” he said pathetically.

“Go on, Hilary. She might know something. I’ll get a paper.”

“Why, there’s Guy!” said Hilary. “Must have just come up from Mace. There, by the door.﻿ ﻿…”

The carpet of colours, on which the men were sprinkled like the black smuts on a town garden, swayed between us and the doorway, but no crowd might hide that man, for he was tall as a tree and his crisp yellow hair glared like a menace above the intervening heads and his frozen blue eyes petrified smoke, noise, and distance. Hilary was standing, about to go towards Iris. He looked rather sheepish at being found by Guy at the Loyalty. Most unsmiling was Guy that night.

“Ross must have told him we were here,” I said. “He’ll have come about Gerald.﻿ ﻿…”

“This foul place!” Hilary snapped. “You go downstairs with Guy, and I’ll get Iris to rid herself of her fancy friend and bring her down.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

那位品位低劣的犹太妇人正用清晰可闻的语调向特雷霍克·塔什先生简明扼要地勾勒着关于艾丽丝的种种不堪恶名。希拉里恶狠狠地瞪视着她。随后他又恶狠狠地越过大厅瞪视着达克上校。达克上校伫立在艾丽丝白皙香肩后侧，身躯健硕宛如一条赤红巨龙，脸上挂着他那招牌式的和蔼可亲微笑。内皮尔似乎根本看不见猎犬队总管达克上校的身影。内皮尔与艾丽丝语速飞快地交谈着，欢笑着，神态间大抵带着几分腼腆。旋即，休整完毕的阿斯托里亚斯指挥麾下的乐手们再次奏响了雷霆般的乐声，席卷向这番交谈；两人的对话英勇顽强地支撑了一两秒钟（1、2秒钟），随后便在乐声中支离破碎分崩离析；内皮尔抽身离去，艾丽丝则同达克上校一道走向一张餐桌——达克上校脸上那副赤红、顽固的慈眉善目神色，全未泄露出他下回光顾自家的八卦俱乐部时定会四处宣扬的铁板钉钉的丑闻：他定会宣称内皮尔·哈彭登（Napier Harpenden）曾是艾丽丝·斯托姆众多“裙下之臣”中的一位，且鉴于艾丽丝·斯托姆的放浪天性，日后旧情复燃亦大有可能。鉴于猎犬队总管达克上校在各项体育狩猎竞技中技艺超群，正派绅士们倒向来对他礼敬包容。

“不知她是否知晓杰拉尔德的底细，”我正说着，她却仿佛在大厅对过的餐桌旁遥遥招手示意。是在招呼希拉里，而非招呼我。她的神情显得极其凝重。那枚祖母绿戒指正在她右手无名指上熠熠生辉。她似乎根本未曾看见我的存在。我心头不禁涌起一阵酸涩。

“哼，”希拉里低声嘟囔道。他巴不得旁人好生劝他过去。他巴不得自家表现得万分不情愿。“最恶心达克那个混账东西了，”他可怜巴巴地说道。

“快去吧，希拉里。她或许知晓些内情。我去取一份晚报。”

“哎呀，盖伊居然也来啦！”希拉里说道。“准是刚从梅斯庄园（Mace）风尘仆仆赶回城里。且瞧瞧大门旁……”

五彩缤纷的地毯——置身其间的男宾们宛如城市花圃中散落的煤烟黑点一般——在我们与大门之间摇曳起伏，然而任何人群亦休想遮掩住那个男人的身影：皆因他高大挺拔如乔木，那一头卷曲金发在周遭攒动的人头之上宛如一道凛冽威慑般耀眼夺目，而那一双冰封的蓝眸更是瞬间令满堂的烟雾、喧嚣与空间距离尽皆凝固石化。希拉里原本已然起身正欲走向艾丽丝。眼见自家在忠诚俱乐部被盖伊当场抓个正着，他神情颇有几分讪讪的尴尬。那一夜的盖伊，脸上全无半点笑意。

“定是罗斯告知他我们在此处，”我说道，“他准是为了杰拉尔德的事特意赶来的……”

“这乌烟瘴气的鬼地方！”希拉里厉声啐道，“你且同盖伊一道下楼去，我想法子教艾丽丝摆脱掉她那位风流男伴，随后领她一道下楼……”

### II / 第二节

### II / 
===Original===

And that was how, soon after midnight that night, I found myself for the first time in the car of the flying stork. For the first time.﻿ ﻿… Iris had dropped her boyish-looking chauffeur in the course of the evening, because, she said, she only liked driving at night, when the air blew clean and chill. She drove with assurance, that is to say, she drove as though her mind was not in the same world as the steering-wheel. The great bonnet swept round by the squat Palace and up the slope of Saint James’s Street, which only by night may remember a little of the elegance it has long since forfeited by day.

“But that’s not the point,” I remember saying. “He won’t care a button what anyone else is thinking about it. He’ll just go mad at the humiliation in himself, he’ll worry it, making a mountain of sordidness.﻿ ﻿…” I had told her that Gerald had sent her his love, and her eyes had lit up at that, and she had laughed, shyly. “That’s better,” she had said, and now she said: “Yes, that’s the point. He’s proud, proud as Lucifer﻿ ﻿… and such a baby! Oh, Gerald, you sensitive beast! I’m going abroad tomorrow, and he must either come with me or he must join me quickly, quickly. You’ll persuade him, too, won’t you?” I did not say that, if I knew Gerald, he would probably be in a state far beyond persuasion. But, I thought, there was no harm in trying to see him.

At first, when Guy had told me downstairs at the Loyalty, I had just laughed. It seemed so absurd, fantastic. Gerald had been arrested in Hyde Park for “annoying women!” It was, you can see, unbelievable. How could Gerald “annoy” a woman, Gerald who was so shy that he could never even speak to one? “But there it is,” said Guy.

===Chinese===

正因如此，在当天夜里午夜刚过不久，我生平头一回（第1次）置身于那辆翱翔银鹳跑车之中。头一回……艾丽丝在当晚早些时候便打发走了那位生得宛如少年般的司机，皆因她声称，自家唯独喜欢在夜间驾车——当夜风吹拂得清冽而料峭之时。她驾车时笃定从容，也就是说，她掌舵时的心神仿佛同方向盘根本不在同一个世界里。巨大的引擎盖呼啸着掠过矮小的圣詹姆斯宫（Palace），径自冲上圣詹姆斯街（Saint James’s Street）的斜坡——唯独在深夜里，这条街方能依稀追忆起早已在白昼间丧失殆尽的少许典雅风貌。

“但关键不在于此，”我记得自家这般说道，“旁人究竟对此作何感想，他根本连半点都不在乎。他只会为了自身所蒙受的奇耻大辱而彻底发狂，他会日夜纠结咀嚼这桩耻辱，将其小题大做化作一座深不见底的污秽大山……”我先前已告知她杰拉尔德托我向她问好，听闻此言，她的眼眸曾骤然泛起神采，带着腼腆展颜轻笑。“这倒好些了，”她当时这般说道，而此刻她又说道：“不错，关键正是在于此。他骨子里傲慢得很，傲慢得宛如路西法（Lucifer）……偏偏又生就一副婴儿般的心肠！哦，杰拉尔德，你这敏感多疑的冤家！我明日便要启程出国了，他要么随我同行，要么必须尽快、火速同我汇合。你也会帮着劝劝他的，对不对？”我并未点破：依我对杰拉尔德的了解，他届时所陷入的狂乱境地恐怕早已非言语所能劝抚。然而我暗忖，前去探望他一眼横竖并无坏处。

起初，当盖伊在忠诚俱乐部的楼下告知我这桩变故时，我甚至忍不住当场笑出了声。这听上去委实太荒诞离奇、太不可思议了。杰拉尔德居然在海德公园（Hyde Park）因“寻衅骚扰妇女”而被拘捕就范！诸位能看出，这简直是天方夜谭。杰拉尔德怎可能去“骚扰”女人？杰拉尔德腼腆怯懦得甚至连当面同女人吐露一个字都办不到！“可事情偏偏就这般发生了，”盖伊说道。

===Original===

Perhaps it is because that was the last time I was ever at the Loyalty, but I remember the most irrelevant details and the vivid way each one of them seemed to impress some part of my mind. Guy and I stood in the deserted Bar. Through the open door at the far end came the clean, somehow biting tang of a marble swimming-bath: a faint splash now and then, a rustle of water: a boyish American voice calling sharp and loud: “Dive, you Julie, dive and get it over! You’ve got no hips, kid, and you can’t drown without hips. I want to go eat some food.” Then, I remember, Billy Swift walked intently past us, towards the Cloakroom. He comes to mind vividly because that was the last time I saw Billy Swift alive. His thin, lined, scarlet face glowed with the health-giving breezes that penetrate into corners of clubs and restaurants where men sit drinking brandy; his blue eyes always peered eagerly and kindly at you, as though he had something of the first importance to say. He said, very hoarsely: “There’s a boy up there dancing with two wooden legs. Good boy, I call him. Good night.” And in a minute or two he repassed us, walking intently, his crimson grey-haired head, immaculate in every detail, sticking like an old fighting-bird’s out of the wide astrakhan collar of the coat that he always wore against the midnight chill. Two months later he was found on the cliffs near Dover with that head beaten in, and someone was hanged. Billy Swift wouldn’t have had him hanged. “My fault,” he would have said hoarsely. “My fault, chaps.”

“But there it is,” Guy said thoughtfully. “Sickening, isn’t it? Might appeal, of course.﻿ ﻿…”

“He’ll not appeal,” I said. Imagine Gerald “appealing” against a five-pound fine for “indecently annoying” a woman in Hyde Park!

Guy always spoke low, he murmured in a chill voice, but you could always hear every word he said. Not that you didn’t, after a while, know all his words by heart, for Guy’s was one of those vocabularies that a classical education is supposed to have expanded. As he spoke he would always be looking at some point just above the crown of your head.

===Chinese===

或许全因那是我生平最后一次涉足忠诚俱乐部，我至今仍清晰铭记着当时诸多最不相干的琐碎细节，以及每一个细节是如何栩栩如生地烙印在我心头的某处角落。盖伊与我伫立在空无一人的酒吧间里。透过远端敞开的门扉，飘来大理石游泳池那股清冷、不知怎的颇为刺鼻的气息：间或传来微弱的水花飞溅声，以及池水的荡漾翻卷声；一道带有浓重美式口音的年轻嗓音尖锐而嘹亮地高呼道：“跳啊，朱莉（Julie），快跳下去省得磨蹭！你根本没长胯骨，小妞，没胯骨的人是绝不会淹死的。我还急着赶去吃宵夜呢。”随后我记得，比利·斯威夫特（Billy Swift）步履匆匆地从我们身旁穿过，径直走向衣帽间。我之所以如此清晰地忆起他，全因那是我生平最后一次见到活生生的比利·斯威夫特。他那张瘦削、布满皱纹、涨得猩红的面孔，正泛着男人们坐饮白兰地的俱乐部与餐馆角落里所特有的“益寿延年”微醺红光；那一双蓝眸总是急切而慈祥地端详着你，仿佛他心头揣着天底下头等紧要的机密大事非同你倾诉不可。他嗓音极其沙哑地说道：“楼上有个年轻小伙正拄着两条假腿（2条假腿）跳舞呢。依我看，真是个好小伙。晚安。”一两分钟后（1、2分钟后），他又步履匆匆地从我们身旁折返而过，那颗泛着猩红、夹杂着白发、每一处细节皆一丝不苟的脑袋，宛如一只凶悍的老斗禽一般，自那件他平素专门用来抵御午夜严寒、配有宽大阿斯特拉罕羔皮领（astrakhan）的大衣领口处探将出来。两个月后（2个月后），有人在多佛尔（Dover）附近的悬崖下发现了他的尸首，脑袋被人彻底砸烂，凶手最终伏法绞死。其实倘若依着比利·斯威夫特的脾性，他是决计不愿让那人偿命绞死的。“是我的罪过，”他定会沙哑着嗓子说，“老兄们，全怪我自己。”

“可事情偏偏就这般发生了，”盖伊若有所思地说道，“真教人反胃恶心，不是么？当然，他或许可以提起上诉……”

“他决计不会上诉的，”我说道。且设想一下杰拉尔德为了在海德公园因“下流猥亵骚扰妇女”而被判处五镑罚金（5镑罚金）一事去提起“上诉”的滑稽场面吧！

盖伊说话向来语调极低，用清冷的嗓音轻声低语，但你总能清清楚楚听清他吐露出的每一个字眼。倒不是说不多时你便不能将他的话倒背如流——皆因盖伊的词汇储备正是那种世人认定借由古典精英教育所能开拓出的典型范本。交谈之际，他的目光总会直勾勾定格在你头顶正上方的某处虚空之中。

===Original===

“Sorry about that boy,” he was saying thoughtfully. “He’s had no luck. And this Hyde Park business might happen to anyone nowadays.﻿ ﻿…” He looked down at me suddenly from that height of his, and I was, as always, surprised by the profound childishness which would suddenly sweep the ice out of the blue of those eyes.

“Beasts,” he went on, almost pathetically. “But aren’t they﻿—those Park police? Arresting nice old clergymen, Privy Councillors, anyone, just because a poor old boy who’s been brought up too well feels like having a word or two with a sickening woman. I mean, you need torpedo-netting around you to get round the Park in safety nowadays. Well, don’t you? And now they plant poor young Gerald. I’m sure, aren’t you, that these police put the women there on purpose as﻿—what d’you call them?”

“Agents provocateurs?”

“Well, have it your own way. But I’ve been watching the police round about here lately, and of course they’re mostly very good fellows, the best, but the police round the Park are quite a different lot. I’d like to kick them for the way they look those poor devils of women up and down as though they were dirt. I never thought much of the type of sneak who went for the Military Police during the war, and these fellows seem rather like that. Anything for an arrest and promotion.” He smiled faintly. Guy’s eyes seemed always to get most frosty when he smiled. “I once promoted some of them the wrong way for being inhuman. Inhuman, that’s what these blighters get if you don’t keep an eye on them. And these Park fellows seem somehow to have got spoilt since the war. I mean, it just looks like that to an outsider. Good Lord, you’ve got to have laws and to keep laws, but you needn’t set a lot of dirty sneaks at the Bolshevik game of ruining gentlemen just for being silly old asses.”

I stared at the one black pearl that from time immemorial had stained Guy’s shirtfront, which somehow seemed to fit him as no one else’s ever could. Guy was easy to listen to, because you always knew what he would say and how he would say it. (He had an enormous reverence for any man of the smallest talent, any man “who did things with his brain.”)

“I saw him for a minute this evening,” I said. “He seemed rather queer, but he said nothing about it.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“真替那小伙感到惋惜，”他若有所思地低语道，“他生平向来时运不济。而如今海德公园这类破事，随时可能落到任何人的头上……”他冷不丁自高耸的个头居高临下俯视着我，正如往常一样，我再次为那一双蓝眸间冷不丁将寒冰一扫而空、取而代之的深沉稚气而惊异称奇。

“简直是帮畜生，”他近乎可怜巴巴地继续念叨道，“难道不是么——那帮公园巡警？动辄抓捕慈眉善目的年迈牧师、枢密院顾问官（Privy Councillors），乃至任何人，仅仅是因为某个家教太好的可怜老绅士心血来潮想同某个教人生厌的妇人家搭上一两句话（1、2句话）。我的意思是，如今年头你在公园四周散步若想求得起码的周全，周身非得拉上一圈防鱼雷铁丝网不可。怎么，难道不是么？眼下他们又把栽赃陷害的黑手伸向了可怜的年轻杰拉尔德。我敢断定——难道你不以为然么——那帮警察分明是故意把那些女人们安插在那儿充当……该怎么称呼来着？”

“引诱犯罪的卧底密探（*Agents provocateurs*）么？”

“随你怎么称呼吧。但我近来一直在留意这一带的警察；当然，他们大多数皆是顶好、无可挑剔的好小伙，可公园周遭的那帮警员却完全是另一路货色。瞧着他们把那些可怜巴巴的落魄妇人从头到脚打量得宛如污泥粪土一般，我真恨不得狠狠踹他们几脚。大战期间我向来对那些自告奋勇去当宪兵（Military Police）的下作卑鄙之徒嗤之以鼻，而眼前这帮家伙横竖也差不离。只要能立功抓人获得擢升，他们什么下三滥勾当都干得出来。”他淡淡地笑了一笑。每逢盖伊展颜微笑之际，他的眸光似乎反而最为冰冷刺骨。“有一回，因为某些宪兵泯灭人性，我曾特意‘反向提拔’痛整过他们几人。泯灭人性，这帮混账东西只要你稍不留神便会变成这副德性。大战之后，公园里的那帮警员不知怎的彻底败坏变质了。我的意思是，在外人眼中事情分明便是这般光景。老天爷在上，世间固然要有王法律条、亦必须恪守王法，但你断无理由纵容一帮下作无耻的告密之徒去大玩布尔什维克（Bolshevik）那一套阴谋把戏，仅仅因为绅士们偶尔犯了糊涂像个老蠢才，便非要把人家整得身败名裂不可。”

我凝视着自古以来便镶嵌在盖伊衬衫胸前的那唯一一颗黑珍珠（1颗黑珍珠）——那颗黑珍珠佩戴在他身上不知怎的显得是如此浑然天成，换作天底下任何旁人皆断无这般风姿。倾听盖伊的言谈是极其省心省力的事，皆因你永远心知肚明他究竟要说些什么、以及他会用何等调门娓娓道来。（他对任何身怀微末才干之人、对任何“凭脑子吃饭的人”，皆抱有至高无上的由衷敬意。）

“今晚我曾同他见过一面，”我说道，“他神态颇为古怪，却对此事只字未提……”

===Original===

“But imagine the young devil! This business happened one night last week, and he doesn’t then come to see you about it﻿—or even Hilary or me, because, of course, I’d have done all I could for him, for old Barty’s sake as well as because he behaved himself in the war. I mean, this will almost kill old Eve Chalice when she sees it in the morning papers. It’s her I’m sorry for, for she’s always been fighting this sticky patch in the March brood﻿—first her eldest brother, old Portairley, then her younger brother, Barty, then her niece Iris, and now young Gerald comes along to make the poor old dear cry her eyes out again. God, the vileness of it! Picking up odd women in parks. I haven’t got a paper with me, but you ought to see the vile way they put down every beastly detail, and you can see as clear as anything that it was more bad luck and childishness on Gerald’s part than anything else. But, good Lord, what’s the matter with the man! I mean, one simply doesn’t go into the Park for women! The accuser, or whatever you call them, was a woman called Spirit, and in evidence two plainclothes men and a constable. I’m going to have an eye kept on Mrs. Spirit, just to see all’s fair and square. I mean, it’s beginning to look as though the law was the ass that St. George forgot to kill while he was showing off with that sickening dragon. This Mrs. Spirit said﻿—wish I had a paper﻿—that she was sitting on a bench waiting for her brother, when Gerald sat down beside her and made ‘indecent’ proposals. Whereupon she was so shocked﻿—and she a grown-up married woman, too﻿—that she jumped up like a scalded cat and let out some sickening howls, and up come the police. Now you can’t help thinking they were waiting behind a tree with old Spirit as a bait, can you? and caught young Gerald instead of a Dean.﻿ ﻿… They’d get more promotion, I shouldn’t wonder, for a Dean.﻿ ﻿…”

And as Guy spoke I saw Gerald glancing at the evening-paper on the curb of Curzon Street, and I saw him suddenly throw back his head and laugh at the heavens.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“可且设想一下那个浑小子吧！这起烂事分明是上周某一夜发生的，随后他竟压根没去登门找你商议——甚至连希拉里或我都没找，皆因看在故友老巴蒂的情分上、看在他当年在大战中舍生忘死的功勋上，我断然会竭尽所能为他周旋到底。我的意思是，当明儿个早报一出，老伊芙·查利斯一见报纸恐怕当场便要活活气死。我唯独替她感到揪心难过，皆因她一生都在苦苦同马奇家族这股劣根性搏斗抗争——先是她的大哥老波泰利，接着是她的胞弟巴蒂，随后是她的侄女艾丽丝，眼下年轻的杰拉尔德又冒了出来，教那可怜的老太太再次痛哭流涕伤心欲绝。上帝啊，那是何等下流龌龊的丑闻！在公园里搭讪野女人。我手头没有带报纸，但你真该瞧瞧报纸上将每一处恶心细节铺陈得何等不堪入目，可旁人一眼便能看出，这纯粹是杰拉尔德时运不济加上心智幼稚所致，绝非真有什么不可告人的歹念。然而，仁慈的主啊，那汉子究竟是发了什么神经！我的意思是，凡人体面绅士断不可能跑去海德公园寻觅女人！原告——或者随你怎么称呼那号原告妇人吧——是个名叫斯皮里特（Spirit）的女人，出庭作证的则是两名便衣暗探与一名巡警（2名便衣与1名巡警）。我打算派人暗中盯死那位斯皮里特夫人，好教查明事情究竟是否光明正大。我的意思是，眼下瞧着这王法律条，倒真像当年圣乔治（St. George）在大战恶龙大出风头时忘却斩杀掉的一头蠢驴。这位斯皮里特夫人声称——真恨自家手头没带报纸——她当时正坐在长椅上静候自家的兄弟，杰拉尔德冷不丁挨着她坐下，并提出了‘下流猥亵’的非分非礼要求。闻听此言，她吓得大惊失色——而且她分明还是个已婚的成年妇人家呢——当场宛如一只被开水烫了的野猫般一跃而起，扯着嗓子发出一阵令人作呕的尖叫哀号，于是警察便应声扑了上来。眼下你情不自禁会断定，警察分明是把那个老斯皮里特充作诱饵暗中埋伏在大树后头，不是么？结果他们没能钓到一位教长大人（Dean），反倒把年轻的杰拉尔德给逮了个正着……我丝毫不怀疑，倘若逮到一位堂堂教长，他们所能获得的擢升必定更为丰厚……”

正当盖伊侃侃而谈之际，我脑海中浮现出杰拉尔德伫立在寇松街街沿翻阅晚报的身影，浮现出他猛然仰起头颅冲着苍穹放声狂笑的光景……

===Original===

Gerald, Gerald! The despiser of the world caught by the meanest trap of the world’s unrest. The worshipper of the hero who had died “for purity” figuring in the filthy columns of the cheap Sunday Press as another peer’s nephew gone wrong. Gerald, starved of life, Gerald who knew no woman, Gerald who wrote the tale of a man who had lived “for purity”﻿ ﻿… and he had sat down beside a woman called Spirit on a bench in Hyde Park. Those nightmare women who rave in the minds of lonely men, soft women marvellously acquiescent, possible, the woman Aphrodite, goddess of love and beauty, silent as marble, but acquiescent﻿ ﻿… and Aphrodite had dwindled into Mrs. Spirit, who was sitting waiting for her brother in Hyde Park, and the law lurking nearby to give the Sunday papers “copy.” And I saw Mr. Auk in an angle of the little tunnel, telling a friend of his something funny about Gerald.﻿ ﻿…

“It makes one just sick, Guy. Sick.﻿ ﻿…”

“Now look here,” Guy murmured, tapping my shoulder with one finger. “Don’t you waste any time being sick just now, but go round and see the young devil﻿—”

“I’m going straight away.”

“Bright boy. And just﻿ ﻿… Oh, tell him it’s all right and not to be an ass all his life. Tell him we’re all on his side, and if there’s going to be any being sick that we’re all going to be sick together and in one corporate body, or words to that effect. Poor young devil. And I know he’ll be feeling this, because I had a sort of eye on him in France, and he seemed as sensitive as a violin string﻿—”

“And drink’s made him worse now. He’s almost certain to be nearly speechless tonight. But I’ll see.”

“Lord, O Lord, what a mess Barty left behind him! But you see what I mean? All you’ve got to tell young Gerald is not to make a mountain of this in his mind, as it’s the sort of thing that might happen to anyone who is ass enough to go into the Park at night without an escort, and you never know but they mightn’t one night arrest the Bishop of London himself for saying ‘How do’ to his aunt.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

杰拉尔德啊，杰拉尔德！这位蔑视尘寰万物的狂客，竟被尘俗纷扰中最卑劣下作的陷阱生生套牢。这位将殉道“为了纯洁”的英雄奉若神明的膜拜者，如今竟作为又一个堕落败德的贵族侄儿赫然登上了廉价周日报刊污浊不堪的版面。杰拉尔德，对真实生活饥渴至极的杰拉尔德，生平从未涉足过女人的杰拉尔德，亲手谱写出一位“为了纯洁”而活的男子之传奇的杰拉尔德……偏偏在海德公园的长椅上挨着一个名叫斯皮里特的妇人坐了下来。那些在孤寂男儿心头狂乱游荡的梦魇妇人，那些柔媚驯服得不可思议、触手可及的女人，爱与美之女神阿芙洛狄忒（Aphrodite），沉寂如大理石、却驯服顺从的女神……而阿芙洛狄忒最终竟蜕化成了坐在海德公园长椅上苦等兄弟的斯皮里特夫人，而王法律条则潜伏在一旁为周日小报奉上绝妙的“新闻猛料”。我又望见了奥克先生缩在小地道拐角处、正冲着熟人绘声绘色讲述关于杰拉尔德的滑稽笑话的光景……

“这真教人反胃作呕，盖伊。恶心透顶……”

“我说老弟，”盖伊轻声低语道，用一根手指（1根手指）轻轻敲打着我的肩头，“眼下切莫把大好光阴虚掷在作呕反胃上，赶快折返回去看看那个浑小子——”

“我即刻便去。”

“好小伙。顺便……哦，告诉他万事大吉，教他切莫一辈子当个蠢驴。告诉他我们大伙皆站在他这一边，倘若非要有人反胃作呕不可，那咱们大伙也当结成同盟同甘共苦一道反胃，或者大抵诸如此类的话吧。可怜的年轻冤家。我深知他心头定然煎熬难当，皆因当年在法兰西（France）战场上我曾留意关照过他，他敏感得宛如一根小提琴的琴弦——”

“而酗酒如今教他雪上加霜。今晚他几乎注定要醉得语无伦次口不能言了。但我自会前去一探究竟。”

“主啊，我的主啊，老巴蒂身后留下的究竟是怎样一摊烂泥啊！但你可明白我的苦心？你只需正告年轻的杰拉尔德，切莫在心底把这桩破事当成什么天塌下来的大难，皆因但凡哪个糊涂蛋胆敢在深夜孤身涉足公园而没有旁人护卫，此类倒霉事便随时可能摊到头上；说不定哪天深夜，连堂堂伦敦主教（Bishop of London）只因向自家的亲姑母道了声‘你好’，也会被当场扭送法办呢……”

===Original===

Now I have read in books about people “sailing” into places, and I suppose Iris came into the deserted Bar like that. Hilary must have been just behind her, for I heard his voice, but I only saw Iris, and I remember how she seemed to hold the white ermine round her with one clenched hand, and how the great emerald shone like a green fly on the soft, soft white. And the tawny curls danced their formal dance on her cheeks as she came towards us, swiftly, oh swiftly, saying, in that suddenly strong, clear voice: “Oh, Guy﻿ ﻿… and friend of Gerald! Will you help me, dear friend? I want to go round to see Gerald, and Hilary says you still have the key of the house. I went hours ago, but I could get no answer at the door. I wonder, would you come with me?”

“Iris,” said Guy sternly, and I remember the way she threw back her head to look at him, and I thought again of the queer, unconscious way she had of always meeting men on their own ground. “Why don’t you ever look up your old friends when you’re in London, Iris? Or aren’t we your old friends? Or is that fine representative English gentleman, Colonel Duck, your old friend? Answer me yes or no.”

“Oh, Guy!” she said softly, sadly. “I wouldn’t have you be a humbug. I wouldn’t have you and Hilary be humbugs﻿—you two, out of all the world.”

“But, honest, Iris, I’d like to see you. Ask Hilary. ‘Where’s that girl got to?’ I asks, and he says ‘hm,’ says he, if you see what I mean.”

“Whereas I, Guy, have learnt not to regret old friends. I’ve become an old woman on my travels, and one of the first things an old woman must learn is that the best way to keep old friends is not to see them, for then you can at least keep the illusion that they are friends﻿ ﻿… which is, perhaps, a little different from being ‘old friends.’﻿ ﻿…”

“Iris, don’t be so bitter!” snapped Hilary. That, I thought, came rather well from Hilary. Just at that moment a woman screamed from the swimming-bath, there was a resounding splash. Guy was saying: “You’d better take Gerald away for a while, Iris.”

“If he’ll only come,” she said, “that’s what I want to do.﻿ ﻿…”

I remember thinking just then that I mustn’t forget to thank her for that beautiful notepaper, and also to ask her what was that last word in her note.

===Chinese===

我曾自书卷中读到过形容佳人“翩然若仙舟般滑入”厅堂的辞藻，而我想艾丽丝步入这间空荡荡的酒吧间时大抵正是这般光景。希拉里定然紧随在她身后，皆因我听见了他的嗓音；但我眼中唯独望见了艾丽丝，我仍清晰记得她是如何用一只紧握的手（1只手）将周身的雪白鼬皮裹紧在身上，而那枚硕大的祖母绿戒指又是如何在极轻柔纯白的面料上宛如一只翠绿甲虫般熠熠生辉。当她快步——哦，何其迅捷——朝我们走来时，黄褐色的卷发在她两颊跳着齐整的舞蹈，用骤然变得清脆坚定的嗓音说道：“哦，盖伊……还有杰拉尔德的朋友！亲爱的朋友，你肯帮帮我么？我想折返回去看看杰拉尔德，希拉里说你手里尚留着那栋寓所的大门钥匙。我几个钟头前曾去过一回，可任凭怎样叩门皆无人应答。不知你能否陪我走一趟？”

“艾丽丝，”盖伊神情威严地说道；我依然记得她仰头望向他的神态，心头再次忆起她那种奇特天成、永远能在任何男人自家的阵地上与其分庭抗礼的奇特做派。“艾丽丝，为何你每逢身在伦敦，就从来不肯主动登门探望你的老朋友呢？难道我们算不上你的故交老友么？抑或那位堂堂英格兰绅士的典范代表达克上校，才是你的老相识？老老实实回答我，‘是’还是‘不是’。”

“哦，盖伊！”她凄楚柔和地轻声道，“我不愿见到你变成一个道貌岸然的伪善之徒。全天下任何人皆可虚伪做作，我唯独不愿见到你同希拉里变成伪君子——全天下唯独你们两个人（你们2人）不可以。”

“可是，说句掏心窝子的话，艾丽丝，我心里当真巴不得见见你。不信你大可问问希拉里。‘那丫头究竟野到哪儿去了？’我常常这般打听，而他却只顾着嘴里‘哼’个不停，你若明白我的意思便知晓了。”

“反观我呢，盖伊，我在游历生涯中早已学会了不再为老朋友而叹息挂怀。在漂泊之中我已经老成了一个风烛残年的老妇人，而做老妇人必须领悟的头一条真谛，便是捍卫老朋友交情的绝佳妙法莫过于避而不见；皆因唯有如此，你至少尚能维系住彼此仍是朋友的幻象……而这，或许同沦为名存实亡的‘老相识’略有不同吧……”

“艾丽丝，休要这般尖酸刻薄！”希拉里没好气地喝道。我暗自寻思，这话出自希拉里之口倒委实再贴切不过了。恰在那一当口，自地下游泳池方向传来一声女子的尖叫，伴随着一声震耳欲聋的水花飞溅响动。盖伊正说道：“艾丽丝，你最好领着杰拉尔德出外避避风头，离开一阵子。”

“只要他肯随我走，”她说道，“那便是我心心念念渴盼去办的事……”

我记得自己彼时心头正暗忖，切莫忘却了当面向她寄赠精美信纸一事致谢，并务必好生探问一番她在字条结尾处所写下的那个字究竟是何名堂。

===Original===

“I’ve got an idea,” Hilary was saying, in the specially detached voice he keeps for ideas, “that now we *are* in this foul nightclub we might as well do a bit of good. There’s old Pollen upstairs, and we might﻿ ﻿… hm, well, perhaps not.”

“Perhaps not what, Hilary?”

“Hm. I was thinking of Eve seeing the thing in tomorrow morning’s papers. She only reads one wretched picture-paper, and that’s Pollen’s, so I thought, hm, that if we asked him not to.﻿ ﻿…”

“Eve, the poor darling!” Iris whispered. We seemed to be in a desert, three shadows of men, three shadows of voices, and Iris, very white and alight. That is how I always remember her, alight.

“No good, Hilary,” Guy was murmuring. “He won’t, because it’s what those fellows call News. And if you try you will only upset young Venice and make her perhaps feel she’s in the other camp, rather the wrong camp for her, she might think, and just as she’s marrying Naps. She’s a good girl, loyal as anything to her father﻿—and he’s a good fellow enough, but he’s got a queer complaint called Consistency. It’s something you make money out of, I think. I know him very well, as I’ve blackballed him from three clubs. My God, ever seen the man’s jaw?”

“She’s lovely, I thought,” Iris said.

“Good girl, Venice.﻿ ﻿…”

“Hell﻿ ﻿…” said Iris suddenly, breathlessly.

“What?” Hilary jumped.

“Only﻿ ﻿… hell is raving with millionaires with jaws like Mr. Pollen’s. I’ve dreamt, I know. People who snap ‘Yes’ and ‘No’ very brusquely and then stick to it, no matter what it is. This century likes them like that. Come along, my friend, come along!”

===Chinese===

“我倒有个主意，”希拉里用他那套专门用来陈述锦囊妙计的超然语调说道，“既然我们如今身在这间乌烟瘴气的夜总会里，倒不妨顺手做点善事。老波伦（old Pollen）眼下正在楼上，咱们或许能……哼，罢了，大抵行不通。”

“大抵怎么个行不通，希拉里？”

“哼。我是在盘算，明儿清早伊芙一见报纸定会瞧见这桩丑闻。她生平唯独只翻阅一份蹩脚的画报，而那份画报偏偏是波伦麾下的产业；于是我心想，哼，倘若咱们出面央求他网开一面不要登载……”

“伊芙，可怜的心肝宝贝儿！”艾丽丝低语道。我们四人仿佛置身于一片荒原瀚海之中：三道男人的黑影（3道黑影），三股虚无缥缈的嗓音，以及通体雪白、熠熠生辉的艾丽丝。在我的记忆中，她永远是这副模样——光华夺目。

“白费心机，希拉里，”盖伊轻声低语道，“他决计不会答应的，皆因在那帮报贩子眼里，这正是所谓的‘头条新闻’。倘若你贸然出面说情，只会平白惹恼年轻的威尼斯，教她觉得自己仿佛站在了对立阵营——而在她看来那无疑是个错误的阵营，尤其是在她即将嫁给纳普斯的大喜当口。那姑娘是个好孩子，对自家的老父亲孝顺忠诚至极；而老波伦人倒也着实不坏，但他身上患有一种名为‘始终如一顽固不化’的怪疾。依我看，那正是他发家致富的独门秘方。我对他的底细一清二楚，皆因我曾亲手在三家俱乐部（3家俱乐部）里投票将他拒之门外。老天爷在上，你们可曾端详过那汉子的下颌？”

“依我看，那姑娘美丽绝伦，”艾丽丝说道。

“威尼斯是个好姑娘……”

“地狱……”艾丽丝冷不丁气喘吁吁地说道。

“什么？”希拉里吓得一跳。

“我不过是想说……在地狱深渊里，到处充斥着生着波伦先生那般下颌的百万富翁。我曾在梦中得见，我心知肚明。那帮人嘴里干脆决绝地吐出‘是’与‘否’，随后无论对错便顽固执拗到底。当今这个世纪，偏偏最钟爱这般人物。走吧，我的朋友，快走吧！”

===Original===

And in a trice Iris and I were walking up the long passage which connects the Loyalty Club to the pavement of Pall Mall. On one side it is hung (but this is two years ago) with glass cases laden with fine cut jades and ambers, while small blue and green figures of animal men, human animals, and bestial gods will delight the eyes of Egyptologists: on the other the faces of beautiful women and children will testify to the photographic art of Sebastian Roeskin of Dover Street. Iris walked swiftly, heroically, her eyes intent before her, impersonal, utterly unselfconscious. The glaring lights in the passage lit her swiftly-moving green-and-silver shoes, or were they sandals with high heels? and so intent were the flippant silver-flashing ankles, briskly striding on, as though chiming the never-to-be-known marching song of a lady who must always meet men on their own ground.

She said: “You’ll be wondering how I came to dine with a man like Victor Duck. Well, I’ve been wondering myself. Poor Victor Duck. He has taken to caddishness like a drug, and he goes on increasing the doses. It’s almost fascinating to watch, just to see what inevitable things he will say next. And he said and did them all, every one, even to ‘Dear little girl’ and to ordering a private room. But I said I never dined in private rooms on Fridays.”

There was a group of tall young men at the entrance, maybe waiting for their women from the Cloakroom, maybe waiting for sirens to come to them from the night, maybe waiting for taxicabs, maybe only waiting for the next minute, as young men will. Admirably formal they looked, admirably toned to the dress-coats of Davies, the trousers of Anderson and Sheppard, the hats of Lock, the waistcoats of Hawes and Curtis, the ties of Budd. Handkerchiefs by Edouard and Butler. The glory to God. They looked furtively at Iris in the way that decent men will at a woman who is said to have had lovers, like cows at a bull. One of them said gloomily: “Might go to the Albert Hall Ball.”

===Chinese===

转瞬间，艾丽丝与我便穿行在连接忠诚俱乐部与蓓尔美尔街人行道的那条漫长通道之中。通道的一侧（这乃是两年前，2年前的光景）悬挂着满载精雕美玉与琥珀的玻璃陈列柜，其中那些由兽面人身、人面兽身以及兽性神祇构成的蓝绿小雕像定能教埃及学家们大饱眼福；而在另一侧，绝色佳人与纯真孩童的面庞则彰显着多佛尔街（Dover Street）塞巴斯蒂安·罗斯金（Sebastian Roeskin）卓绝的摄影技艺。艾丽丝步履迅捷、意气风发，那一双眼眸凝神直视前方，淡漠出尘，全无半点扭捏做作。通道里刺目的华灯映照着她那双疾步前行的翠绿配纯银色高跟鞋——抑或者那是一双高跟凉鞋？——那一双闪烁着银光的轻盈脚踝步履是如此专注决绝，大步流星向前迈进，仿佛正奏响着一支属于那位永远能在男人们自家阵地上与其分庭抗礼之贵妇的、旁人永远无从知晓的行军曲。

她说道：“你大概在纳闷我究竟为何会同维克多·达克（Victor Duck）这号人共进晚餐吧。实不相瞒，我自己也纳闷得很呢。可怜的维克多·达克。他把下流无赖行径当成麻醉药一般嗜饮成瘾，而且剂量越服越大。瞧着他那副德性倒真教人叹为观止，只为瞧瞧他接下去究竟还会吐露出何等意料之中的陈词滥调。结果他当真把那些陈词滥调全说全做了一遍，样样不落，乃至连‘亲爱的小乖乖’这等肉麻称谓与预订私密包厢都用上了。但我正告他说，每逢星期五（周五），我向来从不在私密包厢里用膳。”

大门口伫立着一帮身材高挑的青年才俊，或许正等候着女伴自衣帽间款款而出，或许正期盼着夜色中能有绝色妖姬投怀送抱，或许正等候着出租车，抑或正如年轻人惯常所为的那般仅仅是在虚掷下一分钟的光阴。他们瞧上去体统极了，从戴维斯（Davies）的燕尾礼服、安德森与谢泼德（Anderson and Sheppard）的西裤、洛克（Lock）的礼帽、霍斯与柯蒂斯（Hawes and Curtis）的西装马甲，到巴德（Budd）的领带，周身搭配得严丝合缝无可挑剔。手帕则出自埃杜阿尔与巴特勒（Edouard and Butler）。荣耀归于上帝。他们偷偷摸摸地斜睨着艾丽丝，正如同体面正经男儿面对据说养过不少入幕之宾情郎的妇人家时惯常的目光一般，恰如母牛呆呆注视着公牛。其中一位郁郁寡欢地嘟囔道：“不妨去阿尔伯特音乐厅舞会（Albert Hall Ball）逛一遭。”

===Original===

Pall Mall seemed wrought of stately marble palaces, and Iris said that the reason why so many English people seemed to prefer Paris to London was that English people saw Paris mostly at night, while if they could see enough of London by night they would never leave it. “And the people!” she said. “All these years I’ve spent abroad, and never met any people so good, so decent, as the English. Couldn’t you sometimes kill people for the *quality* of their admiration? Oh, I’ve committed so many murders in foreign streets.﻿ ﻿…”

“But, if you like England﻿ ﻿… why are you going away? You’re free.﻿ ﻿…”

“Ah,” she mocked, and, as we walked, a hand darted out from her white cloak and touched my sleeve, and startled me very much. “Wait till you’re so free that you just daren’t do what you like. Wait till you’re so free that you can be here one minute and there another. Wait till you’re so free that you can see the four walls of your freedom and the iron-barred door that will let you out into the open air of slavery, if only there was someone to open it. Ah, yes, freedom.﻿ ﻿…”

Then up the street of ghostly dandies we flew behind the silver stork, and the wind rushed down from Hampstead Heath and the wind ran out of Jermyn Street and jumped like a drunken man on the tawny cornstalks that were her hair, and waved them about and danced with them. But not she to notice, she who seemed to have a great talent for just not noticing things! She was silent, serious, intent. The light of an arc-lamp kissed the long slender legs into silver.

Once she turned to me, smiled, and looked away again. I wondered if she meant me to see that our friendship was in that smile. I hated her, I think, because she made me feel so incapable, unwise. As the stork, with scarcely a rustle of its wings, flew towards the Christian Science Chapel at the head of Half-Moon Street, she said: “I’m tired. All day seeing lawyers and trustees, and then taking sweet old Eve all round and round Selfridge’s because she had never been there before and someone had told her she could find everything she wanted there. And she was quite upset at being unfaithful to Harrod’s.﻿ ﻿… And Gerald! Oh, but why couldn’t they let Gerald alone! Just because, I suppose, the Marches are never let off anything.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

蓓尔美尔街仿佛全由巍峨庄严的大理石宫殿筑就而成，艾丽丝说道，如许多英国人之所以偏爱巴黎胜过伦敦，全因英国人大多只在深夜领略巴黎的风情，而倘若他们能在夜间好生端详一番伦敦的景致，便决计再也舍不得离开了。“还有这里的人民！”她说道，“这些年我流寓异国他乡，生平从未见过有哪个国度的人民像英国人这般善良淳朴、通情达理。可有时难道你就不曾因为旁人投向你的爱慕所蕴含的某种*品质*而动过杀心么？哦，在异国他乡的街头，我曾犯下过不知多少桩心底的凶杀案……”

“可是，既然你如此钟爱英格兰……为何还要离开呢？你是自由之身……”

“啊，”她自嘲地讥笑道；正当我们漫步之际，一只纤手自白色的斗篷下探将出来，轻轻触碰了一下我的衣袖，令我浑身猛然一惊。“等到哪天你自由到压根不敢随心所欲去行事的地步吧。等到哪天你自由到前一分钟尚在此处、后一分钟便身在彼处的地步吧。等到哪天你自由到能清清楚楚看清自家庭‘自由’的四面高墙（4面高墙），看清那扇只要有人肯施以援手替你开锁便能将你放逐进奴役之清新空气中的铁栅大门吧。啊，不错，自由……”

随后，紧随在那头翱翔银鹳身后，我们飞驰穿过那条游荡着纨绔子弟幽魂的街巷；长风自汉普斯特德荒原（Hampstead Heath）呼啸而下，自哲敏街猛冲而出，宛如一个酩酊大醉的汉子猛然扑向她那一头宛若黄褐色玉米秆般的秀发，肆意摆弄拨弄、同发丝狂乱起舞。然而她却浑然未觉——她似乎天生自有一种对周遭一切皆视若无睹的超凡本领！她沉默不语，神色凝重，全神贯注。弧光灯的冷光洒在她修长纤细的双腿上，将其吻成了一抹白银。

有那么一回，她转过头来望向我，莞尔一笑，随即又移开了视线。我心下暗忖，她莫非是想让我自那一抹微笑中窥见我们之间的情谊。我想，我当时是恨她的，皆因她让我觉得自己显得是如此无能、如此愚顽。正当那头银鹳几乎未曾扇动双翼、便径自飞向半月街尽头的基督教科学派礼拜堂（Christian Science Chapel）之际，她开口道：“我累极了。整整一天都在会见各路律师与信托管理人，随后又领着可亲的老伊芙把塞尔福里奇百货（Selfridge’s）从头到脚逛了个遍，全因她生平从未涉足过那里，且有人向她打包票说在那里能买到她心仪的一切物事。结果老太太因为背叛了哈罗德百货（Harrod’s）而懊恼了一整天……还有杰拉尔德！哦，他们为何就不能放过杰拉尔德呢！我想，纯粹是因为马奇家族的人向来从不被命运宽恕放过任何劫难吧……”

===Original===

“Here we are,” I said, and she pulled up beneath the lamp by The Leather Butler in East Chapel Street. From the footboard a lane of low houses and shops stretched in a vague, squalid line towards the open Market Place at one end and the darkness of the mews at the other; somehow like an etching in a clouded light by an uncertain hand. Bits of newspapers and torn placards, the nameless odours of yesterday’s economies. The wind that came from Hampstead Heath could find no way into Shepherd’s Market, and it lay still as a tramp sleeping. Cats watched us intently from the middle distance, and a striped cat leapt with a scream from the shadow of the door of my old house. Gerald’s light was on. “What’s that mean?” Iris whispered. She seemed to be frightened, and she said sharply: “Why are you looking at me like that?”

“I was just thinking,” I told her, “that if one could judge by appearances, which of course one must never do, in that white cloak in this mean lane you look as nearly an angel as this world could ever see.”

“Don’t let’s mock the angels. What does it mean, Gerald’s light being on?”

“Only what it has always meant, that I must turn it out.”

“Ah, you’ve been very good to Gerald.﻿ ﻿…”

And I am glad that, just then, I said that I was very fond of Gerald.

Then we were on the narrow landing of my old flat, in the darkness. The musty stillness of that little old house brought six years of nights into my mind, and I wondered how people ever regretted their first youth, those intolerable uncertainties and enthusiasms that stare at you from the dead past like condemned gargoyles. The incapability of youth goes on long enough, Heaven knows, if not so long as the savagery of childishness. In the darkness I could feel the soft ermine of her cloak against me, and that faint dry scent whose name I shall now never know. She was very, very still, and I could not even hear her breathing.

===Chinese===

“我们到了，”我说道，于是她将车停靠在东礼拜堂街“皮男仆”酒馆门旁的街灯下。自踏脚板望去，一条由低矮瓦舍与店铺交织而成的阴暗小巷，以一种模糊污秽的线条绵延伸展，一端通向开阔的集市广场，另一端则没入马房的幽暗深渊之中；不知怎的，那景致宛如一幅由一双颤抖犹豫的巧手在昏暗天光下勾勒出的铜版蚀刻画。散落的报纸碎屑与残破的招贴标语，空气中弥漫着昨日贫寒度日的无名馊气。自汉普斯特德荒原刮来的长风根本寻不见钻入牧羊人集市的缝隙，整个小巷沉寂得宛如一个正自熟睡的流浪汉。远处的野猫们定定注视着我们，一只条纹花猫伴着一声刺耳的尖叫自我的旧寓所门影下猛然蹿出。杰拉尔德窗前的灯火依然亮着。“那代表着什么？”艾丽丝低语道。她似乎有些惊恐，厉声发问道：“你为何用这般眼神打量着我？”

“我刚才不过是在暗自忖度，”我正告她道，“倘若凡人能够以貌取人——当然世人向来万万不可这般行事——在这条逼仄破败的小巷里，披着那袭雪白斗篷的你，瞧上去真真切切是这尘世间所能目睹最接近天使的模样了。”

“咱们休要拿天使打趣取笑。杰拉尔德窗前灯火依然亮着，究竟是何名堂？”

“无非代表着它一贯的含义：我必须上去替他把灯关了。”

“啊，你待杰拉尔德当真太好啦……”

而我由衷庆幸，自家彼时恰巧吐露了一句：我打心底里极其偏爱杰拉尔德。

随后，我们便双双伫立在我旧寓所那逼仄黑暗的楼梯转角平台上。那座破旧小屋散发出的霉烂死寂，教我心头冷不丁浮现出在此度过的整整六年（6年）宵夜岁月，我不由得纳闷世人究竟为何会对最初的青春年华依依不舍——那些令人不堪忍受的彷徨迷惘与盲目狂热，宛如遭受天谴的石雕滴水兽一般，自死寂的往昔岁月中死死凝视着你。苍天在上，青春年华的软弱无能往往绵延良久，若非同孩童天性的野蛮残忍一般漫长的话。在幽暗的阴影中，我能真切感受到她斗篷上柔嫩白鼬皮贴在身上的触感，以及那缕其名目我至今亦无从知晓的淡淡干燥馨香。她是那般沉寂、那般纹丝不动，我甚至连她的一丝吐息都未曾听闻。

===Original===

“It is very kind of you to come with me,” she said suddenly, seriously. We were very still on that landing, and I drew back my arm where it touched her cloak. It was very soft, that cloak. “I have thought of you, and decided that if you ever thought of me you had a right to think with dislike.﻿ ﻿…” She was talking smoothly, calmly, when suddenly her voice completely broke, into little bits. “Oh!” she whispered. I was silent. She said quickly: “To me there’s something terribly indecent about humanity, all humanity. It’s as though, in the whole lovely universe, humanity was cooped in this musty little house, talking vaguely of dislike, eternally talking of like and dislike, love and unlove, of doings and undoings, purposeless yet striving and savage. The other night I was motoring alone from Paris to Calais, and it seemed to me that no law was strong enough, no crime was big enough, not even disloyalty, to stop us, when we had the chance of rising above the beastly limitation of living as we were born to live. Because we humans are not born to live, we are born to die.﻿ ﻿…”

“Something has happened to you tonight,” I said. She was a faint white shape in the darkness, and it seemed to me that that was as much of her as I should ever see; and I was right.

“No, nothing at all. Just a dream. But, oh, failing the dream, how I would like a child!”

“A dream-child!”

“Ah, I’ve had those, a many! No, a real one. To be playmates with.﻿ ﻿…”

I said: “I will go up first tonight and see how Gerald is. Will you wait here?”

“I’m tired and frightened,” she said faintly. “Don’t be long.”

I don’t think I stayed up there more than a few seconds. I don’t know. I switched out the light, and as I went down the dark narrow stairs I did not strike a match.

“Well?” she whispered from the darkness.

I don’t know what I said. I suppose I must have said that he was in the same state as when she had seen him before. Then I pretended I had no matches left, and said I had better go down first while she held on to my shoulder. “Then if you fall, I’ll fall,” she complained, but I said I would not fall.

===Chinese===

“承蒙你肯陪我一道前来，委实太感激你了，”她冷不丁神色严肃地说道。我们在转角平台上伫立得极静极沉，我轻轻缩回了触碰到她斗篷的手臂。那袭斗篷委实柔软极了。“我曾念及过你，且认定倘若你哪天念及我，你完全有权对我深恶痛绝……”她原本语调平缓从容，冷不丁，她的嗓音彻底哽咽碎裂成了一片片残丝断屑。“哦！”她低语道。我默不作声。她语速飞快地说道：“于我而言，整个人类身上皆有一种骇人听闻的不知廉耻。仿佛在整个辽阔美妙的宇宙之中，人类偏偏被生生幽禁在这座发霉的逼仄陋室里，含混不清地谈论着厌恶，永无休止地咀嚼着喜欢与厌恶、爱与不爱、作为与弃绝，全无终极旨归，却偏偏拼死挣扎撕咬野蛮成性。前几天夜里我独自一人驱车自巴黎开往加来（Calais），在我看来，当我们有幸得以超越那‘生来便浑浑噩噩苟活’的野兽般局限时，世间断没有任何律法足够强大、没有任何罪愆足够庞大——乃至背叛不忠亦不足道——能拦阻我们超越自我。皆因我们人类生来本就不是为了苟活，我们生来乃是为了赴死……”

“今晚你身上定然生出了什么变故，”我说道。她在幽暗中不过是一道模糊微弱的雪白轮廓，而我只觉得，这大抵便是我此生所能见到的她的全部了；而事实证明，我所料丝毫不差。

“不，什么变故都没有。不过是一场幻梦罢了。可是，哦，倘若幻梦终归难圆，我该是何等渴望能有一个孩子啊！”

“一个梦中的孩子么！”

“啊，此类梦中孩童，我见识得太多太多了！不，是一个真真切切的活骨肉。能做个玩伴……”

我说道：“今夜且由我先上去探探杰拉尔德的动静。你在此处稍候片刻可好？”

“我又累又怕，”她虚弱地低语道，“切莫耽搁太久。”

我想我在楼上停留了大抵绝不超过几秒钟。我记不大清了。我熄灭了电灯，顺着阴暗狭窄的楼梯向下走去时，我甚至未曾擦亮一根火柴。

“怎么样啦？”她隔着黑暗低声探问道。

我记不清自家究竟说了些什么。我料想自己当时定是借口说杰拉尔德眼下的光景同她先前所见的烂醉模样一般无二。随后我假装自家手头再无多余火柴，便说最好由我在前头引路下楼，教她攀住我的肩头前行。“那倘若你失足摔下去，我也得跟着栽下去了，”她埋怨道，可我正告她说我决计不会摔倒。

===Original===

Stair by stair we slowly descended in the darkness. I wanted particularly to see Guy. There were certain things to be done, I supposed. My mind was vacant as a plate on which was drawn a confused picture that would, on looking closely, mean something horrible. There had been a stain on the wall, a great jagged dripping stain, and bits of hair sticking to it.

“Oh, God, this drink!” she said frantically; then almost sobbed: “What’s that!” But it was only the telephone-bell from the hall downstairs, queerly strident and unrestrained in that still, musty little house. Brrr! Brrr! Brrr!﻿ ﻿… “I never knew a telephone could be so shrill! Will it be someone for Gerald?”

“It will ring forever if I don’t answer it,” I said, opening the door into the lane. “I’ll follow you to the car.” I hoped it was Guy ringing up on the chance of catching us.

“Well?” his cold murmur came through the night. He said he would meet me at my door in ten minutes’ time. “What are you doing about Iris?” he asked me and I think I said: “Nothing. What can I do?”

Iris was waiting by her car under the lamp. The car was like a great yellow beast with shining scales, and Iris, tall and gentle and white, the lovely princess of the tale who has enslaved the beast. Far above them towered the pile of Sunderland House, enchanted almost into dignity by the darkness. She looked at me gravely as I came, she seemed to crouch like a tired fairy into her white cloak.

“You look very white,” she said.

“Now, Lady Pynte!” I made to mock her, and I suppose we laughed. Then she was at the wheel, sunk into the low seat, staring up at the darkness of the faint London stars. “I’m tired,” she said again, and again I thought, what could I do? Then she did something to the dashboard with her left hand, and the engine hummed. I was on the curb, above her. Nearby a policeman was flashing his lamp on a door. I supposed one told the police.﻿ ﻿…

“Will you see Gerald in the morning?” the slightly husky voice just reached me. “And tell him to follow me to Paris? I shall be at number﻿—Avenue du Bois for a week or so, and then﻿ ﻿… Goodbye,” she said sharply, as though impatient with herself. “Goodbye, dear. You’ve been very kind﻿—to the twin Marches. Goodbye﻿ ﻿… perhaps for a long time. You have your work in England, and I’m the slave of freedom. Goodbye, my friend.”

===Chinese===

一步接着一步，我们在幽暗中缓缓拾级而下。我迫不及待渴盼着见到盖伊。我想，有些后事必须着手料理了。我的脑海中一片空白，宛如一只绘着杂乱图景的磁盘，细看之下内里分明昭示着某种可怖的大祸。墙壁上原本留有一摊血迹，一摊犬牙交错、向下淌滴的污秽血迹，上面还粘着几绺发丝。

“哦，上帝啊，这该死的黄汤！”她发疯似地叫喊道；随后近乎抽泣起来：“那是什么声响！”然而那不过是楼下门厅里的电话铃声，在那座死寂陈腐的小楼里显得异常刺耳放肆。铃……铃……铃……“我生平从不知道电话铃声竟能这般刺耳尖锐！莫非是有人找杰拉尔德？”

“倘若我不去接听，它准会没完没了响个不停，”我一边说着，一边推开通往小巷的大门。“你且先去车旁，我随后便来。”我暗自企盼那正是盖伊碰运气打来找寻我们的电话。

“怎么样啦？”他冰冷低沉的嗓音穿透夜色传来。他说他会在十分钟内（10分钟内）赶到我的寓所大门前同我汇合。“关于艾丽丝，你打算如何安置？”他追问我，而我记得自家当时应道：“什么也没做。我又能做得了什么呢？”

艾丽丝正伫立在街灯下的跑车旁静候着。那辆跑车如同一头披挂着闪亮鳞甲的明黄色巨兽，而身材高挑、温存娴静、周身雪白的艾丽丝，宛如神话中驯服了恶兽的绝美公主。在他们头顶遥远的高处，耸立着桑德兰大厦（Sunderland House）巍峨的轮廓，被夜色施了魔法般凭添了几分庄严尊贵。见我走来，她神色凝重地端详着我，整个人宛如一个精疲力竭的仙子般蜷缩在雪白的斗篷里。

“你脸色煞白得很，”她说道。

“哎呀，佩恩特夫人附体啦！”我故意打趣揶揄道，想来我们当时大抵皆笑了一笑。随后她便坐回了驾驶座上，身子深陷在低矮的座椅里，仰头凝视着伦敦微弱星光背后的无尽幽暗。“我累极了，”她再次低语道，而我心头再次暗忖，我又能为她做得了什么呢？随后她用左手在仪表盘上拨弄了一番，引擎便低沉地轰鸣起来。我伫立在街沿的人行道上，居高临下望着她。不远处一名巡警正拿着手电筒照向一扇大门。我想凡人终归是要向警方报案的……

“明儿清早你会去看看杰拉尔德么？”略带沙哑的嗓音依稀飘进我的耳畔。“告诉他随后动身来巴黎（Paris）同我汇合好么？我大约会在森林大道（Avenue du Bois）……号逗留一周左右，随后便……再会啦，”她急促地说道，语气仿佛在同自家赌气生厌一般。“再会了，亲爱的。你待马奇家的这对孪生兄妹……当真太好了。再会……或许有很长一段时间无缘相见了。你在英格兰自有你的锦绣事业，而我不过是自由的囚徒奴隶。再会了，我的朋友。”

===Original===

I could not tell her just then. She lay aslant in the driving seat, and her tawny curls flamed in the light, and she looked sad and tired. I could not tell her, and as she took her hand from mine the great car leapt down the fat little slope of East Chapel Street to the end, turned in a blaze of light and colour, rushed up the parallel little street to Curzon Street.

I was at the corner where I had last seen Gerald putting his shoulder against the saloon-door of The Leather Butler; and as Iris’s car turned into Curzon Street a two-seater passed me swiftly, going the same way. I thought I heard a cry of “Iris!” above the rustle of the two engines, and I thought I heard Iris’s surprised voice, and the rear-lights of the two cars seemed to draw together, but I was not sure.

I crossed towards Queen Street, sure only that I wished to see Guy. From Jolley’s corner I saw, far up, two red rear-lights twisting into South Audley Street, and then, from afar, came the scream of a Klaxon, the growl of a horn. I wondered who was in the two-seater, but at that moment the tall figure of Guy came towards me from my door, where a taxi had just dropped him. “Sorry,” he murmured. “Poor young devil. Only hope the other side won’t disappoint him as much.”

“I couldn’t tell her,” I said.

Guy smoked thoughtfully, looking over my head. “I’ll tell her,” he said, “in the morning. Had an idea he might blow his brains out.”

===Chinese===

在那一当口，我实实无法将真相和盘托出。她斜倚在驾驶座上，黄褐色的卷发在灯光下如烈焰般闪烁，瞧上去显得是如此哀伤疲惫。我无法向她吐露实情；正当她将手自我的手心抽离之际，巨大的跑车猛然自东礼拜堂街肥硕平缓的小斜坡上一跃而下冲向尽头，在一片耀眼夺目的光影与色彩中悍然转弯，呼啸着冲上平行的狭窄街巷直奔寇松街（Curzon Street）。

我正伫立在先前最后一次目睹杰拉尔德用肩膀顶开“皮男仆”酒馆雅座大门的拐角处；当艾丽丝的跑车拐入寇松街时，一辆双座敞篷车（1辆双座跑车）自我的身旁飞驰而过，同向疾驰追去。在两台引擎（2台引擎）的轰鸣呼啸声之上，我隐约听见了一声“艾丽丝！”的呼喊，又隐约听见艾丽丝惊诧的回应，而两辆轿车尾部的红灯（2辆车的尾灯）似乎正急剧贴近聚拢，但我不能笃定。

我穿过马路朝着皇后街（Queen Street）走去，心头唯一确信的便是渴盼立刻见到盖伊。自乔利药房（Jolley’s）拐角处，我望见在极远的高处，两盏红色的车尾灯（2盏红灯）拐入了南奥德利街（South Audley Street），随后自遥远处传来了汽车电喇叭的尖锐鸣叫与刺耳长啸。我暗忖那辆双座跑车里坐着的究竟是何方神圣，而恰在那一刹那，刚被出租车送至寓所门前的盖伊那高大挺拔的身影正迎面朝我大步走来。“真令人痛心，”他低语道，“可怜的年轻冤家。唯愿黄泉之下的另一端，不至于像阳世这般教他大失所望才好。”

“我实在没法当面向她吐露实情，”我说道。

盖伊若有所思地吸着烟，目光越过我的头顶望向虚空。“待到明儿清早，”他说道，“我亲自告诉她吧。我心底早有预感，料到他终会饮弹自尽轰掉自家的脑袋。”

## V / 第五章 幽暗的信笺
===Original===

The Dark Letter

===Chinese===

幽暗的信笺

### I / 第一节
===Original===

On a bitter afternoon in the last week of January of the year 1923 the writer found himself in the Place Vendôme in Paris.

Now here, in the Place Vendôme, is material ready to the hand of the as yet undiscovered chronicler of lofty frivolities: such, unfortunately, as am not. But I can, at least, count up to fifty. There were forty-eight motorcars in the Place Vendôme, and one coach-and-six.

The Place Vendôme is a paradox in grey stone. Spacious, noble, monumental, it is cast, even at the stranger’s first glance, in an everlasting mould. The Place Vendôme is, without a doubt, one among the few things about which we may say with certainty: “That will last.” And yet, monumental and everlasting though it is, what do we find in the Place Vendôme? Do we find therein the practice of the seven arts, the learning of the nine humanities, the study of any one among the august array of sciences, nay, the application of any one among the Ten Commandments? We do not. We find forty-eight motorcars and one coach-and-six. We find that it is given over only to the frivolities of the trivial of two worlds and to every sort of “high-minded depravity” that may occur to the enfeebled wits of the exquisite. We find, in other words, that the Place Vendôme is the centre of that floating population of a few thousand dressing-tables, sables and Cachets Faivre of which, under the lofty title of *l’aristocratie internationale*, the Chevalier Gilulio di Risotto is the ultimate servitor. The Place Vendôme is, therefore, no place for a plain man, nor by any means a safe station for the man in the street: there are motorcars kept in readiness to run them over.

Across the Place, from the rue de la Paix to the rue de Castiglione, dash forever the nimble green Citroën taxicabs; whilst from the rue de Castiglione to the rue de la Paix will march the Renaults de luxe with scarlet wheels, passing in a fancy of cool brown eyes and the *poudre à la maréchale* of Bourbon days. Here and there among them, maybe, will flash the racing Bugattis of the dark young men a gigolo, a *rastacouère*, a “racingman.” They will come to no good.

===Chinese===

1923年1月最后一周的一个阴冷下午，笔者置身于巴黎的旺多姆广场。

如今，在这旺多姆广场上，到处都是尚未被发现的记录高雅轻浮之士的现成素材：不幸的是，笔者并不是这样的人。但至少我能数到五十。旺多姆广场上有四十八辆机动车，以及一辆六马大马车。

旺多姆广场是一个灰石构成的悖论。宽阔、雄伟、壮观，即便初来乍到的陌生人一眼望去，它也仿若由不朽的模具浇铸而成。毫无疑问，旺多姆广场是少数几样我们可以确信地说“那将永存”的事物之一。然而，尽管它巍峨耸立且历久不衰，我们在旺多姆广场上又发现了什么？我们可曾在此发现七艺的践行、九门人文学科的研修、对任何一门庄严学科的钻研，抑或是十诫中任何一条的奉行？并没有。我们发现的是四十八辆汽车和一辆六马大马车。我们发现这里完全沦为了来自两个世界的微不足道之徒的浮华享乐场，沦为了精巧之士虚弱智识中所能想出的各种“高尚堕落”。换句话说，我们发现旺多姆广场是漂泊人口的聚集中心，由数千张梳妆台、紫貂皮草和法夫尔止痛片构成，而在“国际贵族圈”的崇高头衔之下，朱利奥·迪·里索托骑士则是其最极端的侍从。因此，旺多姆广场绝非普通人该来的地方，也绝非街头过客的安全驻足点：因为随时都有汽车准备将他们碾过。

横穿广场，从和平街到卡斯蒂利奥内街，轻捷的绿色雪铁龙出租车永远在飞驰；而从卡斯蒂利奥内街到和平街，配有朱红色轮毂的豪华雷诺汽车则缓缓驶过，掠过一抹清冷棕眸的幻想与波旁王朝时代的元帅香粉。其间或许偶尔会闪过黑发年轻人的布加迪赛车——他们是男舞伴、南美暴发户，或是“赛车手”。他们绝不会有好下场。

===Original===

And always the great column on which Napoleon stands rises to the clouds, but no one cares about that. All they care about are the forty-eight automobiles and one coach-and-six which stretch, in ordered array of two lines, from the foot of his column to the entrance of the Ritz. The shops are loaded with diamonds as large as carnations and with carnations as expensive as diamonds. The shopkeepers are very polite, and courteously do not mind how many you buy. Americans buy. Englishmen watch the Americans buying. Grand Dukes wait for the Englishmen to dare them to have a cocktail. A few Frenchmen are stationed at those strategic points where they can best be rude to the English and Americans. Then the English and Americans tip them. The women do not wear stays, and insist on their men shaving twice a day.

“Well, at last!” sighed my sister, as her car, colourless with dust, was added to the forty-eight. I had been in Europe for four months, had lately joined some friends at Cannes, had chanced on my sister there, and had motored back with her to Paris. We were foul with dust, numbed with cold, aching with tiredness, and this was because we had “done” the six hundred odd miles from Cannes in two days and a few hours. The devil was in it if there was any reason why we should not have taken three days, or four, or five. But, then, why do people say “phone” for “telephone”? Thus, they get an illusion of speed.

We went into the hotel. The long, narrow, crimson lounge was crowded with tea-drinkers. “But what a crowd of women!” said my sister. But there were quite a few people in men’s clothes. *Au Réception.*

“I-want-a-room-and-bathroom-please,” my sister said.

“Madame?” The dark-suited gentlemen of the *Réception* looked up from their desks at my sister, saw that her clothes were not bad and that she was in a hurry, and looked away again.

My sister repeated herself, in that dead and faintly aggressive tone in which women ask for what is very probably going to be denied them. “I wired,” she added. Liar.

I went towards the concierge’s box. He was a nice man, and had a white imperial.

“Is Mrs. Storm staying in the hotel?”

“Sir?”

“Could you tell me if Mrs. Storm is﻿—”

“No, sir, no, sir. Not at present, sir.”

“I thought that, as her car was outside.﻿ ﻿… A yellow Hispano.”

“That is so, sir. *Parfaitement. L’Hispano jaune.*”

===Chinese===

拿破仑伫立其上的巨大圆柱始终耸入云霄，但无人关心这一点。大家关心的只有那四十八辆汽车和一辆六马大马车，它们排成两列整齐的队伍，从圆柱脚下一直延伸到丽兹酒店门前。商店里摆满了像康乃馨一样大的钻石，以及像钻石一样昂贵的康乃馨。店主们彬彬有礼，谦逊地不在乎你买多少。美国人买。英国人看着美国人买。大公们等着英国人怂恿他们喝一杯鸡尾酒。少数法国人驻守在最容易对英美客人傲慢无礼的战略要冲。随后英美客人给他们小费。女人们不穿紧身胸衣，并坚决要求她们的男伴每天刮两次胡子。

“哎，总算到了！”我姐姐叹道，她那辆沾满尘土、看不出颜色的车也加入到了那四十八辆车之中。我已经在欧洲待了四个月，最近去戛纳与一些朋友会合，在那儿偶遇了我姐姐，便坐她的车一起开回巴黎。我们浑身是尘，冻得发僵，累得浑身酸痛，这是因为我们在两天零几个小时里“跑”完了从戛纳出发的六百多英里路程。要说我们为什么不花三天、四天或五天走完，那简直见鬼了。不过，话又说回来，人们为什么把“telephone”简称为“phone”呢？如此一来，他们便获得了速度的幻觉。

我们走进酒店。狭长而深红的休息厅里挤满了喝下午茶的人。“怎么全是女人！”我姐姐说。不过穿着男装的人也相当不少。在前台。

“请给我一间带浴室的房间，”我姐姐说。

“女士？”前台穿深色西装的先生们从柜台后抬头看了看我姐姐，看出她的衣着不错，而且正赶时间，便又把目光移开了。

我姐姐又重复了一遍，用的是那种女人在索要极可能会被拒绝之物时特有的生硬而略带攻击性的语调。“我发过电报，”她补充道。骗子。

我走向礼宾台。礼宾是个和气的人，蓄着一撮白色的帝国式胡须。

“斯托姆夫人住在酒店里吗？”

“先生？”

“你能否告诉我斯托姆夫人是否——”

“没有，先生，没有。目前没有，先生。”

“我想着既然她的车停在外面。……一辆黄色伊斯帕诺。”

“确实如此，先生。一点没错。那辆黄色伊斯帕诺。”

===Original===

“But Mrs. Storm, you say, is not in the hotel?”

“No, sir. Not at present, sir.”

“Then, perhaps you may know, she has sold or lent her car to someone?”

“That is so, sir. Madame *a prêté l’Hispano. Merci, monsieur.*”

“You couldn’t possibly give me any idea of Mrs. Storm’s present address?”

“*Pardon, monsieur.﻿ ﻿… Timbre, monseigneur? De quinze centimes, un. Merci, monseigneur. L’automobile à huit heures moins quart? Parfaitement, monseigneur.﻿ ﻿…* I have no instructions, sir. That was the gentleman to whom madame has lent her motor. *Le duc de Valaucourt.*”

“Thank you. But Mrs. Storm, you say, is in Paris?”

“Sir? *Je suis sans instructions, monsieur. Madame?*”

“What is it, what is it?” asked my sister.

“Nothing,” I said. “Got a room? Good. I am going to the Westminster, and I’ll come at half-past eight, shall I, and take you out to dinner?”

“Yes, but not here. It’s crowded with minor royalties that you can’t stand with your back to anyone except the orchestra. Larue?”

She had no sooner turned towards the lift than my name was cried in an agony of exultation. My sister says that my face as I started round was a face of fear.

“Only the other day,” cried Mr. Cherry-Marvel, exercising, with incredible perfection of gesture, his eyes, shoulders, hands, wrist, beautiful teeth, tiepin and handkerchief, “we were talking about you.﻿ ﻿…” But it was ever one of Mr. Cherry-Marvel’s many social charms that, the instant he saw you after an absence, he would make it his business to give you the impression that people had been interested in nothing else but you during your absence. Not, of course, that he stopped there; he had other things to say, too. “Of course what I *really* must tell you first of all, is that Henri Daverelle, whom, of course, you know as well as I do, was saying to me only the other day, apropos of something which I am positive that you, with your sort of mind, will appreciate at its full value.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“但你说斯托姆夫人不在酒店里？”

“是的，先生。目前不在，先生。”

“那么，也许你知道，她是把车卖了还是借给别人了？”

“正是如此，先生。夫人把伊斯帕诺借出去了。谢谢您，先生。”

“你无论如何也不能透露一下斯托姆夫人目前的住址吗？”

“对不起，先生。……要邮票吗，阁下？十五生丁的一枚。谢谢您，阁下。汽车差一刻钟八点到？一点没错，阁下。……我没有接到指示，先生。刚才那位就是夫人把汽车借给他的绅士。瓦洛库尔公爵。”

“谢谢。但你说斯托姆夫人在巴黎？”

“先生？我没有奉令透露，先生。夫人有什么事？”

“怎么了，怎么了？”我姐姐问。

“没什么，”我说，“拿到房间了吗？太好了。我去威斯敏斯特酒店，八点半来接你去吃晚饭，好吗？”

“好，但别在这儿吃。这儿挤满了小贵族，除了对着乐队，你背对着谁都不礼貌。去拉吕餐厅？”

她刚转向电梯，我的名字就被一声极其狂喜的呼喊叫了出来。我姐姐后来说，我猛然回头时的脸色是一副受惊的模样。

“就在前几天，”切里-马维尔先生喊道，以令人难以置信的完美姿态活动着他的眼睛、肩膀、双手、手腕、美丽的牙齿、领带针和手帕，“我们还在谈论你呢。……”然而，每次久别重逢，切里-马维尔先生的诸多社交魅力之一，便是立即让你觉得在你离开的这段时间里，人们除了你之外什么都没关心。当然，他并不仅限于此；他还有别的话要说。“当然，我首先真正必须告诉你的是，亨利·达夫雷尔——你当然和我一样对他再熟悉不过了——就在前几天对我说起一件事，我相信凭你的这种心智，一定会充分领略它的价值。……”

===Original===

Cherry-Marvel was an artist enslaved by his art: he could not see you but he and you must instantly fall under its dominion; for it was an art too perfectly modulated to admit of hurry, it was an art too sensitive to admit of interruption. Indeed, a wicked little gleam would flash across his wicked old eyes if you so much as made to interrupt him. Pitiless to himself, he was only the less pitiless to you in so far as you were not himself; and, should you be a boor and leave him suddenly, you might hear the dry, clear voice dying in the distance, but dying hard, rising and falling to the fullest and most pregnant sense of each period; for his, you understand, was an art not of selection but of detail, and must always and be continually expending itself.﻿ ﻿…

“Ava Mainwaring, whom, of course, you know as well as I do, was saying to me only the other day, apropos of something which I am positive that you, with your sort of mind.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

切里-马维尔是一位沦为其艺术奴隶的艺术家：只要一见到你，你和他便必须立刻臣服于其支配之下；因为那是一门过于讲究节奏、容不得半点仓促的艺术，那是一门过于敏锐、容不得任何打断的艺术。确实，哪怕你稍有打断他的意图，他那双狡黠的老眼里就会闪过一丝恶意的微光。他对自己毫不留情，对你也只是因为你不是他本人而稍显宽容；倘若你举止粗鲁、陡然离去，你或许会听到那干涩明亮的声音在远处逐渐消散，但它消退得极其顽强，起伏顿挫，将每一个句段最充分、最耐人寻味的含义表达得淋漓尽致；因为你要明白，他的艺术不是删繁就简，而是精雕细琢，必须时刻不断地宣泄自身。……
