“艾娃·梅因沃林——你当然和我一样对她再熟悉不过了——就在前几天对我说起一件事，我相信凭你的这种心智。……”

===Original===

Essentially an aristocrat, in person dainty, neat, fastidious, Cherry-Marvel’s art was essentially democratic, for it abhorred all limitations and exacted from him its complete display on every occasion, whether lofty, literary, or plebeian, which came before his relentlessly alert eyes; and you can hear, through the last sixty years of English social history, the rise and fall of Cherry-Marvel’s voice, each word dropping on a stunned silence like a long-polished jewel. Eager, exquisite, always prepared, always with a handkerchief fluttering between his breast-pocket and the corner of his eye, you must imagine him against the tapestry of wasted time, a figure of ancient, aesthetic *dandysme*, on immaculate lawns, in drawing-rooms, up and down terraces of palazzos, in clubs and cabarets. You might enter a spacious drawing-room in Rome, a museum in Naples, a friend’s villa in Capri, you might stray from your boat in a South Sea lagoon into the smoking-room of the hotel, you might steal a moment from your companions to see the moonlight on the Pyramid.﻿ ﻿… Oh, you might be anywhere, and suddenly you would hear that voice, rising and falling, relentless, ageless, enchanting even lions to silence, with here and there a sudden, profound drawl on one word, any word, “de‑ar,” and you would, fascinated, be compelled to face him﻿—there, with full pale lips drawn wide apart, wicked blue eyes absorbed with cunning ecstasy in your stunned attention, the while, infinite as fate, he joined together the perfected pieces of his art with the word “whereupon,” which lounged from his tongue in a crescendo to a cry of sadic exaltation. And while you laughed at some elaborately phrased conceit, wondering how he had remembered the order of the words so well, he would watch the effect of his art with kind, cunning eyes, one wrist suspended in the air, his handkerchief fluttering towards the corner of his eye, in consummate politeness to show how he, too, by your laughter, was appreciating the full flavour of his art﻿ ﻿… “whereupon Elsa, who, by the way, had really a very amusing experience in Venice last Autumn, and one which I am positive that you, with your sort of mind.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

从本质上讲，切里-马维尔是一个贵族，其人娇小、整洁、挑剔，然而他的艺术在本质上却是民主的，因为它憎恶一切局限，并强求他在任何场合——无论是高雅的、文雅的还是平民化的——只要呈现在他那不知疲倦的警惕目光前，都要全盘展现；贯穿过去六十年的英国社交史，你都能听到切里-马维尔嗓音的起伏跌宕，每一个字音落在目瞪口呆的寂静中，犹如一颗历经长久磨砺的珠宝。热切、精妙、永远有备而来，胸袋与眼角之间永远拂动着一方手帕，你必须想象他在虚掷光阴的织锦背景下，在无可挑剔的草坪上、在客厅里、在宫殿的露台上下来回踱步、在俱乐部和歌舞表演厅里，宛如一位古老唯美主义花花公子的化身。你走进罗马宽敞的客厅，走进那不勒斯的博物馆，走进朋友在卡普里岛的别墅，你从南太平洋环礁湖的小船上走下来步入酒店的吸烟室，你从同伴身旁偷闲片刻去看金字塔上的月光。……噢，你可能身处任何地方，突然间你就会听到那个声音，起伏跌宕，毫不留情，不知老之将至，甚至令雄狮也静默倾听，其间不时在某个词、任何词上突然拖出长长的腔调，比如“亲——爱的”，你便会被其深深吸引，身不由己地面向他——就在那儿，丰满苍白的双唇大大张开，狡黠的蓝眼睛带着诡秘的狂喜沉浸于你目瞪口呆的专注之中，与此同时，如同命运般漫无边际，他用“随后”一词将他精妙绝伦的艺术碎片串联起来，这个词从他的舌尖漫不经心地滑出，渐强至一声施虐狂般的兴奋尖叫。当你为某句精心构思的妙语发笑，纳闷他怎能将字词的顺序记得如此分毫不差时，他便会用和善而狡黠的眼睛注视着自己艺术的效果，一只手腕悬在半空中，手帕拂向眼角，以极致的礼貌表明他自己也同样通过你的笑声品味着其艺术的全部韵味……“随后艾尔莎——顺便说一句，她去年秋天在威尼斯真有一段非常有趣的经历，我相信凭你的这种心智。……”

===Original===

Now, if anyone could tell me where Iris was to be found, Cherry-Marvel was that man. Cherry-Marvel knew, of course, everybody, and he knew everything about everybody﻿ ﻿… “of course it’s absurd to suppose that Alice, with her intelligence, which I am positive makes its full appeal to you﻿—it is absurd to suppose that Alice could for one *moment* have thought that her husband, whom of course you know as well as I do, would divorce her for going to Brighton with Cubby Tyrell, because, as I was pointing out to her sister only the other day, for one thing no decent man, and I am sure you will agree with me about this, would care to let it be known that his wife had ever gone to Brighton, and for another, and this, of course, is a Biblical detail which I am sure that you will grasp at once, Cubby Tyrell, who is a very intimate friend of mine, has been allowed, in spite of having been married twice, to remain a member of the Celibates Club.﻿ ﻿…”

At this time I hadn’t the remotest idea as to where Iris was or how she did. I had not seen her since the night of her brother’s death; and had been permitted to gather from Hilary that he knew as little as I did of her whereabouts. Secret she had always been in her absences, Hilary said, or, rather careless, but now she seemed positively in hiding.

She had, a few weeks after that terrible night, written me one long letter: from some place near Rome, from a draughty house, so she wrote, on a hill of strangled olives. There was no address on the notepaper, and this, she wrote, was because she did not want me or anyone to write to her. “Please,” she added to that.

Her letter was presumably in answer to two of mine addressed to the care of Mrs. Oden of Montpellier Square, but she was at the pains to excuse it on the ground that she and I were tied together﻿—“no, tied apart!”﻿—by a bond, the existence of which I would never, never know. Well! It was, you can see, a feverish, mysterious letter; and made how much more mysterious by that almost illegible, pencilled scrawl! There were whole sentences on the first few pages which I could not make out at all, which I made almost blind guesses at, while at some I could not even contrive so much.

===Chinese===

如今，如果有人能告诉我艾丽丝身在何处，切里-马维尔就是那个人。切里-马维尔自然认识每一个人，而且对每个人的底细了如指掌……“当然，认为艾丽丝凭借她的聪慧——我相信这种聪慧完全能打动你——认为艾丽丝有一瞬间会觉得她的丈夫（你当然和我一样对他再熟悉不过了）会因为她和库比·蒂雷尔去了布莱顿就跟她离婚，这简直是荒谬的；因为正如我前几天向她姐姐指出的那样，一方面，没有任何正派男士——我确信你在这点上会赞同我——愿意让人知道自己的妻子曾经去过布莱顿；另一方面，这当然是一个圣经般的细节，我相信你立刻就能明白，库比·蒂雷尔是我的一位挚友，尽管结过两次婚，却仍被允许保留单身汉俱乐部的会员资格。……”

当时我对艾丽丝身在何方、近况如何没有丝毫概念。自从她哥哥去世的那天晚上之后，我就再没见过她；而且从希拉里那里我得知，他对她的下落和我一样一无所知。希拉里说，在外出不归这件事上，她向来行踪隐秘，或者不如说是随性粗心，但眼下她似乎完全躲藏了起来。

在那个可怕的夜晚过去几周后，她曾给我写过一封长信：寄自罗马附近的某个地方，按她的信中所写，寄自一座长满受扼橄榄树的山丘上一处漏风的屋子。信纸上没有地址，信中写道，这是因为她不希望我或任何人给她回信。“求你了，”她在信末补充道。

她的信大概是回复我寄往蒙彼利埃广场奥登夫人转交的两封信，但她费尽周折以此作为辩解的理由：她和我被一种纽带连结在一起——“不，是被隔绝开来！”——而这种纽带的存在，我将永远、永远无法知晓。哎！你可以看出，这是一封狂乱而神秘的信；而那几乎难以辨认的铅笔潦草字迹，更让它显得神秘莫测！在前几页上，有些整句我根本无法看懂，几乎全凭瞎猜，有些句子我甚至连瞎猜都无从下手。

===Original===

“It is your fault, my friend. You paved the road up which I raced in chase of the Blue Bird. Yours was the appointed dark finger in the darkness. May God forgive you, for I can’t. I will try, but I think I can’t. There is a waterfall of fire.﻿ ﻿…”

Sheets upon sheets of it, that letter is before me now, and still I am unable to decipher whole sentences from that maze of pencil-marks on the thin Italian paper. There was one that stared at me, shocked me, in the middle of the second page﻿—“I may hate you”﻿—but I could not, do what I would, make out the words above or below.

“… I am lonely beyond bearing, and afraid. I am so afraid. I wonder, will you understand? But if I bore you take courage, for I will not bore you again. You are my friend, and this is my goodbye. Forgive me, dear, the arrogance of calling you my friend. But I am so afraid. *Et, satyr bien-aimé, j’ai raison.﻿ ﻿…*”

I could, you can understand, make neither head nor tail of it. She might hate﻿—me! She might, heaven knew, be indifferent to me, but why, how, hate? And *satyr bien-aimé* was all very well, but it meant nothing.

On the later pages she seemed to have controlled her hand a little, but her mind, if one might judge, remained﻿ ﻿… well, was that, perhaps, the effect on a mind of a draughty house on a hill of strangled olives? “I am lonely, but I have always been lonely since I was eighteen. Yes, I can trace my loneliness since then. It is a long time.”

===Chinese===

“这是你的错，我的朋友。是你铺就了道路，让我一路疾驰去追寻青鸟。你在黑暗中伸出了那根命定的黑暗手指。愿上帝宽恕你，因为我做不到。我会努力，但我想我做不到。那是一场烈火的瀑布。……”

信写了一页又一页，那封信此刻就摆在我的面前，可我依然无法从薄薄的意大利信纸上那一团乱麻般的铅笔印记中辨认出整句整句的话。在第二页的正中间，有一句话直勾勾地盯着我，让我震惊不已——“我可能会恨你”——但我无论如何也辨认不出上面或下面的字句。

“……我孤独得难以承受，而且害怕。我很害怕。不知你是否能够明白？但如果我惹你厌烦了，请振作起来，因为我不会再烦你了。你是我的朋友，这是我的告别。亲爱的，原谅我狂妄地称你为我的朋友。但我真的很害怕。而且，心爱的萨堤尔，我是对的。……”

你可以明白，对此我完全摸不着头脑。她可能会恨——我！天晓得，她可能对我漠不关心，但为什么、怎么会恨呢？而“心爱的萨堤尔”固然动听，却不知所云。

在后面的几页里，她的字迹似乎平复了一些，但依人看来，她的心绪依然……好吧，那或许就是长满受扼橄榄树的山丘上一间漏风的屋子对心绪产生的影响吗？“我很孤独，但自从我十八岁起，我就一直很孤独。是的，我可以从那时起追溯我的孤独。那是漫长的一段岁月了。”

===Original===

This letter, you must remember, came only a month after Gerald’s death. She wrote of that night, and here her haunted pencil was at its most firm, if that is saying anything. “There I stood in the old, old darkness﻿—how old darkness is, have you ever felt?﻿—while you were upstairs in Gerald’s room. And I listened, but I could not hear you moving, so I imagined you to be staring at Gerald from the door, as you and I did that night a million years ago, when, do you remember, you suddenly, strangely from your heart, made that defiant courtesy to my hand? And, do you know, I almost cried because of your kindness to that poor, helpless sweet. Oh, Hilary has told me about you, and you luring Gerald off to a Home, but all in vain, my poor Gerald. And then I heard you switch out the light, and down you came, slowly, slowly, more silent than the darkness, and when you spoke your voice was as old as the darkness. But you are very young really, else you couldn’t be so defiantly, so imperiously, kind. And I remember wondering why you said you had no matches left, for before you went upstairs I had seen a box half-full in your hand, but I said to myself: ‘He has forgotten, and he is wretched at his friend’s weakness.’ Ah, you should have told me about Gerald there and then, indeed you should! But you did not, for my unworthy comfort’s sake. Dear, you have a fine touch for the affections﻿—but cruel, that is what you were, cruel. You laid your foot down on the soil of kindness, but where your foot fell there leapt up a dandelion﻿ ﻿… and in the heart of the dandelion a tiny little rose; but what, my friend, is one little rose surrounded to suffocation by a huge dandelion?”

Well! Puzzle this way, puzzle that way, I couldn’t make a glimmer of sense out of that passage. I was pleased, of course, that she seemed to like me, but as to the rest.﻿ ﻿…

Guy, as he had told me he would, had been to see her early in the morning. He had﻿—another friend of childhood﻿—overruled Mrs. Oden, saying it would be better not to wake Iris and bring her downstairs at that hour, for could there be a better place than bed in which to receive bad news? Mrs. Oden knew him of old, he was Apollo Belvedere to Mrs. Oden. She had been desperately upset about his news, coming as it did on top of what she had read about Gerald in that morning’s paper. Poor Mrs. Oden.

===Chinese===

你必须记住，这封信是在杰拉尔德死后仅一个月寄来的。她写到了那一夜，而在这一处，她那着了魔的铅笔写得最为坚定有力，如果这话能说明什么的话。“我就站在那古老古老的黑暗中——黑暗是多么古老啊，你曾感受过吗？——当时你就在楼上杰拉尔德的房间里。我侧耳倾听，却听不到你走动的声音，所以我猜想你正从门口注视着杰拉尔德，就像一百万年前的那天晚上你我所做的那样，当时，你还记得吗，你突然、奇特地发自肺腑地，对着我的手行了一个倔强的屈膝礼？而且，你知道吗，看到你对那个可怜无助的可人儿如此体贴，我几乎要哭了出来。噢，希拉里已经把你的事都告诉我了，说你想引诱杰拉尔德去疗养院，但一切都是徒劳，我可怜的杰拉尔德。接着我听见你关掉了灯，你走下楼来，缓缓地，缓缓地，比黑暗还要寂静，当你开口说话时，你的声音和黑暗一样沧桑。但你骨子里其实很年轻，否则你不会如此倔强、如此傲然地体贴。我记得当时自己还在纳闷，你为什么说没有火柴了，因为在你上楼之前，我还看到你手里拿着半盒火柴，但我对自己说：‘他忘了，而且他为朋友的软弱而心碎。’啊，你当时就该把杰拉尔德的事告诉我，你真该说出来的！但你没有，只为了让我免遭打击。亲爱的，你对情感有着敏锐的触觉——但你太残忍了，你就是很残忍。你把脚踏在善良的土壤上，但在你脚步落下之处，却猛然冒出了一朵蒲公英……而在那朵蒲公英的中心，有一朵小小的玫瑰；可是，我的朋友，被一朵巨大的蒲公英窒息般包围的一朵小玫瑰，又算得了什么呢？”

哎！横思竖想，那段话我愣是找不出半点由头。当然，得知她似乎喜欢我，我很高兴，但至于其余的部分……

正如盖伊曾告诉我的那样，他清晨就去探望过她。他——又是童年的另一位好友——驳回了奥登夫人的意见，说最好不要在那个钟点叫醒艾丽丝并让她下楼，因为难道还有比躺在床上接受坏消息更好的地方吗？奥登夫人早就认识他，在奥登夫人眼里他就是贝尔维德勒的阿波罗神。听到他的消息，她难过得要命，更何况这消息偏偏赶在她那天早晨在报纸上读到关于杰拉尔德的报道之后。可怜的奥登夫人。

===Original===

Iris was asleep﻿—“Oh, as no man can ever know sleep!”﻿—when she awoke dimly to a tall shape at the foot of the bed. (“As no man can ever know sleep!” That, too, puzzled one, as well it might.) Dark it was, the curtains drawn, “and I remember them flapping peevishly because the door behind the tall shape was ajar. And I, scarcely awake, could think but of one thing, my awakening mind was hugging, in pain and joy, but one thing﻿ ﻿… and I called the shape at the foot of the bed by a certain name, a name which was not his name. He made no sign that he had heard the name which was not his name, and I am sure he instantly made himself forget it. For, as you know, Guy would defend a secret not only against the angels of God, but also against himself. ‘Guy!’ I cried at last, and he seemed to smile faintly, like the handsome absentminded god he is. ‘Yes, Guy,’ he said. ‘Sickening, isn’t it?’ Those high good looks of Guy’s, that small poised head﻿—frozen, tireless Guy! But that morning he was very gentle with me.﻿ ﻿…”

He had spoken for me, too, saying that I hadn’t told her of Gerald’s death at the time because she had looked so tired and sad. “Poor Iris,” Guy had said, “the men who don’t know you very well care very much for your comfort, but the three young men who have known you best of all have not cared enough.” Guy had said that, and she lying in bed, stunned, staring, while he sat holding her hand, as he might be an elder brother and she a hurt baby.

===Chinese===

艾丽丝当时正在沉睡——“噢，任何男人都决不可能体会那样的睡眠！”——这时她隐隐醒来，看见床脚处立着一个高大的身影。（“任何男人都决不可能体会那样的睡眠！”这也同样让人费解，倒也合情合理。）屋里很暗，窗帘拉着，“我记得窗帘在烦躁地拍打着，因为那个高大身影身后的门半开着。而我几乎还没完全醒来，脑海里只能想到一件事，我渐渐苏醒的心智在痛苦与欢愉中死死抱紧的只有一件事……我冲着床脚的那个身影叫出了一个特定的名字，一个并非他本名的名字。他没有表现出任何迹象表明他听到了那个非他本名的名字，而且我确信他立刻强迫自己将其忘掉。因为，正如你所知，盖伊不仅会对上帝的天使严守秘密，甚至也会对自己严守秘密。‘盖伊！’我终于喊道，他似乎微微笑了一下，宛如那位英俊而心不在焉的神明。‘是的，盖伊，’他说，‘真让人恶心，不是吗？’盖伊那出众的相貌，那高傲小巧的头颅——冷若冰霜、不知疲倦的盖伊！但那天早晨他对我却十分温柔。……”

他也为我说了好话，说我当时之所以没告诉她杰拉尔德去世的消息，是因为她看起来太疲惫、太悲伤了。“可怜的艾丽丝，”盖伊曾说，“那些不太了解你的男人非常在乎你的舒适，而最了解你的那三个年轻男人却关心得不够。”盖伊说了这番话，而她躺在床上，目瞪口呆，凝视着他，他则坐在那里握着她的手，就仿佛他是一个哥哥，而她是一个受伤的婴孩。

===Original===

“He knew, you see, that I loved Gerald, that Gerald was a part of me, although Gerald had spent ten years in pretending that he hated me. Do you think, my friend, that I would have let myself be crucified on Boy’s death only for the sake of Boy’s cruel relations and friends? Two people Gerald worshipped in the world, but always he would have sacrificed Iris to Boy, that was always the way of Gerald’s heart. Above all things in this world I love the love that people have for each other, the real, immense, unquestioning, devouring, worshipful love that now and then I have seen in a girl for a boy, that now and then I have seen in a boy for a boy, that playmate love. It isn’t of this world, that playmate love, it’s of a larger world than ours, a better world, a world of dreams which aren’t illusions but the very pillars of a better life. But in our world all dreams are illusions, and that is why the angels have crows-feet round their eyes, because they are peering to see why all dreams in our world should be illusions.

“But you can’t, you see, get rid of the funny love between twins like Gerald and me just by the word ‘hate.’ Even Boy couldn’t really upset that. There was something peculiarly *us* about Gerald and me, something of blood and bone peculiarly us which nothing but death could destroy. And so Mrs. Spirit was sent into Hyde Park that the thing that was us might be forever destroyed.”

She had suddenly asked Guy, half-sitting on the bed beside her, what it was in the world he loved most, and he had said he was sorry to admit that he loved his son more than all the world. “I could have killed him for jealousy, just then I could, he who had everything to have also that. You don’t know the body-ache for a child, the ache that destroys a body﻿ ﻿… the lament for a child of love, a child of lovers.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“你看，他知道我深爱着杰拉尔德，杰拉尔德是我生命的一部分，尽管杰拉尔德花了十年时间假装恨我。我的朋友，难道你以为我任由自己被钉死在‘男孩’的死上，仅仅是为了‘男孩’那些残忍的亲友吗？杰拉尔德在这世上崇拜两个人，但他总是会为了‘男孩’而牺牲艾丽丝，杰拉尔德的心一向如此。在世间所有的事物中，我最爱的是人们彼此之间的爱，那种真实、浩瀚、毫无保留、吞噬一切、令人膜拜的爱，我偶尔在女孩对男孩的眼中见过，偶尔在男孩对男孩的眼中见过，那种玩伴般的爱。这种玩伴般的爱并不属于这个世界，它属于一个比我们更广阔的世界，一个更美好的世界，一个梦想不是幻觉、而是更美好生活的真正支柱的世界。但在我们的世界里，所有的梦想皆是幻觉，这就是为什么天使们的眼角会长出鱼尾纹，因为他们正努力探究，为何我们世界里所有的梦想都注定是幻觉。

“可是你看，像我和杰拉尔德这样的双胞胎之间那种古怪的爱，是绝不能单凭‘恨’这个字就能抹煞的。就连‘男孩’也无法真正破坏它。在我和杰拉尔德身上，有一种特属于‘我们’的东西，一种特属于我们、唯有死亡才能摧毁的血肉相连。因此斯皮里特夫人被派到了海德公园，好让属于‘我们’的那样东西被永远摧毁。”

她当时半坐在床边，突然问盖伊这世上他最爱什么，而盖伊说很遗憾地承认，他爱他的儿子胜过世间的一切。“当时我简直嫉妒得想杀了他，真的，他拥有一切，竟然还拥有那个。你不知道渴求一个孩子时身体的那种撕心裂肺之痛，那种摧毁身躯的痛楚……对一个因爱而生的孩子、一对恋人结晶的哀恸。……

===Original===

“He would be two and a half years old now, my son. Hector, you see, didn’t know anything about his son, because he left me in a temper before even I was certain. And naturally when I was certain I wasn’t going to be outdone in silliness by my own husband, and besides, I thought it would be mean to force him to come back if he didn’t really want to come back, and so I didn’t let him know. For men, I would have you know, might make an awful row and stamp away in a tearing jealous fit, and when they are away they might be as pleased as anything to have got away. You can never tell about men, especially when they are convinced that they are being genuine. But, of course, I knew he would rush back quickly enough when the baby came. Oh, I would see to that! And, my dear, the fun I would have all by myself, for Hector and I had always longed for a son, the fun I had thinking of the look on his face when one day he would get my wire in Ireland: ‘Arrival of Hector-not-so-proud. You come too.’ Can you imagine what he’d look like then, and he stern and handsome and all covered with V.C.’s and oddments, wasting his time chasing disgusting Sinn Feiners who wouldn’t know a country of their own if they saw it. I had that wire all nicely written out months beforehand, and I went and hid my ugliness in my old nurse’s home near Peterboro’ and stuck the wire with a pin over my bed, being superstitious, you see, and wanting a winner for once in a way. Well, and then﻿—Oh, and then they killed Hector just in time, and when Hector-not-so-proud came along he thought, the poor sweet, that the proper way for a gentleman to arrive in the world was toes first to slow music, and so away he had to go again.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“如果我儿子还活着，现在该有两岁半了。你看，赫克托对他儿子一无所知，因为甚至在我自己还不确定之前，他就负气离我而去了。而当我确定之后，我自然不愿在愚蠢上输给自己丈夫，况且，我觉得如果他不是真心想回来，逼他回来未免太卑劣了，所以我没有告诉他。因为我得让你知道，男人们可能会大吵大闹，在嫉妒的狂怒中跺着脚离去，而一旦他们离开了，他们可能会为能够逃脱而高兴得不得了。你永远说不准男人，尤其是当他们坚信自己很真诚的时候。不过，我当然知道等孩子出生时，他肯定会飞快地赶回来。噢，我一定会安排好的！而且，亲爱的，我独自一人能享受到多大的乐趣啊，因为赫克托和我一直渴望要个儿子，我一想到有一天他在爱尔兰收到我的电报时脸上的表情就乐不可支：‘没那么骄傲的赫克托降生了。你也来吧。’你能想象他当时会是什么表情吗？他那么威严、英俊，满身挂着维多利亚十字勋章和零碎勋饰，却把时间浪费在追捕令人作呕的新芬党人上——那些人就算亲眼见到自己的国家也认不出来。那封电报我提前几个月就写得工工整整，然后我躲到彼得伯勒附近我老保姆的家里去遮掩我的丑态，并用大头针把电报别在我的床头上方，你看，因为我很迷信，难得想赢一次。嗯，然后——噢，然后他们恰巧在那时杀死了赫克托，而当那个‘没那么骄傲的赫克托’来到人世时，那个可怜的小宝贝却以为，一位绅士来到世上的正当方式，是在舒缓的哀乐声中脚尖先着地，于是他不得不又离去了。……

===Original===

“I have done with England, and England has done with me. But I don’t think I shall be able to go and have tea with the Empress of China yet awhile, for just now I love England as I never before have loved it. The captains and the kings of England﻿—clean eyes, long shadows, low voices﻿ ﻿… why, I must hover, held in running as in a nightmare. And from the distance, from these lands of loud shrill voices, I will hear the low, low voices that I had long since thought I had given up regretting. Indeed, I was quite sure I had given up regretting them. But I am regretting them now, like a baby. Goodbye, dear, and God bless you. And when you think of me think instead of your words, ‘He has, with you,’ and you will have the sum of my pride in being liked by you.”

Often, during these past eight or nine months, that scrawled writing would pass my mind, but as I could hit on no clue to her fantasies, and as I might never see her again, I had put Iris carefully away into that part of our minds wherein we keep fancies, images, regrets, the things that we will do one day, the things that we would like to do one day, the things that we will never do again﻿ ﻿… when, but a moment ago, the great yellow car had leapt from the Place Vendôme into the first place in my mind, and I would like, I thought, to learn from my friend, Cherry-Marvel, anything that might be learnt about Iris. But as I listened to him, the way he had said this and had done that and had heard the other, I wondered how I would ever get the chance to suggest so much as her name to him. “… apropos of something which I am positive that you, with your sort of mind.﻿ ﻿…”

We stood, for we had not yet had time to sit down, in the little reading-room of the Ritz that leads from the entrance-doors, while stern-faced Americans turned over the pages of *The New York Herald* on the long marble-topped table in the centre, and a woman or two sat here and there absorbed in waiting, and the dowager Lady Tekkleham’s voice nearby was grimly suggesting to the Baron de Belus that he could not do better than let her drive him in her coach-and-six to dinner at her villa at Saint Germain-en-Laye.

===Chinese===

“我与英国缘分已尽，英国也与我一刀两断。但我看我一时半会儿还无法去和中国皇后共进下午茶，因为就在此时此刻，我爱英国胜过以往任何时候。英国的统帅与君王——明澈的眼眸、长长的身影、低沉的话音……唉，我必须徘徊盘桓，如在噩梦中那般奔跑却寸步难行。而在远方，在这些充斥着喧嚣尖叫的国度里，我将听到那些低沉柔和的声音，而我本以为自己早已不再眷恋它们了。确实，我原以为自己已经不再眷恋。但我现在又像个婴儿一样眷恋起它们来了。再见，亲爱的，愿上帝保佑你。当你想到我时，不如想一想你说过的那句话，‘有了你，他就算有了’，那便是我因被你喜欢而感到骄傲的全部。”

在过去的八九个月里，那歪歪扭扭的字迹常在我脑海中闪现，但由于我找不到解读她那些奇思怪想的线索，而且我可能再也见不到她了，我便将艾丽丝小心翼翼地搁置在心底的一隅——我们在那里保存幻想、意象、遗憾，保存我们终有一天会去做的事、终有一天想去做的事，以及我们再也不会去做的事……然而就在片刻之前，那辆黄色大轿车从旺多姆广场跃入我心头的第一要位，我想，不妨从我的朋友切里-马维尔那里打听一切有关艾丽丝的消息。可是，当我听着他讲述他如何说了这个、做了那个、听到了别的什么时，我纳闷自己究竟何时才能有机会向他提起哪怕她的名字。“……说到一件事，我相信凭你的这种心智。……”

我们站在丽兹酒店从正门通往的狭小阅览室里（因为我们还没来得及坐下），面容严肃的美国人在中央那张长长的浮雕大理石面上翻看着《纽约先驱报》，一两名女子零星坐在各处全神贯注地等待着，身旁寡居的特克勒姆夫人正语气严厉地向贝吕斯男爵提议，他最好坐她那辆六马大马车去她在圣日耳曼昂莱的别墅吃晚饭。

===Original===

Weighed down I was by the chill of my journey and my heavy coat, and weighed down, too, by the gloom of the early winter evening that was falling about us, so that my eyes, borne down by Cherry-Marvels amenities, could scarcely make out the chairs and flowers and vases in the long courtyard through the windows; and suddenly I fell to wondering how it had come about that Iris, who loved her proud swift car, had lent it to a friend, but the instant I mentioned her name Cherry-Marvel’s little eyes gleamed with fury at the interruption. I was abashed, yet I would try again, but﻿ ﻿… “whereupon Auguste de Maupin, whom, of course, you know as well as I do.﻿ ﻿…”

But at last I achieved the impossible, in inserting a wedge into the fabulous monologue, and then I murmured: “Ill? But are you sure, Cherry? Mrs. Storm is ill?”

But illness appalled Cherry-Marvel, from illness he could not help but turn away the neat, lined mask of his face, from illness his Florentine *dandysme* trembled away in the only unaristocratic emotion I have ever observed in Cherry-Marvel, the emotion of fear. “Quiet we call Silence, the merest word of all!” For, appalled by illness though he might be, his art could always rise to a general view.﻿ ﻿… He had heard in a roundabout way that Iris had had a “sort of minor operation﻿—”

“But,” I said﻿—

===Chinese===

旅途的寒意和我厚重的外套压得我透不过气，周围降临的初冬黄昏的阴郁也同样压得我透不过气，因此，在切里-马维尔滔滔不绝的客套话压迫下，我的双眼透过窗户几乎看不清长庭院里的桌椅、花朵和花瓶；我突然纳闷起来，艾丽丝向来珍爱自己那辆骄傲敏捷的汽车，怎会把它借给朋友呢？但我刚一提到她的名字，切里-马维尔那双小眼睛就因被打断而闪过一丝怒火。我感到很尴尬，但我还想再试一次，可是……“随后奥古斯特·德·莫潘——你当然和我一样对他再熟悉不过了。……”

但最终我做到了这件不可能的事，在那场神话般的独白中插进了一个楔子，接着我低声说道：“病了？但你确定吗，切里？斯托姆夫人生病了？”

然而疾病令切里-马维尔生畏，面对疾病，他不由自主地把那张整洁、布满皱纹的面具般的脸扭向一旁，面对疾病，他那佛罗伦萨式的唯美主义在一种恐惧的情绪中瑟瑟发抖退缩——这是我在切里-马维尔身上见过的唯一一种非贵族的情绪。“我们把寂静称为宁静，世上最平凡的词！”因为，尽管他可能对疾病感到骇然，他的艺术却总能上升到一种普遍的宏观视角。……他辗转听说艾丽丝做了一场“某种小手术——”

“但是，”我说——

===Original===

“Whereupon,” said Cherry-Marvel, his little eyes gleaming for a second with fury, “what *I* said was, ‘Operations, where are thy stings?’ for, as of course you know as well as I do, women are scarcely women without them, and I have not the faintest doubt that in Lesbos they suffered, if I may put it like this, from the impolite insistence of their womanhood even more than if there had been any men there, for as I was saying to Marc only the other day, apropos of the particular shade in which she had dyed her hair, men may come and men may go, but the moon, my dear boy, is always there. Now here, for instance, is Iris, quite one of the loveliest women I have ever seen, and one who, I am convinced, must be very fond of you with your sort of mind, here she has, I hear on the very best authority, fallen a victim to one of those mental derangements which seem, if I may put it like this, to be an irresistible incitement to polite surgery in quite another and more individual part of the person. But what I have always said about Iris is this, that I admire her so, and I am so positive that you also must, with your sort of mind, because she is one of the very few Englishwomen I have ever met who can, as I am sure you will agree with me, live abroad without becoming more and more English.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“随后，”切里-马维尔说，他的小眼睛瞬间闪过一丝狂怒，“我当时说的是，‘手术啊，你的毒刺在哪里？’因为，你当然和我一样清楚，女人要是没有这些就几乎不成其为女人了，而且我毫不怀疑，在莱斯博斯岛上，如果可以这么说的话，她们遭受女人天性的无礼纠缠，比有男人在场时还要严重，因为正如我前几天对马克所说的那样，说到她把头发染成的那个特殊色调，男人来来往往，但月亮啊，我亲爱的孩子，却永远挂在那里。举例来说，看看艾丽丝，她真是我见过的最动人的女人之一，而且我确信，凭你的这种心智，她一定会非常喜欢你；据最可靠的消息来源透露，她成了某类精神紊乱的受害者，如果可以这么说的话，这种紊乱似乎对身体另一个更为私密的部位进行体面的外科手术构成了不可抗拒的诱惑。但我对艾丽丝的一贯评价是：我是如此钦佩她，而且我相信凭你的心智也必定如此，因为她是我见过的极少数几个能够长居国外而不会变得越来越英国气的英国女人之一，我相信你在这点上一定会赞同我。……”

### II / 第二节
===Original===

Paris rises in a cloud of chill darkness, the rain falls like whips of ice, the streetlamps loiter on vague, bitter errands, confused strings of light, a stealthy, idiot wind glories in being corrupted by corners. The platforms of the omnibuses are packed tight with small men whose overcoats are too short for them, the brims of their felt-hats too narrow, their trousers turned up too high, their eyes too dark, their faces too pale. The jargon of the traffic on the rue de Rivoli, as it squabbles for every step between the deserted pavement beneath the railings of the Tuileries and the reeking pavement under the long archway lit by impudent shop-lights falling on imitation jewellery, is multiplied an hundredfold by the shrewish air into a noise that hurts like warm water on a chill hand.

The taxi, a clever little Citroën taxi, darted hither and thither among the squabbling hosts, and nimbly we capered across the dark face of the Louvre, nimbly over the Pont Royal and the river paved with broken darkness, and so down the slope into the rout of the Boulevard Raspail.

Maybe it is true that there are times when we can detest Paris more deeply than any other city. Other cities stare back calmly at our sudden hatreds, other cities grow more impersonal as we execrate them, while as for Paris, she is always personal, but when we are nervous and detest her for being Paris she becomes even more herself, she insists on being herself with a nerveracking insistence, like a silly woman who, seeing she is getting on her man’s nerves, gives a loud, nervous laugh and simpers: “I can’t help it, it’s my nature to be like this.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

巴黎在冷冽的黑暗浓雾中升起，雨水如冰鞭般抽打而下，路灯在迷茫阴冷的差使中徘徊，交织成一片凌乱的光带，一阵诡秘而痴愚的风因在街角肆意穿凿而洋洋自得。公共汽车的站台上挤满了矮小的男人，他们的大衣太短，毡帽的帽檐太窄，裤脚卷得太高，眼睛太黑，面容太苍白。里沃利街上的车流喧嚣嘈杂，在杜伊勒里宫栏杆下冷清的人行道与长长拱廊下弥漫着恶臭的人行道（那里被照射在仿造珠宝上的无礼橱窗灯光所照亮）之间的每一寸路面上争抢不休，尖刻的空气将这种喧杂放大了百倍，变成一种刺痛人的声响，宛如温水浇在冻僵的手上。

那辆出租车是一辆灵巧的小雪铁龙，在争道抢行的车流中穿来穿去，我们轻捷地掠过卢浮宫幽暗的立面，轻捷地跨过皇家桥和铺着破碎黑暗的塞纳河，随后顺坡而下，冲入拉斯帕伊大道的混乱洪流。

也许确实如此，有时我们对巴黎的厌恶会超过其他任何城市。面对我们突如其来的憎恨，其他城市只会报以冷漠的凝视；当我们咒骂它们时，其他城市会变得更加冷淡超然；至于巴黎，她永远那么带着个人色彩，但当我们神经过敏、因她是巴黎而厌恶她时，她反而变得更加任性本色，以一种令人崩溃的执拗坚持自我，宛如一个愚蠢的女人，眼看自己让男伴心烦意乱，却发出一阵尖锐神经质的笑声，娇嗔道：“我没办法呀，我的天性就是这样。……”

===Original===

Now why were the people yelling here, what was the matter? Millions of them there were, joined in some strife between the Bon Marché, the Hôtel Lutetia and the entrance of the Nord-Sud railway, while omnibuses and trams made strategic movements against each other, while *facteurs* in dirty blue, fabulously moustachioed, pushed carts about in all directions, irritating anyone they could, and a motionless gendarme or two played with his bâton, heedless, unheeded. The eager face of a young artist I knew, shadowed by a great black hat, artistic, anarchistic, strode out of the white mass of the Hôtel Lutetia and turned greedily towards Montparnasse. At last my clever little Citroën and I plunged into quieter wastes, lit here and there by the bastard glitter of a Cinema Theatre falling on posters livid with three colours, red, blue, and yellow.

That strange unstormy exquisite, Cherry-Marvel! That most aesthetic creator of a monster more terrible than Frankenstein’s, for it devoured the spirit of all who passed beside it! Why I should be worried about Iris I could not tell, indeed I was too tired to inquire, but worried I was despite Cherry-Marvel’s so well-informed badinage about the white woman’s burden, and the more worried too, as the taxi plunged into nameless darknesses beyond the Bal Bullier, towards the address of the nursing-home which Cherry-Marvel, that confidant at third-hand, had of course known.

===Chinese===

如今这儿的人们究竟为何大呼小叫，到底出了什么事？数以百万计的人聚在廉价百货商场、鲁特西亚酒店和南北地下铁道入口之间争执不休，公共汽车和电车彼此展开战略包抄，身穿脏蓝色制服、蓄着惊人大胡子的搬运工推着手推车横冲直撞，肆意激怒目所能及的每一个人，一两个纹丝不动的宪兵摆弄着警棍，漠不关心，也无人理会。一位我认识的年轻艺术家面带急切的神色，在黑色大帽的阴影笼罩下，带着艺术与无政府主义的气质，大步走出白色的鲁特西亚酒店，贪婪地朝蒙帕纳斯走去。最后，我和我那辆灵巧的小雪铁龙冲入了较为僻静的荒凉地带，这里被一两家电影院那杂乱闪烁的灯光照亮，灯光洒在红、蓝、黄三色交织的惨白海报上。

那个奇特而不受风暴侵扰的精致之人，切里-马维尔！那个极具唯美情调的创造者，造出了一个比弗兰肯斯坦的怪物更可怕的怪物，因为它吞噬了所有从它身旁经过之人的灵魂！我为何会为艾丽丝感到担忧，自己也说不清楚，事实上我已经疲惫得无力深究，但尽管切里-马维尔对“白种人的负担”开着消息极其灵通的玩笑，我依然担忧不已；随着出租车冲过布利耶舞场，驶入无名的黑暗，朝切里-马维尔——这位三手知情者——自然知晓的那家疗养院地址奔去，我的忧虑更是有增无减。

===Original===

Montparnasse lay somewhere behind, or to the east, or to the west. We were in unknown Paris, silent, ill-lit, fantastic Paris: silent but for a rending crash here, a jarring cry there. Cold as the devil it was now, as though because the prickly warmth of many lamps and shops was withdrawn. Carefully we traversed a broad avenue as yet scarcely paved, beneath the skeleton shapes of great tenement-houses. Ah, Paris, that we should have come to this, you and I! Paris, that we should have come together down to this! In how many moods you and I have passed the time of day and night together, we have sat in strange places and dared the most devilish shadows, we have wandered from the Rotonde to the crowning grubbiness of the Butte, we have raced in the Bois and up the Mont Valérien, we have laughed at painted boys and been reviled by painted women, we have danced, loved, gambled, drunk, and together we have been bored by the unmentionable and terrified by that which makes the eyes bright and the face white as a soiled handkerchief, while Mio Mi Marianne danced a *minuet du cœur* with a crimson garter and the moon fell across the French-windows of Berneval’s house to be lost in the soft shadows of giant poppies. Paris, that we should now have come down to this, lost together in these nameless darknesses beyond even the low darkness of the Bal Bullier, that glory of another time than ours.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

蒙帕纳斯被抛在了身后，或东或西。我们置身于陌生的巴黎，寂静、幽暗、奇异的巴黎：除了此处刺耳的撞击声或彼处刺耳的叫喊声，一片死寂。此刻冷得要命，仿佛是因为众多灯火与店铺那针扎般的暖意都已消退。在巨大出租公寓楼的骨架阴影下，我们小心翼翼地穿过一条几乎尚未铺设路面的宽阔林荫道。啊，巴黎，你我竟沦落到了这般境地！巴黎，我们竟然一起沦落到了这一步！在多少种情绪之中，你我和谐共度了白昼与黑夜，我们坐在陌生的地方，敢于直面最险恶的阴影，我们从圆顶咖啡馆漫游到蒙马特高地那极致的肮脏，我们在布洛涅森林和瓦莱里安山上飞驰，我们嘲笑涂脂抹粉的男孩，遭受浓妆艳抹的女人辱骂，我们跳舞、相爱、赌博、痛饮，我们一同为难以启齿的事物感到厌烦，一同为那令双眼明亮、面色白如脏手帕的事物感到恐惧，与此同时，缪·米·玛丽安系着深红吊袜带跳着“心灵的小步舞曲”，月光洒在贝尔纳瓦尔宅邸的法式落地窗上，迷失在硕大罂粟花的柔和阴影中。巴黎，如今我们竟然沦落至此，一同迷失在这无名的黑暗中，甚至超越了布利耶舞场那低矮的昏暗——那是属于另一个时代的辉煌。……

===Original===

And now we tore up a dark, endless boulevard, even as a shifty maggot in a pit of darkness. But surely this was the murderer’s Paris, here lived the fathers and grandfathers of Apaches, here were born the daughters of the drinkers of blood and the sons of the mothers of crime. It stretched never-ending between lamps fixed at astronomical distances, and on each side tall naked trees thrashed the shadows of very high black walls. They hid from the world, the people of this boulevard of the high walls, and who shall say that they had no reason to hide? And then, do you know, a lion leapt out of the night, a huge lion that was black as sin and crouched for prey in the centre way between five lanes of darkness made even darker by confused strings of light. And as we breathed a prayer in thanks for our deliverance from the lion of darkness trams crawled near us and stayed a while, and tram, at the impulse of a vice peculiarly continental, was joined to tram and crawled away; while we, having regained our breath, came beneath the shadow of a terrible wall. It was the wall of a castle, a fortress, a something satanically majestic. This, I thought, is another of Carlyle’s mistakes, this is no less than the Bastille skulking in these parts until such time as the Camelots du Roy shall have left the kindergarten and can crown the duc d’Orleans King of France. Far above our laboured passage glowed a long, long row of small windows faintly lit, and it seemed to me that they were striped with bars of iron. And there was a great gate of iron, and a black soldier with a beastly bayonet to his rifle, and an old woman with a great brown parcel under her arm, waiting. The clever little Citroën stopped. It is tired, I thought, and will go on later.﻿ ﻿…

“*Eh, numéro quarante-neuf, Boulevard Pierre Abel?*” the taxi-driver threw at me reproachfully, and I got out, and I stared up at the great fortress which towered above me like a beast with a row of unclean eyes about his forehead, and the rain whipped my face.

“*C’est un prison?*”

“*Mais oui, monsieur. Le Paradis.*”

===Chinese===

现在我们沿着一条黑暗而漫无边际的大道疾驰，就像黑暗深坑里一条狡黠的蛆虫。但这无疑是属于凶手的巴黎，这里居住着阿帕奇流氓的父辈与祖辈，这里诞生了嗜血者的女儿和罪恶之母的儿子。它在设置在天文级遥远距离的路灯之间无休无止地延伸，道路两旁高大光秃的树木抽打着极高黑墙的阴影。他们躲避着尘世，这条高墙大道上的居民，谁又能说他们没有理由躲藏呢？然后，你知道吗，一头狮子从黑夜中跃出，一头巨大如罪恶般漆黑的狮子，在五条因错综光带而显得更加阴暗的黑暗通道中央蹲伏着等待猎物。正当我们为从黑暗之狮脱险而心怀感激地祈祷时，电车在我们身旁缓缓爬行并短暂停留，随后在一种带有特殊欧洲大陆特色的冲动下，电车与电车车厢相连并爬行远去；而我们喘过气来，来到了一堵可怕的高墙阴影之下。这是一座城堡的墙，一座要塞，某种带着撒旦般威严的东西。我想，这是卡莱尔的又一个错误，这分明就是潜伏在这些地方的巴士底狱，直到国王的商贩们从幼儿园毕业、能够为奥尔良公爵加冕为法国国王为止。在我们艰难穿行的头顶高处，闪烁着一长排微微亮着的小窗户，在我看来，那些窗户上似乎排列着铁栏杆。那里有一座巨大的铁门，一个步枪上插着令人讨厌刺刀的黑人士兵，还有一个腋下夹着硕大棕色包裹的老妇人在等待。灵巧的小雪铁龙停了下来。它累了，我想，稍后还会继续前行。……

“喂，皮埃尔·阿贝尔大道四十九号？”出租车司机责怪地冲我嚷了一句，我下了车，仰望着那座高高耸立在我头顶的巨大要塞，它宛如一头额头长着一排肮脏眼睛的巨兽，雨水抽打着我的脸。

“这是监狱吗？”

“可不是嘛，先生。叫天堂监狱。”

===Original===

The pavement was broad, of mud and asphalt. The prison towered on our right, filling the sky with darkness﻿—but for those distant, terrible windows. The rain whipped down, stinging like little animals. Nearby one forlorn lamp lit the putrefying colours of the advertisements circling a lavabo. What, I wondered and wondered, could Iris be doing here? Facing me across the broad pavement of mud and asphalt was a great gate which had once been brown, lit by a lamp on which had once been inscribed the number of the Nursing Home. Iris was here. Were we, then, always to meet in darkness, Iris and I? She was here, and perhaps, I thought, on the other side of her is a Morgue or an Asylum.

A yard or so from the great door there was let into the high wall a small door inscribed “Concierge.” I was startled at the clatter made by the bell. A nun stood in the dim doorway.

===Chinese===

人行道很宽，由泥浆和沥青铺成。监狱耸立在我们右侧，除了那些遥远而可怕的窗户，将天空完全遮蔽在黑暗中。雨水抽打而下，像小动物一样蜇人。附近一盏孤零零的路灯照耀着环绕在公共盥洗室四周的海报上那些腐烂褪色的色彩。我苦思冥想，艾丽丝究竟在这儿做什么？隔着宽阔的泥泞与沥青人行道，对面是一扇曾经是棕色的大门，门上方的灯原本刻着那家疗养院的门牌号码。艾丽丝就在这里。难道我和艾丽丝注定总要在黑暗中相遇吗？她就在这里，我想，或许在她墙壁的另一侧就是一间停尸房或疯人院。

距离大门约一码左右的高墙上嵌着一扇小门，上面刻着“门房”。门铃发出的叮当声让我吓了一跳。一位修女站在昏暗的门廊前。

## VI / 第六章 红色灯火
===Original===

The Red Lights

===Chinese===

红色灯火

### I / 第一节
===Original===

The shape of her coif against the dim light was like some legendary thing’s head, and she was eating. I heard her. That she was old and very stout was all I could see. I could smell just a little, too. Poor Iris.

I asked if I might have news of Mrs. Storm.

“*Ah, la dame anglaise!*” She ate, but not finally. “*Madame est assez bien, je crois. Mais pardon, monsieur. Je n’ai pas d’instructions à vous donner*﻿—”

“But!” I pleaded. “But﻿—”

“*Je regrette, monsieur. C’est pas ma faute, vous savez. Pardon.*”

She was closing the door! Terse as you like. I was helpless. “*Madame est assez bien, je crois!*” Dear Heaven, but didn’t one know those *assez biens*! Isn’t there a company in Heaven wholly recruited from those who have been *assez bien*, and daily augmented by those who are *assez bien*!

I lifted up my voice.

“*Pardon, monsieur.*”

I lifted up my voice in vain. So I was active. She stared at me, panting. I withdrew my first impression as to her being a nun. She was no nun. She had a crucifix and a coif, but she was no nun. She was a woman scorned. She said many things and used many words which I did not understand. But I didn’t care. I somehow thought, you know, of Iris dying.

“I am here,” I said in effect, “and here I stay until I can speak to a doctor or a matron. I am sorry, but you have made me anxious as to the lady’s health.”

“*Mais je vous l’ai déjà dit, jeune homme! Madame est assez bien!*”

The ordinary dingy concierge’s lodge: a black stove, a table covered with frayed red cloth, a chair, a stool, an indescribable odour, a plate of food on the table﻿—*bœuf bouilli*, which is French for the salvaging of grey matter from liquid dungeons of onions, carrots and potatoes. I sat on the stool. It was unbelievable that her coif had ever been white. Somehow my eyes were transfixed by the small wooden crucifix which, like a dinghy on a choppy sea, rolled on her bosom as she ate. I wondered how long I would have to wait. I wondered if I could smoke. I wondered if this was one of those convent-nursing-homes. I wondered if one called a nun madame or mademoiselle. They were maidens presumably, so I supposed mademoiselle.

“*On peut fumer, mademoiselle?*”

I was wrong. She looked at me with contempt. “*C’est défendu, monsieur.*”

“*Merci, madame.*”

===Chinese===

在昏暗的光线下，她的软帽轮廓就像某种传说中怪物的头颅，而且她正在吃东西。我听得见她吃东西的声音。我所能看到的只是她年纪很大、身体极胖。我还能隐约闻到一点气味。可怜的艾丽丝。

我询问能否打听一下斯托姆夫人的近况。

“啊，那位英国夫人！”她一边吃着一边说，但嘴没停下。“夫人的情况还算好，我想是吧。但对不起，先生。我没有奉令向您透露任何消息——”

“可是！”我恳求道，“可是——”

“我很遗憾，先生。您知道，这不是我的过错。对不起。”

她正准备关门！干脆利落得很。我束手无策。“夫人的情况还算好，我想是吧！”天哪，难道大家还不知道那些所谓的“还算好”意味着什么吗！天堂里难道没有一整队完全由那些曾经“还算好”的人招募而成的队伍，而且每天还因那些眼下“还算好”的人而不断扩充吗！

我提高了声音。

“对不起，先生。”

我白白提高了嗓门。于是我采取了行动。她气喘吁吁地瞪着我。我收回了起初以为她是修女的印象。她根本不是修女。她戴着十字架和软帽，但她绝不是修女。她是一个满怀怨怼的女人。她说了许多话，用了许多我不懂的词汇。但我不在乎。不知怎的，你明白，我想到了濒死的艾丽丝。

“我就在这儿，”我实际的意思是这样说的，“而且我要一直待在这里，直到我能和医生或护士长说上话为止。我很抱歉，但您让我对这位女士的健康感到担忧。”

“可我都已经跟您说过了，年轻人！夫人情况还算好！”

普通的昏暗门房：一个黑色的火炉，一张铺着磨损红布的桌子，一把椅子，一张凳子，一股难以形容的气味，桌上一盘食物——法式炖牛肉，这在法语里意味着从洋葱、胡萝卜和土豆的液体地牢中打捞出灰白色的肉质。我坐在凳子上。简直难以置信她的软帽曾经会是白色的。不知为何，我的目光被那个小小的木制十字架牢牢吸引住了，那十字架就像波涛汹涌海面上的一艘小艇，在她吃东西时随着她的胸口起伏晃荡。我不知道自己得等多久。我不知道自己能不能抽烟。我不知道这是否是那些修道院疗养院之一。我不知道该称呼修女为夫人还是小姐。她们大概是未婚女子，所以我猜应该叫小姐。

“可以抽烟吗，小姐？”

我猜错了。她鄙夷地看了我一眼。“这是被禁止的，先生。”

“谢谢您，夫人。”

===Original===

I wondered if she really could be a nun. I wondered if one could tip a nun. Out of sheer hatred one acquires a passion for tipping in France and Italy. Detestable it was on this detestable day to sit like this, being hated. I made a muttering noise and gave her a ten-franc note, and it was in a more amiable spirit that she went on with her salvaging. At last there were only two bits of carrot and an awful looking onion left to engage her attention, and I felt that one might perhaps converse.

I was right about her being no nun. She was a lay-sister, she said. And this place, she told me, was a convent-nursing-home. “*Nous avons ici*,” she was pleased to add, “*la clientèle européenne la plus chic*.”

Perhaps that was the worst stroke of that day, so far. Iris among a *clientèle européenne la plus chic*.﻿ ﻿… One saw the cosmopolitan divorcées, their secret illnesses and guileful pains, their nasty little coquetries and the way they would blackmail their lovers with their sufferings, and one felt the sticky nightclub breath of all the silly, common harlotries of England, France, America. My poor ten-franc note must have seemed pathetic to this old lay-sister, who probably thought nothing of receiving a mille from an anxious Dago.

I had until then been trying not to wonder about Iris in the vile shadow of a prison. Suddenly I was furiously hot. What on earth was I doing here! Intruding where I was not wanted! I was about to go, to run, when the lay-sister was as though distracted from the last piece of carrot by the opening of a door in the back room. Frantically she hurried towards it. It would look too silly of me to run now. I could but ask, anyhow.

The lay-sister’s voice, voluble, vindictive, explanatory. Much good my ten francs had done! Then steps came towards me, into the lodge. “Eh,” I said. How afraid one always is of the callous French doctors with their cynical eyes and purple beards.﻿ ﻿…

A man, bald, sharp-featured as a bird, in a rough brown greatcoat, a tired-looking, an anxious-looking, middle-aged﻿—Englishman!

“Masters! Conrad Masters!”

“Well,” muttered that anxious-looking man. He looked just the same when he was playing bridge. He was always playing bridge, that man. And he said he hated playing bridge. That kind of man. “Well? How are you?”

“Glad,” I said, “glad it’s no worse. Glad it’s only you. I was afraid of a purple beard.”

===Chinese===

我不知道她是否真算得上是修女。我不知道能不能给修女小费。纯粹出于仇恨，人在法国和意大利会养成一种给小费的癖好。在这个令人厌恶的日子里，像这样坐着招人厌恶，真教人难以忍受。我嘟囔了几句，递给她一张十法郎的钞票，随后她便以一种更为和蔼的心情继续她的打捞大业。最后，只剩下两小块胡萝卜和一个看起来可怕的洋葱吸引着她的注意力，我觉得也许可以攀谈了。

我猜对了，她确实不是修女。她说自己是一名在俗修女。她告诉我，这里是一家修道院疗养院。“我们这里，”她高兴地补充道，“拥有全欧洲最时髦的客户群。”

也许这是迄今为止那一天最沉重的一击。艾丽丝竟然身处全欧洲最时髦的客户群之中。……人们能联想到那些国际名流离异女子，她们隐秘的病痛与心机的痛苦，她们下流的小卖弄，以及她们用自己的痛苦勒索情人的嘴脸，人们能感受到来自英格兰、法国、美利坚那些愚蠢庸俗的娼妓行当的黏腻夜总会气息。我那可怜的十法郎钞票对这位老在俗修女来说一定显得微不足道，她面对焦虑的南欧暴发户递来的一千法郎大钞大概早就习以为常了。

在那之前，我一直努力不去联想艾丽丝身处监狱那卑劣阴影之中的情景。突然间我浑身燥热，怒火中烧。我究竟在这里干什么！擅闯到不欢迎我的地方来！我正准备离开，准备落荒而逃，这时在俗修女仿佛被后屋开门的声音从最后一块胡萝卜上分了心。她慌乱地朝那边匆匆走去。如果我现在逃跑，未免显得太可笑了。无论如何，我至少可以问问看。

在俗修女的声音滔滔不绝、充满怨怒且带着辩解。我的十法郎真管用啊！接着脚步声朝我走来，走进了门房。“呃，”我说。人们总是多么害怕那些眼神冷酷、蓄着紫色胡须的冷血法国医生啊。……

一个秃顶、相貌如鸟般尖锐的男人，穿着一件粗糙的褐色大衣，面带倦容、神色焦虑的中年——英国人！

“马斯特斯！康拉德·马斯特斯！”

“哎，”那个神色焦虑的男人嘟囔道。他打桥牌时看起来也是这副模样。那个男人总是在打桥牌。而且他说他讨厌打桥牌。就是那种人。“哎？你怎么样？”

===Original===

“And how did you get here?” A man given to muttering, that. One could hear what he said or not just as one pleased. One couldn’t, you understand, be afraid of Conrad Masters.

“Masters, the fight I’ve had with this Cerberus to see you!”

“Rules﻿ ﻿… must have rules, you know.﻿ ﻿…” A decidedly undecided man. Soft-speaking but not plausible, a combination peculiarly English. A man of nerves. Shifty without suavity﻿ ﻿… and then, suddenly, apt to bite your head off like a very captain of men: “And how did you know Mrs. Storm was ill? Here?”

“Oh,” I said. “Well.﻿ ﻿…” And I thought of many things. Of Conrad Masters, of “Should a doctor tell?” of Cherry-Marvel, that confidant at third-hand, of Mrs. Conrad Masters. A dashing lady, that.

“Who but Cherry-Marvel told me!” said I.

“God in Heaven, that man!”

But Iris swept out of my mind her doctor’s problematical indiscretions to his dashing wife.﻿ ﻿…

“Ill,” he muttered. “Decidedly ill﻿ ﻿… mm.﻿ ﻿…”

“I heard,” I said desperately, “that she’d had a sort of operation﻿—”

“There’s been no operation!” snapped that captain of men. “Simply maddens a man, the way these things get about.﻿ ﻿…”

“Well, I’m only repeating what I heard, Masters. And you can’t hope for secrecy once our friend gets hold of anything﻿—”

“Who said anything about secrecy?” A dangerous, feline muttering. “I don’t want secrecy.﻿ ﻿…”

Silence. Anxieties walked across it arm-in-arm with that lank man’s doubtful heat.

“I say, Masters, is she﻿—is she very ill? But, of course, if I’m intruding.﻿ ﻿…”

Those worried eyes were fixed on the feet stuck far out from the chair on which he lay as though exhausted. The lay-sister appeared to be pottering about in the next room. “Thinking of Donna Guelãra, are you? Haven’t much faith in me and Martel-Bonnard, have you?” Faintly amused those worried eyes looked to be. That was that man’s way. You would think he was being shifty with you when he might be just laughing at you.

===Chinese===

“你怎么跑到这儿来了？”那是一个习惯于喃喃自语的人。别人听不听得清他的话全凭自愿。你要明白，一个人是不会害怕康拉德·马斯特斯的。

“马斯特斯，为了见你，我和这个看门恶犬打了一场恶仗！”

“规矩……总得有规矩，你知道的。……”一个极其优柔寡断的人。说话轻声细语却不令人信服，这种特质带有一种典型的英国味道。一个神经过敏的人。狡黠却不圆滑……随后，突然之间，他又会像个真正的统帅一样猛然把你训得狗血喷头：“你怎么知道斯托姆夫人生病了？而且在这儿？”

“噢，”我说，“嗯。……”我想到了很多事情。想到了康拉德·马斯特斯，想到了“医生是否该守口如瓶”，想到了切里-马维尔——那个三手知情者，想到了康拉德·马斯特斯夫人。那可是一位风风火火的贵妇。

“除了切里-马维尔还能有谁告诉我！”我说。

“天哪，那个家伙！”

但艾丽丝将她的医生对他那位风骚妻子的可疑泄密从我脑海中一扫而空。……

“病了，”他嘟囔道，“病得不轻……嗯。……”

“我听说，”我绝望地说，“她做了某种手术——”

“根本没动过手术！”那位领袖人物厉声喝道，“这些风声到处乱传，简直能把人逼疯。……”

“好吧，我只是重复我听到的消息，马斯特斯。而且一旦咱们那位朋友知道了什么，你就别指望能保密了——”

“谁提保密了？”一声危险而如猫般轻柔的嘟囔，“我不需要保密。……”

沉默。焦虑与那个消瘦男子可疑的怒火携手漫步在沉默之中。

“我说，马斯特斯，她——她病得很重吗？当然，如果我打扰了的话。……”

那双充满忧虑的眼睛盯着他从仰卧如虚脱般的椅子上伸得老远的双脚。那位在俗修女似乎在隔壁房间里瞎忙活。“你是在想盖拉拉夫人吧？你对我还有马特尔-波纳尔不太有信心，是吗？”那双忧虑的眼睛似乎带着一丝微弱的自嘲。那是那个男人的处事方式。你以为他在跟你耍滑头，其实他可能只是在嘲笑你。

===Original===

Some would speak well, very well, of Dr. Masters; whilst others almost libellously, saying that, working as he did with Eugene Martel-Bonnard, the surgeon, he couldn’t be over-scrupulous in advising profitable but unnecessary operations. Martel-Bonnard’s wife wore a famous pearl rope, of which it was said that each pearl had been bought at the price of a woman’s life. But a brilliant surgeon’s life. Martel-Bonnard would say, is full of drawbacks. He charged accordingly. I think that he and Mrs. Masters must have bullied Masters every now and then﻿—not that he wouldn’t have looked worried in the Elysian Fields. Between them, those three had once made poor Anna Estella Guelãra very sorry she had ever left Chile. She was quite well, Martel-Bonnard said she was very ill, he almost killed her, then he saved her, and how he hurt her! “Naturally,” smiled Martel-Bonnard. “Such things hurt. But, my friend, she was﻿—*pouf!*﻿—but for me.” How one would have liked to operate on that sleek little man, unsuccessfully! He despised you if you differed from him, operated on you if you were fool enough, and robbed you according to a special system he had of discounting the exchange. One hundred thousand francs, poor Anna Estella’s life had cost her that time. And pain, such as falls only to the lot of women!

“But. Masters, it’s surely not as bad a case as that!”

“Mm﻿ ﻿… not as bad? Well﻿ ﻿… different shall we say?”

“But that was an internal operation! You just said﻿—”

“Quite. That’s why it’s different.﻿ ﻿…”

Talking with Conrad Masters was like playing a game in which he who made out the most of the other’s words scored the most points.﻿ ﻿… But Iris alone here, in this obscure place as full of crucifixes as a cemetery!

“I’m sorry,” I said, rising from the stool. “I’m intruding.﻿ ﻿…”

“You’re all right,” he mumbled. “So you heard about it from that femme fatale, did you? Damn that man! Bla, bla, bla!”

Those worried but faintly amused eyes were on me. “Been hearing quite a lot about you lately. Nurses would have your dossier complete by now if they could understand English. You seem to have put your foot in it somewhere. Rather sorry for you if.﻿ ﻿…”

This bantering﻿ ﻿… medical bantering! Only doctors dare do it. “Well, how are we today?” But by paying close attention to the game I had scored one point. She was delirious. So far, delirious. Then﻿ ﻿… “if!”

===Chinese===

有些人会把马斯特斯医生夸得天花乱坠；而另一些人则几乎带着诽谤的语气，说由于他与外科医生尤金·马特尔-波纳尔共事，在建议做有利可图却毫无必要的手术时，他不可能过于讲究良心。马特尔-波纳尔的妻子戴着一条著名的珍珠项链，据说上面的每一颗珍珠都是以一个女人的生命为代价买来的。但马特尔-波纳尔会说，一个杰出的外科医生的生活充满了种种不顺。他的收费也相应水涨船高。我想他和马斯特斯夫人必定时不时地欺负马斯特斯——倒不是说他在极乐世界里就不会看起来满面愁容。他们三个人曾一度让可怜的安娜·埃斯特拉·盖拉拉极度后悔自己离开了智利。她本来好端端的，马特尔-波纳尔却说她病得很重，他差点要了她的命，随后又救了她，而且把她折腾得多痛苦啊！“自然嘛，”马特尔-波纳尔微笑道，“这种事总是很痛的。可是，我的朋友，要不是我，她就——砰！——没了。”人们多么想给那个圆滑矮小的男人动一次手术啊，而且是一次失败的手术！要是你和他的意见不合，他就鄙视你；要是你蠢到听他的话，他就给你动手术；并且按照他自己特有的一套汇率贴现系统来敲诈你。那一次，可怜的安娜·埃斯特拉为保全性命花掉了十万法郎。还有痛苦，那种唯有女人才会承受的痛苦！

“可是，马斯特斯，她的情况肯定没那么严重吧！”

“嗯……没那么严重？好吧……咱们说是另一种情况好吗？”

“但那次是内科手术！你刚才说——”

“没错。所以才说情况不同。……”

和康拉德·马斯特斯说话，就像在玩一场谁能从对方话里听出更多内容谁就能得高分的游戏。……然而艾丽丝竟然孤身一人留在这里，留在这个像公墓一样到处都是十字架的幽暗地方！

“对不起，”我从凳子上站起身来说，“我打扰了。……”

“你待着吧，”他含糊地说，“这么说你是从那个红颜祸水那里听说的，对吧？该死的那家伙！嗒嗒嗒，说个没完！”

那双忧虑却又带着微嘲的眼睛落在我身上。“最近听到了不少关于你的事。那些护士要是懂英语，你的档案现在早就备齐了。你好像在什么地方把事情搞砸了。要是……我倒挺同情你的。”

这种打趣……医学式的打趣！只有医生才敢这么干。“那么，咱们今天感觉怎么样？”但通过全神贯注地投入这场游戏，我得了一分。她在说胡话。到目前为止，是在发谵妄。随后……“要是”！

===Original===

“Masters,” I said, “are you telling me that she is dying?”

“Mm﻿ ﻿…” he muttered impatiently, and as he jumped up from his chair the rough brown greatcoat seemed to fill the dingy lodge. It smelt of England, that coat. And, protruding from it, that sharp, naked, weary face with the worried eyes.﻿ ﻿…

“Look here, Masters﻿—”

“Here you are,” he muttered. I could not understand why he muttered. “Here you are” until I found a cigarette in one hand and one of those wretched spirit-lighters in the other. A man without conviction even in his ability to strike a match.﻿ ﻿…

“Known her for years,” he muttered towards his feet. “At Deauville that year﻿ ﻿… terrible for her. Poor child.﻿ ﻿…”

“Masters, you said Donna Guelãra might die. You know you did. But she didn’t, did she?”

He looked at me sharply. “If only she’d help herself, lift a finger to help herself! That’s what beats a man. Doesn’t lift one finger, she doesn’t.”

“Oh!” I said, trying to look reasonable. But I couldn’t, for the life of me, accommodate myself to the idea of Iris dying. “I suppose this is the crisis, is it, Masters?”

The rough greatcoat gave one vindictive flounce, filled the room. “Crisis! The way you people talk of crisis this and crisis that! Hear a word once and stick to it through life! ‘When does the crisis pass?’ There is no ‘crisis’ in most of these infernal things. Malaria, pneumonia, a few others﻿—yes, crisis, know where you are. But in these things the patient just continues ill, two, three, four weeks, might live, might not. Lysis, not crisis. Crisis!”

“Sorry. Lysis.﻿ ﻿…”

“Oh, here!” He suddenly began fumbling in an ancient pocketbook, from which he extracted a small folded piece of paper. “Might interest you,” he muttered.

Scrawled in pencil across the slip of paper were what looked like two names. That indecipherable scrawl! At last I made out the two names: Hilary’s and mine.

“She said, should either of these two happen somehow to hear I am ill and call, just be nice to them, please. Her very words.﻿ ﻿…”

“Oh!” I said. And I went on staring at the slip of paper. It was a rather grubby slip of paper. And those two scrawled names were like a faint cry of loneliness.

“Known her for years,” Masters was muttering. “Nice! First tells me not to tell anyone, then to be ‘nice’ to you two.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“马斯特斯，”我说，“你是告诉我她快要死了吗？”

“嗯……”他不耐烦地嘟囔了一声，当他从椅子上猛然站起时，那件粗糙的褐色大衣仿佛填满了昏暗的门房。那件大衣散发着英国的气息。从大衣里探出来的，是那张带着忧虑眼神、棱角分明、毫无掩饰的疲倦面容。……

“听我说，马斯特斯——”

“拿着，”他嘟囔道。我不明白他为什么嘟囔“拿着”，直到我发现自己一只手里拿着一支烟，另一只手里拿着那把倒霉的汽油打火机。一个连划火柴的能力都缺乏自信的人。……

“认识她很多年了，”他朝自己的脚下嘟囔着，“那年在多维尔……对她来说太可怕了。可怜的孩子。……”

“马斯特斯，你以前说过盖拉拉夫人可能会死。你知道你这么说过。但她并没有死，不是吗？”

他锐利地看了我一眼。“只要她肯帮帮自己，动一根手指帮帮自己！这才是最让人受不了的。她连一根手指都不肯动，她就是不肯。”

“噢！”我说，尽量显得通情达理。但我无论如何也无法让自己接受艾丽丝濒死的念头。“我想这就是危机期了吧，是吗，马斯特斯？”

那件粗大衣忿忿地抖动了一下，占满了整个屋子。“危机期！你们这些人动不动就危机期长危机期短地瞎嚷嚷！听过一个词就记一辈子！‘危机期什么时候过去？’在这些该死的病症里，绝大多数根本不存在什么‘危机期’。疟疾、肺炎，还有其他几样——对，是有危机期，让你心里有数。但在这些病症里，病人只会持续病着，两周、三周、四周，可能活下来，也可能活不成。是渐退期，不是危机期。还危机期呢！”

“抱歉。是渐退期。……”

“噢，给你看这个！”他突然开始在一只旧皮夹里摸索，从中抽出一张折叠的小纸片。“也许你会感兴趣，”他嘟囔道。

小纸片上用铅笔潦草地写着看似两个名字。那难以辨认的潦草字迹！我终于认出了那两个名字：希拉里的名字和我自己的名字。

“她说，如果这两个人不知怎么碰巧听说我生病了找过来的话，请对他们好一点。这是她的原话。……”

“噢！”我说。我继续盯着那张小纸片。那是一张相当脏旧的纸片。而那两个潦草的名字，宛如一声微弱的孤独呼喊。

“认识她很多年了，”马斯特斯嘟囔着，“好一点！先是让我别告诉任何人，接着又让我对你们两个‘好一点’。……”

===Original===

I gave him back the slip of paper. I don’t know why, and now I wish I hadn’t. I would like to have it now, beside that fiver. “Nice, fivers are.﻿ ﻿…” Thoughtful Iris! She knew her friends, she did. Lying lonely here﻿ ﻿… and having an afterthought about Hilary﻿—and me! “If they should somehow happen to hear and call.” Poor Guy hadn’t a mention. She wasn’t for putting any strain on Guy’s lawfulness. But why lawfulness? I looked at Conrad Masters.

“Septic poisoning,” said Masters. “That’s the trouble.”

That meant very little to me, for never was a man so ill-informed about such things. “But,” I said doubtfully to those gentle-worried eyes, and he murmured:

“Sure you’re not thinking of ptomaine poisoning? Not that that isn’t quite enough to be going on with.﻿ ﻿…”

“Pain,” I said. “Good Lord, pain.﻿ ﻿…” All I could think of was pain, pain, pain. One can almost feel the stabs of someone’s pain. Worst of all, one can mentally hear the faint screams of a voice just recognisable. Conrad Masters, the sight of him, reminded me vividly of Anna Estella’s pain. Once, from a waiting-room, I had heard her screaming. “Pain?” I said.

===Chinese===

我把纸片还给了他。我不知道为什么，现在真后悔那么做。我现在真希望能拥有它，放在那张五镑钞票旁边。“好得很，五镑钞票就是好。……”体贴的艾丽丝！她确实了解自己的朋友。孤单地躺在这里……脑海里却忽然想到了希拉里——还有我！“如果他们不知怎么碰巧听说并找来的话。”可怜的盖伊连提都没被提起。她可不想给盖伊的正统规矩施加任何负担。可是为什么是正统规矩呢？我看着康拉德·马斯特斯。

“败血性中毒，”马斯特斯说，“问题就在这里。”

这对我来说几乎毫无概念，因为从来没有一个人对这种事如此孤陋寡闻。“可是，”我疑惑地对着那双温柔而焦虑的眼睛说，他低语道：

“你确定自己想的不是尸碱中毒？倒不是说那还不够让人受的。……”

“疼痛，”我说，“天哪，疼痛。……”我脑子里所能想到的只有疼痛、疼痛、疼痛。一个人几乎能感受到他人身上刺痛般的疼痛。最可怕的是，一个人的脑海里能听见那勉强能辨认出来的声音发出的微弱尖叫。康拉德·马斯特斯，看着他，让我生动地想起了安娜·埃斯特拉当年的痛苦。有一次，在候诊室里，我曾听见她的惨叫。“疼吗？”我说。

===Original===

“Oh, no﻿ ﻿… no.” He weighed the matter. “Nothing to speak of. Just keep still, that’s the main thing. Very still, for weeks and weeks. Long business, you know. But what worries a man is that she doesn’t try to help herself at all. Letting herself go﻿ ﻿… can’t tell whether consciously or not, but somewhere inside her just not caring. I’ve been sharp with her.﻿ ﻿… Nice business for me, isn’t it? Good Lord, nice! If only she’d take a pull, pull herself together﻿ ﻿… someone just give her mind a jab somehow. No good talking, of course. If she won’t, she won’t. Lies there, you know, just not caring.﻿ ﻿…” He was drawing on a fur-lined glove, and it was to that he spoke; almost, one thought, shyly. A curious, complex gentleman. “She’s said once or twice she’d like to see you and﻿ ﻿… well, learn you a thing or two. Some stuff about roses and dandelions. You seem to have made a gaffe somewhere, and it’s quite on her mind to tell you about it. Hope I’m not giving anything away﻿ ﻿… but might do her good just to see you, feel you’re round about. You can’t tell. We’ll see how she is tomorrow. Extraordinary, I’ve found it, the way a woman will wake up for a second from days of delirium for no other purpose than to feel lonely.﻿ ﻿… Not awake now, though. Ill, this evening. Can’t really, you see, be iller if she tried. It will be good news, really good news, if she is alive in the morning. That’s as much as I can say. Sorry.﻿ ﻿… Well, I must snatch some dinner.﻿ ﻿…”

We were outside. The rain had ceased, it was much warmer. The Masters’s Renault, sleek and shining black but for the scarlet wheels, dwarfed my taxi.

Septic poisoning. I began to remember a little about that. I remembered two words which seemed very like “septic poisoning” in reports of trials of wretched women who had “operated.” Surely, Masters couldn’t﻿ ﻿… she had, after all, trusted me﻿—“be nice to him”﻿—and I must at once think the worst thing. Oh, God, how foul a thing a man’s mind is, how foul! But, Iris, dear Iris, why is one able to think of these awful things in connection with you!

“There’s always hope, you know,” Masters was muttering. “Pity you kept your taxi. I could have dropped you. And Donna Guelãra didn’t die, did she?”

But how Anna Estella had desired to live! “Die, me!” she had later screamed with laughter.

===Chinese===

“噢，不……不。”他权衡了一下，“没什么可说的。只要保持安静，那是关键。安安静静地躺上好几周好几周。这是一场持久仗，你知道的。但让人担心的是，她根本不想帮自己。任由自己沉沦下去……说不准是有意还是无意，但在她内心深处的某个角落，就是不在乎。我对她发过脾气。……这对我来说可真是件苦差事，不是吗？天哪，真好啊！要是她能振作一下，振作起来……要是有人能以某种方式敲打一下她的心智。当然，光说是没用的。如果她不愿意，她就绝不会动。躺在那儿，你知道的，就是不在乎。……”他正在戴上一只皮毛衬里的手套，他是对着那只手套说话的；几乎可以说，带着几分羞怯。一个古怪、复杂的绅士。“她提过一两次想见见你，而且……嗯，教你一两件事。说什么关于玫瑰和蒲公英的话。你好像在什么地方出了洋相，她一心想着要当面告诉你。希望我没泄露什么……不过见见你，感觉到你在周围，也许对她有好处。这很难说。咱们看看她明天情况如何。我觉得真不可思议，一个女人从多日的谵妄中苏醒片刻，竟然没有别的目的，仅仅是为了体会孤独。……不过现在没醒。今晚病得很重。你看，就算她想病得更重，实际上也无法更重了。如果她明早还能活着，那就是好消息，真正的好消息。我能说的就这么多了。抱歉。……好了，我得抓紧去吃晚饭了。……”

我们来到了门外。雨停了，天气暖和了许多。马斯特斯的那辆雷诺轿车通体漆黑油亮，唯有轮毂是朱红色的，把我的出租车衬托得十分渺小。

败血性中毒。我开始回想起来一点了。我记得在审判那些做了“私下手术”的可怜女人的案件报道中，有两个词非常像“败血性中毒”。肯定不会，马斯特斯不会……毕竟，她是信任我的——“对他们好一点”——而我却必须立刻想到最糟糕的情况。噢，上帝啊，一个男人的心思是多么肮脏啊，多么肮脏！可是，艾丽丝，亲爱的艾丽丝，为什么别人一想到你，就能联想到这些可怕的事情呢！

“总是有希望的，你知道的，”马斯特斯嘟囔着，“可惜你留着你的出租车。我本可以捎你一程的。而且盖拉拉夫人当时也没死，不是吗？”

然而安娜·埃斯特拉当时是多么渴望活着啊！“死？我才不呢！”她后来哈哈大笑着叫道。

===Original===

Iris had trusted me. “Be nice to him”﻿—her very words. And I had thought that﻿ ﻿…

“Masters, you won’t mind my coming round again? Perhaps tonight?”

“Sleep here, if you like,” he smiled. “I’ll be coming myself for a second, about midnight. Wife’s got a party. Like to come? Rather good bridge. Well, please yourself.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

艾丽丝曾经信任过我。“对他好一点”——这正是她的原话。而我竟然想到那方面去了……

“马斯特斯，你不介意我再过来吧？也许今晚？”

“只要你愿意，睡在这儿都行，”他微笑道，“我自己半夜十二点左右也会过来看看。我妻子今晚有聚会。想来吗？桥牌打得挺不错的。好吧，随你便吧。……”

### II / 第二节
===Original===

I agreed with my sister that it was abominably rude of her younger brother to be nearly an hour late to take her out to dinner, especially as she had been ready for at least twenty minutes. She was furious. I said: “There is a new dance place open. I heard about it from a friend of mine, Mr. Cherry-Marvel. You will meet him, he is charming. This new place is called La Plume de Ma Tante. It has only been open three nights, so it will be very modish for another two. There is a nightingale there.”

“One cannot dance to a nightingale.”

“But why are you so exclusive?”

“It is cruel and beastly to keep a nightingale caged.”

“Dear, it takes a woman who once had a passion for aigrettes and who loves eating lobsters to be so sensitive. But there is probably baser music to supplement this nightingale. There are, in fact, five lovely niggers. The place is called La Plume de Ma Tante so that English people may know exactly where they stand.”

“You are so funny tonight, but would you mind not polishing your shoes on my dress? This is a very terrible taxi, and I think men are monstrous. If you were taking any woman but your sister out to dinner you would have chosen the taxi with discretion.”

“Rudolf and Raymonde are the dancers. I do not want to go to The Pen of My Aunt, but for your sake I would go anywhere. After dinner.”

She was pleased, loving to dance. We walked up the pavement of the rue Royale to the quiet doors of Larue. She said: “I love Rudolf and Raymonde. I saw them dancing at Monte Carlo, and they say American women give him platinum watches from Cartier and that he was a footman in San Francisco, or was that Rudolf Valentino?”

I said: “I say, do you know anything about septic poisoning?”

“Really, how callous you are! Do I know anything about it! But I had it!”

“No!” One’s sister!

“But of course I had it! It is amazing when one’s own brother is quite unaware that one has been through endless pain and torture.”

“Not pain and torture,” I said. “A little bird told me.”

“But I am not responsible for your feathered friends! I was as good as dead, that’s all I know.”

“But, my dear, that was when you were having a baby! I was in Vienna.”

“So you said. But, of course, it came on after I had a baby. One does not get septic poisoning for nothing. I nearly died, I can tell you.”

“*Vestiaire, monsieur?*”

“… Oh, I see. A baby. After that.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

我赞成我姐姐的看法，她弟弟来接她去吃晚饭迟到了将近一个小时，这确实是极其粗鲁无礼的，尤其是她至少已经准备好了二十分钟。她气坏了。我说：“新开了一家跳舞的地方。我是从一位朋友切里-马维尔先生那里听说的。你会见到他的，他很迷人。这个新地方叫‘我姑妈的钢笔’。它才开了三个晚上，所以在接下来的两个晚上里还会非常时髦。那里有一只夜莺。”

“人不能跟着夜莺跳舞。”

“可你为什么这么挑剔呢？”

“把一只夜莺关在笼子里是残忍而且下流的。”

“亲爱的，只有曾经迷恋过白鹭羽毛、而且酷爱吃龙虾的女人才会这么敏感。不过很可能有更通俗的音乐来补充这只夜莺。实际上有五个很棒的黑人乐手。那地方之所以叫‘我姑妈的钢笔’，是为了让英国人确切明白自己的处境。”

“你今晚真逗，不过你能不能别把鞋蹭在我的裙子上？这辆出租车实在太破了，而且我觉得男人们都很混账。如果你是带除了你姐姐之外的任何女人出去吃晚饭，你都会谨慎挑选出租车的。”

“鲁道夫和雷蒙德是领舞的舞者。我并不想去‘我姑妈的钢笔’，但为了你，我哪儿都愿意去。晚饭之后。”

她很高兴，她酷爱跳舞。我们沿着皇家街的人行道走向拉吕餐厅平静的大门。她说：“我喜欢鲁道夫和雷蒙德。我在蒙特卡洛看过他们跳舞，据说美国女人送给他卡地亚的铂金手表，还说他以前在旧金山当过男仆，还是说那是鲁道夫·瓦伦蒂诺？”

我说：“我说，你懂败血性中毒吗？”

“真的，你多么无情啊！我懂不懂败血性中毒！我得过这病啊！”

“不会吧！”自己的姐姐！

“我当然得过！当自己的亲弟弟对一个人经历过无休止的痛苦与折磨毫无察觉时，真令人吃惊。”

“算不上痛苦和折磨，”我说，“一只小鸟告诉我的。”

“我可不对你那些长羽毛的朋友负责！我当时跟死了差不多，我只知道这个。”

“可是，亲爱的，那是你在生孩子的时候！当时我在维也纳。”

“你当时是这么说的。不过，这当然是在我生了孩子之后发生的。人不会无缘无故得败血性中毒。我当时差点没命，我可以告诉你。”

“要寄存衣帽吗，先生？”

“……噢，我明白了。孩子。在那之后。……”

===Original===

“I have never been so hungry in my life,” my sister said, “and you talk to me of septic poisoning. I suppose you think you will destroy my appetite and therefore the bill will be less. I will begin with caviar.”

“Septic poisoning,” I said, “did not kill you, that is the point. You cannot imagine how glad I am. Let us eat caviar.”

===Chinese===

“我这辈子从来没这么饿过，”我姐姐说，“你却跟我谈什么败血性中毒。我看你是想败坏我的胃口，好让账单便宜点。我要先吃鱼子酱。”

“败血性中毒并没有要了你的命，这才是关键，”我说，“你无法想象我有多高兴。咱们吃鱼子酱吧。”

### III / 第三节
===Original===

La Plume de Ma Tante. Bright green walls splashed with vermilion. A platform at one end, whereon five blackamoors perspired. At the other, a naked woman. She was without hips, according to the fashion for women. Her arms were twined above her head, and raised on the tip of her fingers was a bowl of green malachite from which pink water splashed into a white alabaster basin at her feet. Many English people were present. They would be going to the Riviera, then they would be coming back from the Riviera. Colonel Duck was there, with the quality. Colonel Duck was, no doubt, just returned from some notably swift exploits on the Cresta Run. But he never was so talkative about his outdoor activities. Cherry-Marvel was there, with a great big woman and a nice-looking boy with the hands of a housemaid who was a famous boxer. There was the usual group of Argentines, very well dressed indeed. They talked about *le polo*. All over the room elderly women were dancing with young men of both sexes. Mio Mi Marianne was there, sitting alone, but I might not speak with her because I was with my sister. A demimondaine will feel insulted if you speak with her when you are with your sister. Two years before Mio Mi Marianne had one night tied a silk handkerchief round her wrist, and it became the fashion for women to tie silk handkerchiefs round their wrists. Then Mio Mi Marianne tied a silk handkerchief round her throat, and that became the fashion. She thought of these things while smoking opium. She sat alone, staring into a glass of Vichy Water. A young American polo-player called Blister went up to her table, and maybe he asked her to dance, but she just looked at him and he went away again. Her eyes were intent on an opium-dream, and she was very happy in the arms of the infinite. Mio Mi Marianne will be found one day lying on the Aubusson carpet of her drawing-room. There will be a hole in the carpet where her cigarette has died out.

===Chinese===

“我姑妈的钢笔”。鲜绿色的墙壁上泼洒着朱红色。一端是一个舞台，五个黑人乐手在上面汗流浃背。另一端立着一个裸体女人。按照当时的女性时尚，她没有腰臀曲线。她的双臂交缠在头顶，指尖托着一只绿孔雀石碗，粉红色的水从碗中溅落到她脚下的一只白色雪花石膏盆里。在场的有很多英国人。他们正准备前往里维埃拉，随后又会从里维埃拉返回。达克上校也在那儿，身边簇拥着显贵。达克上校毫无疑问刚从克雷斯塔滑道上那些著名的迅疾战绩中归来。但他对自己的户外活动向来不怎么多言。切里-马维尔也在那儿，身旁伴着一位高头大马的女人和一个长着女仆般双手的英俊男孩，那男孩是一位著名的拳击手。还有那一如既往衣冠楚楚的阿根廷人小圈子。他们在谈论马球。整个大厅里，年长的女人都在与雌雄莫辨的年轻男子跳舞。缪·米·玛丽安坐在那里，形单影只，但我不能和她说话，因为我和我姐姐在一起。当你和姐姐在一起时与一个交际花说话，她会觉得受到了侮辱。两年前的一个夜晚，缪·米·玛丽安在手腕上系了一方丝绸手帕，于是女人在手腕上系丝绸手帕便成了时尚。后来缪·米·玛丽安在脖子上系了一方丝绸手帕，那又成了时尚。她在抽鸦片时想到了这些。她独自坐着，凝视着一杯维希矿泉水。一个名叫布利斯特的年轻美国马球手走到她的桌旁，也许是邀请她跳舞，但她只是看了他一眼，他便走开了。她的双眼专注于鸦片的迷梦，在无尽的怀抱中感到无比幸福。总有一天，人们会发现缪·米·玛丽安躺在她客厅的奥布松地毯上。地毯上会留下一个被她的香烟烧灭的焦洞。

===Original===

A blackamoor beat a warning roll on his drum, the dancers left the floor, the lights dwindled and awoke again in swaying shadows of blue and carmine. A heavily built young man with the face of a murderer danced a tango with a lovely young girl with short golden curls. Then he threw her on the floor, and picked her up again. Rudolf and Raymonde. He did it beautifully. An American woman called the Duchess of Malvern threw Rudolph a pink carnation. The Baron de Belus said harshly: “That is a white carnation really, but it is blushing at the fuss that women make of Dagoes.” In a cage clamped to the bright green wall near us was a dumb nightingale. It kept pecking at the floor of its cage, looking at nothing and nobody. I left my sister in Cherry-Marvel’s care. I said to her that he could dance, and next day she was furious.

===Chinese===

一名黑人乐手在鼓上敲出一阵警示性的滚奏，舞者们离开了舞池，灯光暗了下去，随后又在蓝红相间的摇曳阴影中重新亮起。一个身材魁梧、长着杀手面孔的年轻男子与一个留着金色短鬈发的可爱年轻姑娘跳起了一支探戈。接着他把她摔在地板上，又把她扶了起来。鲁道夫和雷蒙德。他的动作优美极了。一位被称为马尔文公爵夫人的美国女人朝鲁道夫扔了一支粉红色的康乃馨。贝吕斯男爵刻薄地说：“那其实是一支白康乃馨，只因看到女人们对南欧人如此大惊小怪，才羞红了脸。”在我们身旁钉在鲜绿墙壁上的一个鸟笼里，关着一只哑巴夜莺。它不停地啄着笼底，不看任何事物，也不看任何人。我把我姐姐托付给切里-马维尔照看。我对她说切里-马维尔会跳舞，第二天她气急败坏。

### IV / 第四节
===Original===

The burning eyes of the Renault made the grim Boulevard Pierre Abel almost hospitable. That was a conscientious man, Conrad Masters. How glad I was of him at that moment! What had he said about Iris? something about his having known her for years, something about “that year at Deauville﻿ ﻿… terrible for her.” That would mean, then, that Masters had been there during the Boy Fenwick tragedy. Iris, poor Iris! Such punishments﻿ ﻿… for what crimes? What crimes deserved such punishments? Iris, poor Iris! But she wouldn’t mind dying, not she. That was the trouble, Masters had said. But no doubt she knew best.﻿ ﻿…

The Paradis prison was a pit of blackness in the night. The dim lights behind the iron-barred windows were out, and it was impossible not to wonder if they slept up there in their iron cages, the wicked, the foolish, the betrayed. Perhaps the nightingale in its cage did not care. Perhaps those up there did not care, and slept like angels. But the wrongly accused would not sleep, that was certain. Does innocence wrongly-accused profit anyone except a very wise man or a very good man, except a man who cares nothing for the opinion of this world or one who cares only for the love of the next? I said to the taxi-driver: “Hell can know no torment like the agony of an innocent in a cage,” and when he had carefully examined his tip he agreed with me.

Gently as I could, I rang the bell, praying that the old woman would not be angry with me.

“*Aha!*” she chuckled. “*Aha! Monsieur-toujours-de-l’audace! Mais entrez, monsieur, entrez!* The doctor is just this moment arrived. Truly he is a good man, this Dr. Mastaire﻿—but our French doctors, you should see! They come for a moment, they go, and she lives or she dies, what do they care as long as they are paid? But this English doctor, he does not know how to make money easily. Madame his wife was this moment telephoning that he should go home quickly, for they are awaiting him for *le bridge*. *Ah, cet bridge, bridge, bridge!*”

“But you see how anxious I am! Have you heard anything since I last saw you?”

===Chinese===

雷诺轿车那明亮的大灯让阴森的皮埃尔·阿贝尔大道几乎显得亲切起来。康拉德·马斯特斯真是一个尽职尽责的人。在那一刻我是多么感激他啊！关于艾丽丝他说了些什么？说他认识她很多年了，还说什么“在多维尔的那一年……对她来说太可怕了”。那么，这就意味着，在“男孩”芬威克的悲剧期间，马斯特斯也在场。艾丽丝，可怜的艾丽丝！承受了这般的惩罚……究竟是犯了什么罪？什么样的罪过才配得上这般的惩罚？艾丽丝，可怜的艾丽丝！但她并不在乎死去，她不在乎。这就是问题所在，马斯特斯说过。不过毫无疑问，她自己心里最有数。……

夜色中的天堂监狱是一口漆黑的深渊。铁栅窗后微弱的灯光已经熄灭，人们不由得联想，关在上头铁笼子里的那些邪恶之人、愚蠢之人、被背叛之人，此刻是否已经睡下。也许笼中的夜莺并不在乎。也许上面那些人也并不在乎，睡得像天使一样安详。但那些蒙冤受屈的人是绝睡不着的，那是肯定的。被诬陷的无辜除了对极有智慧的人或极善良的人、除了对完全不在乎世人评价的人或只在乎来世之爱的人之外，对谁还能有好处呢？我对出租车司机说：“地狱里再也没有什么折磨能比得上笼中无辜者的痛苦了，”而当他仔细检查完小费后，他赞同了我的看法。

我尽可能轻地按响了门铃，暗自祈祷那个老妇人不会冲我发火。

“哈！”她咯咯笑了起来，“哈！永远大胆的先生！快请进，先生，快请进！医生刚刚到。这位马斯特斯医生真是一个好人——要是换成我们法国的医生，您瞧着吧！他们来一会儿就走，病人是死是活，只要能拿到钱他们才不管呢！但这位英国医生，他不懂怎么轻松赚钱。他夫人刚才还在打电话催他赶紧回家，因为大家都在等他打桥牌呢。唉，这桥牌啊，桥牌，桥牌！”

“可您看我有多着急啊！从我上次见您之后，您听到什么消息了吗？”

===Original===

“To have heard nothing, young man, is to have heard good news. But sit down, the doctor himself will tell you in one moment﻿—” That demoniac bell! It clanged through the place. Perhaps of all the nations in the world the French alone are capable of fixing the loudest possible bell to a nursing-home. The fat old woman grinned vindictively at me. We had been enemies, now we were allies against the intruder. “Bah!” she said, and opened the door. From where I stood I could not see who was without, but I could hear a voice: low, hesitating, in very correct French, in Foreign Office French.﻿ ﻿…

“Napier!”﻿ ﻿…

We stared at each other in the most profound surprise and confusion. Napier, favourite of the gods, shy, sensitive, fine﻿ ﻿… just here, just now, facing me in the obscure silence of the Paris night!

“This is funny,” Napier made to smile. “What?”

Napier Harpenden and I had known each other well, as “well” goes, for years, but never before had we been alone together. But once, some years ago, I had seen him in a curious moment. Late one night I was walking down a villainous alley near the East India Docks when through a lighted window I was astonished to see Napier’s white, thin, fine face and those dark fevered eyes. He was talking earnestly to an old man and a very pretty young girl who was crying, and I felt ashamed to have seen him, for that is how Napier affected one, you were hurt at the idea of hurting him. I had wondered often what he could have been doing there, what secret good work he was at. He was a strange, secret, saintly youth, a favourite of the gods who never once relied on the favouritism of gods or men.﻿ ﻿…

He still stood outside, a serious slack shape in a tweed overcoat. He masked, behind that faint, deprecating smile of his, more than the mere confusion of surprise. He would very much rather it had not been me he had met just there. Napier and I were friends only because all our friends were mutual. We hadn’t ever found, tried to find, any common ground for friendship. Sincerely, I was very sorry to be there. Napier had that effect on one.

===Chinese===

“没有消息就是好消息，年轻人。不过请坐吧，医生自己一会儿就会告诉您——”那该死的门铃！在整栋屋子里回荡。也许在世界上所有的民族中，唯独法国人做得出把最刺耳的门铃装在疗养院门口这种事。那个胖老妇人恶狠狠地冲我咧嘴一笑。我们刚才还是敌人，现在面对不速之客却结成了同盟。“呸！”她说了一声，打开了门。从我站的地方看不见外面是谁，但我能听见一个声音：低沉、迟疑，说着一口非常纯正的法语，一口英国外交部式的法语。……

“内皮尔！”……

我们极其震惊而困惑地面面相觑。内皮尔，众神的宠儿，害羞、敏感、高贵……偏偏就在这里，偏偏就在此时，在巴黎深夜幽暗的寂静中与我相对而立！

“这真奇妙，”内皮尔勉强笑了一下，“嗯？”

内皮尔·哈彭登和我相识多年，就常人所谓的“熟悉”而言彼此知根知底，但在此之前我们从未单独在一起过。不过多年前的一次，我曾在一个奇特的时刻见过他。一天深夜，我沿着东印度码头附近一条肮脏的小巷走着，透过一扇亮着灯的窗户，我惊讶地看见内皮尔那苍白、清瘦、高贵的面孔，以及那双充满狂热的黑眼睛。他正在认真地和一个老人以及一个正在哭泣的漂亮年轻姑娘交谈，我为自己窥见了这一幕而感到羞愧，因为内皮尔给人的感觉正是如此——一想到会伤害他，你就会感到心痛。我曾多次纳闷他在那里做什么，在从事什么秘密的善举。他是一个古怪、隐秘、如圣徒般的青年，一个从不依赖神明或凡人偏爱的众神宠儿。……

他依然站在门外，身穿一件花呢大衣，身形严肃而松垮。在他那淡淡的、带着歉意的微笑背后，掩饰的绝不仅仅是突如其来的困惑。他宁愿在此时此地遇见的不是我。内皮尔和我是朋友，仅仅因为我们所有的朋友都是共同的朋友。我们从未发现过、也从未试图去寻找过任何建立友谊的共同基础。说实话，我非常后悔自己在场。内皮尔给人的感觉向来如此。

===Original===

“Venice is waiting in the taxi,” he said. Whenever Napier and I met he would instantly speak of Venice. This was to show me that he knew Venice and I were great friends and that, if he and I weren’t great friends, that must somehow be his fault. How could you help liking a man like that? The courtesy of that favourite of the gods went so much deeper than anyone else’s: let it one day go a little deeper, and you felt that it might have gone a little too deep, down, down to self-destruction.

I said I had arrived in Paris only that afternoon, and had heard, by chance, that Mrs. Storm was ill. My presence there seemed, you can see, to require a more definite explanation than any he might think fit to give me. One felt, with Napier, uncomfortably familiar to be asking after Iris in this obscure place at this late hour. He and Iris had been “kids.” Then I thought, comically, of the two scrawled names on the grubby slip of paper. Well, I seemed to have rights too. More rights than Napier, really. Conrad Masters had no instructions to be nice to Napier. Poor Napier.﻿ ﻿…

“But,” he said, slowly, slowly, “surely she’s better by now? I only just called on the off-chance﻿ ﻿… really wanted air after the train journey more than anything else. Surely﻿ ﻿… what?”

I stared at him. What to say? You see, the sudden, white way he was staring at me made me feel terribly canny of anything I might say. Besides, one treated Napier *differently*.

“Better?” I repeated. “Well.﻿ ﻿…”

“But, look here,” he said, protested.﻿ ﻿… It was dark, there between the dim lodge and the night. Why on earth didn’t the man come in? “Venice and I are going south tomorrow, and I just thought I’d inquire﻿—but, look here, I never dreamt that she.﻿ ﻿…”

I at last grasped the fact that he had known she was ill. He was the only one among us who had known she was ill. One kid had known that the other kid was ill﻿ ﻿… and had waited until, on his way south, he could conveniently come round and inquire. Well!

“You had better come in, hadn’t you?” I said. I simply couldn’t say slap-out that Iris was ill nearly to death. You couldn’t say things like that to those dark, troubled eyes. You protected Napier from your own impulses, always. A favourite not of the gods alone.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“威尼斯正在出租车里等着呢，”他说。每当内皮尔和我碰面，他总会立刻提起威尼斯。这是为了向我表明，他知道威尼斯和我是极要好的朋友；而且，如果他和我不算挚友的话，那一定不知怎么是他的过错。面对这样一个人，你怎能不喜欢他呢？这位众神宠儿的谦逊礼貌比任何人都深刻得多：若是哪天这种礼貌再深入几分，你甚至会觉得它可能走得太深了，一直滑落、滑落至自我毁灭的深渊。

我说我今天下午才到巴黎，碰巧听说斯托姆夫人生病了。你看，我出现在这里，似乎比他认为合适给我的任何解释都需要一个更明确的说明。和内皮尔在一起，在这个深夜幽暗的地方探听艾丽丝的消息，会让人觉得有一种令人不自在的熟络。他和艾丽丝曾是“青梅竹马的孩子”。接着我滑稽地想起了脏纸片上潦草写下的那两个名字。好吧，看来我也有权利。实际上比内皮尔更有权利。康拉德·马斯特斯可没接到对内皮尔好一点的指示。可怜的内皮尔。……

“可是，”他缓缓地、缓缓地说道，“她现在肯定好多了吧？我只是碰运气顺道过来看看……在坐了火车之后，其实主要是想出来透透气。肯定……嗯？”

我凝视着他。该说什么好呢？你看，他突然投来的苍白凝视，让我对自己可能说的每一句话都感到极度警惕。此外，人们对待内皮尔的态度向来是与众不同的。

“好多了？”我重复道，“嗯。……”

“可是，听我说，”他抗议道。……门房的昏暗与外面的黑夜交界处一片漆黑。这家伙到底为什么不进来呢？“威尼斯和我明天就要南下了，我只是想过来打听一下——可是，听我说，我做梦也没想到她会。……”

我终于意识到他早就知道她病了。在我们所有人当中，他是唯一一个知道她生病的人。一个孩子早就知道另一个孩子生病了……却一直等到自己在南下的路上，能够顺路过来打听一下。哎！

“你最好还是进来吧，不是吗？”我说。我实在无法径直说出艾丽丝已经病得奄奄一息了。面对那双忧郁痛苦的黑眼睛，你根本说不出这种话来。你总是在保护内皮尔，免受你自己冲动的伤害。他不仅仅是众神的宠儿。……

===Original===

But he still stood there in the darkness, staring at me very strangely and scowling in that funny, attractive way he had. Whenever I think of Napier I can see that Napier scowl and I can hear that involuntary “what?” he would tack on to questions.

“Look here, something’s the matter.” His voice trembled absurdly.﻿ ﻿… “Something serious. What?”

“She’s very ill,” I think I said.

“Very!” he snapped. “What? You mean﻿ ﻿… really ill? What?”

“I think so,” I said. “Yes.”

I looked into the room, avoiding those eyes. The lay sister, a pair of horn spectacles on her nose, and without a sign of interest in us, was mending the heel of a black woollen stocking, one end of which lay coiled in a black tin box. I couldn’t somehow look at Napier just then. That, you see, was the first hint I had of the thing, and though it was no more than a hint, it tore at one. The look in Napier’s eyes, I mean. The man’s heart was in his eyes.﻿ ﻿…

“Look here,” he said sharply, “I don’t understand this. What? I mean, I’d no idea it was.﻿ ﻿…”

“I don’t know anything,” I said, “except just that she’s ill.” We stared at each other.

“As ill,” I said, “as can be.”

“Oh,” he said. His eyes on me, not seeing me, he pushed past me through the doorway. And when I saw his face again, I was appalled. It was lost, abandoned, terribly unaware of everything but fear, it was enchanted by fear. He simply didn’t *care* but about one thing.﻿ ﻿…

“Haven’t seen her,” he said, and scowled at me. Not that he had, at that moment, the faintest idea who I was.

“Here, a cigarette,” I said.

He stared at it in his fingers. He crushed it.﻿ ﻿…

“Haven’t seen her for nearly a year,” he said in a rush, and stopped abruptly, seemed to realise me, scowled. “I say, what is it? Pneumonia or something? What?”

I fumbled. I wasn’t, I said, certain. Had only seen the doctor for a moment. Something inside, I thought, had gone wrong.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

但他依然站在黑暗中，非常古怪地盯着我，皱着眉头，带着他那种特有的滑稽而迷人的神态。每当我想起内皮尔，我都能看见内皮尔皱起眉头的样子，都能听见他在问句末尾不由自主加上的一声“嗯？”。

“听着，出事了。”他的声音荒唐地颤抖着。……“出了严重的事。嗯？”

“她病得很重，”我记得自己是这么说的。

“很重！”他厉声追问，“嗯？你的意思是……真的病得很重？嗯？”

“我想是的，”我说，“对。”

我看向屋里，避开那双眼睛。那位在俗修女鼻梁上架着一副牛角眼镜，对我们毫无兴趣，正在缝补一只黑色羊毛袜的后跟，袜子的另一头盘在一只黑色铁盒里。不知怎的，我当时无法直视内皮尔。你看，那是我第一次察觉到那件事的端倪，尽管那不过是一个微小的征兆，却直刺人心。我是指内皮尔眼中的神情。那个男人的心全写在眼睛里了。……

“听着，”他急促地说，“我不明白。嗯？我是说，我根本不知道会是。……”

“我什么都不知道，”我说，“只知道她病了。”我们面面相觑。

“病得不能，”我说，“再重了。”

“噢，”他说。他的眼睛盯着我，却视而不见，他挤过我身旁走进了门内。而当我再次看清他的脸时，我惊呆了。那是一张迷茫、绝望、除了恐惧之外对其余一切茫然无知的面容，他被恐惧彻底摄住了。除了那一件事，他根本不在乎任何东西。……

“没见过她，”他说着，冲我皱起眉头。倒不是说在那个瞬间他对我究竟是谁有哪怕一丁点的概念。

“来，抽支烟，”我说。

他盯着手指间的香烟。他把它捏碎了。……

“差不多有一年没见过她了，”他一口气说了出来，随后猛然顿住，似乎意识到了我的存在，皱起了眉头。“我说，到底是什么病？肺炎还是别的什么？嗯？”

我支支吾吾。我说自己也不确定。只是刚刚匆匆见了医生一眼。我想大概是身体里某个器官出了毛病。……

===Original===

I was immensely lost in all this. He had known she was ill﻿—but not seriously ill, nor of what! I grabbed at one certain point of behaviour for myself. One had to. I was, anyhow, going to make no mischief. Like Guy, I would give no “gratuitous information” of any sort. For better or for worse, I wouldn’t. News of septic poisoning was obviously not for Napier, not for anyone﻿—except for the two names on the grubby slip of paper. This septic poisoning seemed to mean only one of two things, a child or not a child. That was most utterly Iris’s business. Iris the desirous﻿—for a child. “To be playmates with.” And I wondered, just then, if it had been another Hector-not-so-proud. “Like to have a winner once.﻿ ﻿…” I kept on hearing that slightly husky voice saying little things.

“What I mean to say is,” Napier said, with sudden astounding calm, “that this is perfectly idiotic. What? You see, I hadn’t the faintest idea.﻿ ﻿…”

But when, deceived by the calm of his voice, I looked at him, I found it better to look away again at the frowsty old lay-sister sewing away at her stocking. It was mean to look at him, he was too naked. I realised how masked we always are, how this is a world of masked men, how we are masked all day long, even on the most trivial occasions. Then I felt his hands suddenly tight around my arm. And tighter. Now what?

“I’m awfully sorry,” I said idiotically.

“Look here﻿—I say, for God’s sake! You see, I don’t understand. What? She wrote to me weeks ago that she was going to be just slightly ill, and now.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

在这一切当中我感到茫然失措。他知道她病了——但不知道病得如此严重，也不知道是什么病！我为自己抓住了一条确定的行事准则。人不得不如此。无论如何，我都绝不挑拨生事。像盖伊一样，我绝不提供任何形式的“无端信息”。不论好坏，我都绝不说。败血性中毒的消息显然不能告诉内皮尔，也不能告诉任何人——除了脏纸片上的那两个名字。这种败血性中毒似乎只意味着两件事之一：有了孩子，或者没有孩子。那完全是艾丽丝个人的私事。渴望孩子的艾丽丝——“有个玩伴”。而我当时就在想，那是否又是另一个“没那么骄傲的赫克托”。“难得想赢一次。……”我的耳畔不断响起那个略带沙哑的声音说出的点点滴滴。

“我真正的意思是，”内皮尔以突然令人惊愕的平静说道，“这完全是荒唐透顶。嗯？你看，我根本就不知道。……”

然而，当我被他平静的语调所蒙蔽、抬头看向他时，我发现最好还是把目光移开，重新看向那个缝袜子的邋遢老修女。看着他未免太残忍了，他暴露得太彻底了。我意识到我们平时总是戴着面具，这是一个由戴面具之人构成的世界，我们整天戴着面具，哪怕在最微不足道的场合也是如此。接着，我感觉到他的双手突然紧紧抓住了我的手臂。抓得更紧了。现在要怎样？

“我真的很抱歉，”我愚蠢地说。

“听着——我说，看在上帝的份上！你看，我不明白。嗯？几周前她写信给我，说自己只是会稍微生点小病，而现在。……”

===Original===

The fingers dropped from my arm. “Hell!” he muttered. “Oh, hell! What?” He hadn’t the faintest idea of what he was saying. I wished to God he had, I didn’t want to listen to him, I hated listening to him, it was like spying on the man. Spying on Tristram wandering in the forest raving with love for Yseult. But what could I do? How leave him like this? How let him return to Venice like this? Good Lord, and Venice waiting in the taxi! If she saw him like this.﻿ ﻿… Good Lord, was the man mad to have brought Venice with him! Here, to see Iris! The misty impulses of a man of honour﻿ ﻿… do nothing behind his wife’s back. After, you know, having done everything. But﻿ ﻿… Good Lord, if Venice grew tired of waiting in the taxi and came and found Napier like this, like a demented knight in a story! Venice of the lion’s cub head, the mischievous, loyal eyes, dear Venice! adoring and adorable Venice! Napier’s wife.﻿ ﻿…

And, at that moment, I saw Venice again at the Loyalty, that night ten months ago, happily waiting for Napier, whose wife she would be in three days. “Darling, darling, *darling*!” That night of Gerald’s death! And then for the first time I remembered the cry of “Iris!” in the night, and the two red rear-lights swerving into South Audley Street, and I understood how it was that Iris in her letter had called me her “destroyer”﻿ ﻿… her “destroyer” with love, for no lover could have passed her way that night had I told her about Gerald. And Napier had passed her way, Napier whom she had seen that night for the first time in many years, Napier her ancient friend. “There were two roads leading from a certain tree.﻿ ﻿…” And the two roads had come together in the darkness of that night, in the darkness of cruelly blind chance, and now they had come together again in the darkness of this night, while Venice waited outside.﻿ ﻿…

I couldn’t, you can see, not do anything just then. I couldn’t let this love-lost man be found by Venice in her husband’s shoes. Napier and Venice, the happy lovers.﻿ ﻿… I was on Venice’s side. For Venice! Always, I was for Venice. One likes so few people, but one likes those few very, very much. This love-lost man must be woken up, must *behave*. Of course he must behave! Venice, for Venice! How dared he have done this to Venice? Marrying her on the third day from that night.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

那些手指从我的手臂上滑落了。“见鬼！”他嘟囔道，“噢，见鬼！嗯？他根本不知道自己在说什么。我真向上帝祈祷他能明白，我不想听他说，我讨厌听他说，这就像在窥探那个男人。就像在窥探特里斯坦在森林中漫游、因对伊索尔德的爱而陷入癫狂。可我能怎么办呢？怎么能就这样丢下他不管？怎么能让他这副模样回到威尼斯身边？天哪，威尼斯还在出租车里等着呢！如果她看见他这副模样。……天哪，这个男人把威尼斯一起带过来是疯了吗！带到这里来见艾丽丝！一个讲究荣誉之人的朦胧冲动……绝不在妻子背后搞鬼。你知道，在干完了一切之后。可是……天哪，如果威尼斯在出租车里等烦了，走过来发现内皮尔这副模样，像故事里发了疯的骑士一样！长着小狮子般脑袋的威尼斯，带着调皮、忠诚双眼的威尼斯，亲爱的威尼斯！崇拜人又惹人怜爱的威尼斯！内皮尔的妻子。……
